Text version of the family tree — all 194 people

This is the complete text of the family tree — the same data the interactive explorer above renders, written out so it can be read (and indexed) without JavaScript. 194 people, numbered by ahnentafel: #1 is Alexander Timothy Bell, each person’s father is double their number, each mother is double plus one.

Deeper reading: the research view with photographs and scanned evidence, the Bell paternal line as a narrative, the evidence map for the deep Bell line. Machine-readable: pedigree-data.json.

Alexander Timothy Bell (b. 1997, Cincinnati)

Ahnentafel #1 · the starting point of the chart · Documented — person and parent-link established

Alexander Timothy Bell was born in 1997 in Cincinnati. His birth certificate names both of his parents, and it is the record from which this family history begins — every number on this chart is counted outward from him. He is pictured here at twenty-eight.

Evidence

The starting point. Birth certificate naming both parents (redacted for privacy) [BELL-01 / SAR_31].

Robert Lee Bell Jr. (b. 1961, Birmingham)

Ahnentafel #2 · his father (1 generation back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Robert Lee Bell Jr. was born in 1961 in Birmingham, Alabama, and married in 1992. His own memory of the family, carefully recorded, helped reconstruct the 1940 Bell household and preserved the name "Ms Blevon" — a thread that later research traced back several generations. The earliest photograph of him is from the weeks after his birth, in the arms of his grandmother Margaret.

Evidence

Father. Married 1992. Source of MEMORY-02, which reconstructed the whole 1940 Bell household from memory and named “Ms Blevon.”

Cynthia Katharine Tuttle (b. 1959, Evanston IL)

Ahnentafel #3 · his mother (1 generation back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Cynthia Katharine Tuttle was born in 1959 in Evanston, Illinois, as shown on her birth certificate. Her family photo album and her own memory of her grandmother proved decisive in later research, resolving a long-standing question about the identity of her grandmother, Velma Roush.

Evidence

Mother. Birth certificate [FROST-02 / SAR_38]. Her photo album and her memory of her grandmother solved the Velma Roush wall that every archive had failed on.

Robert Lee Bell Sr. (1935 Atlanta – 2001 Chattanooga)

Ahnentafel #4 · his father’s father (2 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Robert Lee Bell Sr. was born in 1935 in Atlanta, Georgia — the 1940 census, read directly, corrected an old family belief that he was born in Alabama. He served nine years in the United States Air Force, from 4 February 1957 to 14 February 1966, reaching the rank of Airman 3rd Class and serving in Korea and Vietnam. He died in 2001 in Chattanooga and is buried in Chattanooga National Cemetery. Three photographs of him survive from his later years: beside a Tennessee lake in winter, out deep-sea fishing in a straw hat, and a plain portrait in a blue polo shirt. A last one, taken in a spring yard about 2000 or 2001, shows him standing with his son, his daughter-in-law and his two grandsons.

Evidence

Last known photograph, spring c.2000–01, with his son and grandsons [BELL-75]. Photographed in later life, c.1985–2000 [BELL-74]. Nine years US Air Force, 4 Feb 1957 – 14 Feb 1966, Airman 3rd Class, “Korea, Vietnam.” Buried Chattanooga National Cemetery, Sec. Ma, Site 36. Born in GEORGIA, not Alabama — read off the 1940 census image.

Outstanding: His 1935 Fulton County birth certificate is wanted for a different reason: it names his father’s birthplace. Optional: NPRC personnel file (SF-180).

Named in his mother’s January 1983 obituary as “Robert L. Bell of Mobile, Ala.” [BELL-106] — placing him alive in Mobile that month, and confirming him as her son from a source independent of family memory. His brothers and sister are named beside him: William E. Bell of Clarkston, Ga., John H. Bell of Warner Robins, Ga., and Mrs. Carolyn B. Miller of Birmingham.

Willie Joyce Stephens (1942 – 1982, Jefferson Co. AL)

Ahnentafel #5 · his father’s mother (2 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Willie Joyce Stephens was born in 1942 and died in 1982 in Jefferson County, Alabama. She married Robert Lee Bell Sr. on 26 August 1960 in Fort Payne, and later remarried John Duncan Lammon in 1980. She is buried in Clarke County among her mother's Truett family — one reason she was hard to trace for years was that she did not die under the Bell name. Three photographs of her survive, all from her last two years: her wedding to John Duncan Lammon on 24 May 1980, married at home beneath a flowered brass arch; a restaurant above the water, his arm around her shoulders; and one of her alone in a panelled room hung with framed stamp collections. They are the only known pictures of her.

Evidence

Photographed on her wedding day, 24 May 1980 [STEPHENS-40], and twice more before her death [STEPHENS-39]. m. Robert Lee Bell Sr. 26 Aug 1960, Fort Payne; m.(2) John Duncan Lammon 1980. Buried Clarke Co., Alabama among her mother’s Truett people. Invisible for years because she did not die a Bell.

Outstanding: The Find a Grave index gives only the county — Oak Grove and New Prospect are the Truett grounds.

Richard Clark Tuttle (1929 Terre Haute – 2015 Cincinnati)

Ahnentafel #6 · his mother’s father (2 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Richard Clark Tuttle, known all his life as Dick, was born in 1929 in Terre Haute, Indiana, and died in 2015 in Cincinnati. He served in the US Army, enlisting at Fort Lee, Virginia, on 3 August 1951 and training there as a supply-records specialist. A photograph kept in the family shows him in uniform in Germany, standing beside the sign for the Headquarters, Heilbronn Sub Post — the first evidence of where he was actually stationed. A senior yearbook placing him in the Evanston Township High School class of 1947 pushed the family's known move north back to about 1945, five years earlier than long assumed. The earliest photograph of him shows him at about nine, in a sweater and short trousers, standing beside his older brother Robert on a patch of spring grass in Terre Haute.

Evidence

Photographed c.1938–40 with his brother Robert Dale and three unidentified girls [TUTTLE-88]. US Army, Supply Records Specialist, Fort Lee, Virginia, 3 Aug 1951; stationed in occupied Germany at Heilbronn Sub Post — a family photograph shows him in uniform before the post headquarters sign [TUTTLE-85]. Evanston Township High School class of 1947 — a senior yearbook, which moved the family’s move north from 1950 back to about 1945.

Outstanding: Cook County marriage licence to Judith Frost (~1955–58) still unordered — and it would name Velma’s maiden name from a second direction.

Judith Katharine Frost (1934 Parkersburg WV – 2013 Cincinnati)

Ahnentafel #7 · his mother’s mother (2 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Judith Katharine Frost — Mimi to her grandchildren — was born in 1934 in Parkersburg, West Virginia, and died in 2013 in Cincinnati. A graduate of Northwestern University, she taught at the Indian Hill Schools and was also a portrait painter and a maker of dollhouse miniatures. A photograph of her as a small child survives from about 1936. Photographs survive of her as a small child about 1936, and at a college formal in the early 1950s.

Evidence

Known in the family as “Mimi.” College formal photograph, c.1952–56 [FROST-29]. Northwestern graduate, teacher at Indian Hill Schools, portrait painter and maker of dollhouse miniatures. Photographed as a child c.1936 [FROST-12].

John Henry Bell (b. 1901/02 Alabama – d. 1944×1956)

Ahnentafel #8 · his father’s father’s father (3 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

John Henry Bell was born on 4 August 1901 in Barbour County, Alabama — a date his family still remembered in 2009, and one he wrote down himself on a draft card in 1942. He was the eldest son of Thomas Jefferson Bell and Alice May Bradley, farmers near Louisville, and he appears in their household in 1910 and 1920 simply as “Henry.”

By 1925 he had left for Birmingham, and a Barbour County court recorded him there — “Henry Bell, Birmingham” — when his grandfather’s estate was divided seven ways among Tom’s children. Two years later, on Friday the thirteenth of May 1927, he married Margaret Beatrice Eaken. Both Birmingham papers thought a couple marrying on such a date was worth a humorous column. Their first son arrived six months later.

He drove a truck for a dry cleaner, then painted houses — in Birmingham, and in Atlanta through the middle thirties. There were six children. In June 1940 he was convicted in Jefferson County of desertion and non-support, and served four months of a twelve-month sentence; the prison ledger records him at 163 pounds, physical condition good, teeth bad.

He was released that October, and about a year later he left for good. On 23 February 1942 he registered for the draft in Cincinnati — five foot nine, 160 pounds, blue eyes, brown hair, a knot on his left leg near the hip — and gave, as the person who would always know his address, his mother in Dothan. It is the last document he is known to have signed.

After that he vanishes. His family moved north to Wheaton, Illinois, where his sixteen-year-old son was killed in a car crash in January 1944; the certificate names the father simply as “Henry Bell.” By the time his mother died in December 1956 her obituary listed six surviving children, and he was not among them. He lies in neither family cemetery. The likeliest trace of him is a Henry Bell, fifty-one, white, born in Alabama and recorded as “separated,” lodging in a home for the homeless outside Cincinnati in April 1950 — the only man of that description in the entire county.

Evidence

The 1940 conviction read in full [BELL-96]: sentenced 17 June 1940, twelve months, serial C 26,574, discharged 17 Oct 1940 by order of Judge Emmett Perry — a new fixed point placing him in Jefferson County that October. Weighed 163 lb; physical condition good, teeth bad. ⭐ And from the reverse of his draft card [BELL-107], a mark that could identify a body no name index will match: “knot on the left leg near the hip.” ⭐ His own 1930 census gives BOTH his parents as Alabama-born [BELL-100] — a test Thomas Jefferson Bell and Alice May Bradley pass, and one that could have failed. Documented 1901 to Jan 1944, then dark. The free online layer is now exhausted [BELL-86, BELL-87]; the live lead is a Henry Bell, 51, born Alabama, separated, lodging at the Mt. Airy home for the homeless in Cincinnati in April 1950 [BELL-85]. ⭐ Strengthened 12 Aug 2026 [BELL-99]: in the whole 1950 census of Hamilton County, Ohio he is the only white male Bell born in Alabama in that decade — one man out of 106 — and “separated” is exactly what a man who walked out in 1942 and never formalised it would say. Still unproven: the age is two years out, and the record gives no parents, no next of kin and no middle name. ⛔ Buried in neither of the two family cemeteries. Not at Forest Hill, Birmingham — his wife’s and infant daughter’s ground, all 91 Bell memorials checked, no Henry at all [BELL-102]. And not at Bethlehem. All 16 Bell memorials in the family cemetery are listed and no Henry is among them [BELL-84]. Death bracketed 24 Jan 1944 × 29 Dec 1956, well supported — TWO complete sibling enumerations omit him: his mother's obituary of 31 Dec 1956 names six surviving children [BELL-79], and his brother W. W. Bell's of 20 Oct 1960 names four sisters and a brother, singular [BELL-90]. ⚠ He had a 1940 desertion conviction and had left the state, so an estranged son could be omitted while living; strong evidence, not proof. ⭐ ADPH certificate #5206 — John H Bell, d. 27 Feb 1951, HOUSTON CO. (Dothan, his mother's town) — is the highest-value order in the project [BELL-80]. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘part’ → ‘ok’, 6 Aug 2026, AT ALEC’S CHALLENGE [AUDIT-01]. The colour grades WHO THIS PERSON IS AND WHOSE CHILD THEY ARE — not whether every fact about them is known. Loose ends belong in Outstanding, below, and this node has some. They do not make the ancestor uncertain. ⚠ Name-collision warning [BELL-104]: Barbour County held a SECOND, unrelated John Henry Bell — b. ~1890, eldest son of B. C. Bell, a Eufaula butcher, named in full in a 1910 probate petition. Eliminated as ours on age by eleven years against five records, but any Barbour "John Henry Bell" must be checked against that family first. THE WALL. Solid to 1940: m. Beatrice Eakens 13 May 1927, Birmingham; dry cleaner 1928, truck driver 1930, house painter 1940; in Atlanta c.1933–36. Convicted of desertion and non-support in Jefferson County 17 June 1940 (Juv. & Dom. Rel. case 6552, Judge Emmett Perry); released 17 Oct 1940. ⭐ Registered for the draft at Cincinnati on 23 February 1942 — Local Board No. 19, 1206 Baymiller Street — giving his birthplace as Barbour County, Alabama and naming Mrs. M. K. Norton of 118 North St., Dothan as the person who would always know his address. He signed it himself. It is the last document he is known to have signed.

Outstanding: ⭐ His parents are now established [BELL-117]. His death is not — bracketed 24 Jan 1944 × 29 Dec 1956 by absence from two complete sibling enumerations, with the estrangement caveat. ⚠ And no instrument names him as Tom and Alice’s son in words: he predeceased every record that lists their children, so the identification is proved indirectly. Each of his six siblings has such a document; he alone has none. ⛔ Illinois is now cleared [BELL-105] — every John and Henry Bell death in the state 1944–1956 was listed from the Illinois State Archives database, and no John or Henry Bell died in DuPage County at any point between 1916 and 1972. Bounded: given names searched exactly, and the database carries no age before 1948. Birth is settled at ~1901–02 [BELL-115]. Sources: 1901 (family compilation), 1903 (1940 census), ~1901-02 (1920 census, as TJB’s son “Henry”) — and his 1927 marriage licence blank reads “21” — ⭐ which is the majority formula, not an age: the packet’s own affidavit says “he is OVER 21 years of age,” while the bride’s “18” is exact because her age mattered legally. Both Birmingham papers that week printed “John Henry Bell, 25.” [BELL-70, BELL-115]. No county and no father’s name on any of it. Disappearance better dated Oct 1940 – Feb 1942 than “around 1942.”

10 Aug 2026 [BELL-63/64/66]: m. Margaret Beatrice Eaken Friday 13 May 1927, Birmingham (the News ran a fearless-bridegroom feature; register Vol. 108 p. 276). Truck driver for a dry-cleaner, 1348 Huntsville Rd (1930); painter, 71 Ormond Ave SE, Atlanta (1936). Named his firstborn THOMAS J. for his father — the boy died at 16 on 22 Jan 1944 after a car crash at Warrenville, Illinois — ⭐ and his death certificate puts the whole family at Wheaton, DuPage County, Illinois, a state this project had never searched [BELL-101]. It also names the father plainly as "Henry Bell", the notice naming brothers John Henry Jr., Billy Jean, “Bobby” (8) and sister Carolyn, and grandmothers Mrs. M. K. Norton and Mrs. Margaret Bloebaum. Walked out ~1942; John Henry Jr. took his SSN at fourteen (Jan 1943); no record of the father after 1942 has ever been found.

⛔ Sweep K, 12 Aug 2026 [BELL-105]: the Illinois State Archives death database (1916–1972, statewide) was searched in full. No John Bell and no Henry Bell died in DuPage County — the family’s own county in Jan 1944 — at any point across those 56 years. The one strong candidate, a John H Bell dying at Aurora, Kane Co. on 13 Mar 1944 in the very city his son died in seven weeks earlier, is refuted: age 72, b. 1871 Pembroke KY, father George Bell, race “Col”. ⭐ His draft card’s unindexed identifying mark is now transcribed — “knot on the left leg near the hip” [BELL-107] — the kind of detail that can name a man in a coroner’s or poorhouse record when no index will.

Margaret Beatrice Eaken (10 Jul 1908 Sumter Co. AL – 16 Jan 1983 Fulton Co. GA)

Ahnentafel #9 · his father’s father’s mother (3 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Margaret Beatrice Eaken was born on 10 July 1908 at Cuba, in Sumter County, Alabama — her mother's home county, where her father would later be buried. She was eighteen when she married John Henry Bell on Friday the thirteenth of May 1927, and their first son was born that November.

She had six children by him. He left about 1942, and by January 1944 she had taken the family north to Wheaton, Illinois, where her eldest son was killed in a car crash at sixteen. She remarried in 1948 — Claude B. Smith — and the 1950 census finds her at Lineville, Clay County, Alabama, with her four surviving Bell children in the household.

She spent her later years in Georgia, at Clarkston, and retired from Rich's department store in Atlanta. She died on 16 January 1983, aged seventy-four, and was brought back to Birmingham for burial at Forest Hill, where her infant daughter Marion had been buried half a century before. Her obituary ran in both cities and listed her husband, her four children, her mother and her two brothers. It did not mention her first husband.

For a long time the family believed she died in 1985 — but 1985 was the year her own mother died, and the two had quietly become confused.

Evidence

Photographed summer 1961 holding her infant grandson [BELL-78]. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘part’ → ‘ok’, 6 Aug 2026, AT ALEC’S CHALLENGE [AUDIT-01]. The colour grades WHO THIS PERSON IS AND WHOSE CHILD THEY ARE — not whether every fact about them is known. Remarried Claude B. Smith in 1948; in the 1950 census at Lineville, Clay Co., Alabama with four Bell stepchildren. Middle initial B proven.

⭐ Her death date is settled at last [BELL-97]. Her Find a Grave memorial gives 16 January 1983, Fulton County, Georgia, agreeing exactly with the Alabama death index — and it gives her birthplace as Cuba, Sumter County, her mother’s home county, which is independent corroboration nobody could have reverse-engineered. The family’s “1985” is withdrawn: that was her mother’s death year (self-corrections #42–#43). She is buried at Forest Hill Cemetery, Birmingham, Section 19.

⭐ Her obituary was found in both cities [BELL-106] — the Atlanta Constitution of 19 January 1983 and the Birmingham News of the 18th. She was living at Clarkston, Georgia, and had retired as a saleswoman at Rich’s department store in Atlanta; she was a member of Vestavia Alliance Church. Her mother Maggie Bloebaum outlived her. ⛔ Neither notice names her first husband.

Outstanding: Her obituary is in the Atlanta papers of mid-January 1983 — not the Birmingham ones, where everyone has been looking. It would list her children, and may name her first husband.

⭐ Her obituary found in BOTH cities, 12 Aug 2026 [BELL-106] — Atlanta Constitution 19 Jan 1983 p.31 and Birmingham News 18 Jan 1983 p.27. A third, contemporaneous witness to the 16 Jan 1983 death (“died Sunday” — 16 Jan 1983 was a Sunday) and to burial at Forest Hill. It gives her a working life: a retired saleswoman at Rich’s department store in Atlanta, latterly of Clarkston, Georgia, a member of Vestavia Alliance Church. It names her four surviving children, and her mother as Mrs. Maggie Bloebaum — which identifies the informant on her son’s 1944 Illinois death certificate [BELL-101]. ⛔ It does not name John Henry Bell, in either version.

Jess Steve Willard Stephens (29 Apr 1917 – 18 Mar 1971)

Ahnentafel #10 · his father’s mother’s father (3 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Jess Steve Willard Stephens was born on 29 April 1917 and died on 18 March 1971 in Charleston, South Carolina, having worked as a mechanic. He was named for Jess Willard, heavyweight champion of the world just thirteen months before his birth, and went by both “Willard” and “J. W.” through his life. In 1920 his family lived at Election Precinct 6, York, in Sumter County — the very precinct where the Swain family had lived twenty years earlier.

Evidence

Mechanic; died at Charleston, South Carolina. Christened for Jess Willard, heavyweight champion of the world thirteen months earlier, and signed himself both “Willard” and “J. W.” — which is what made him hard to find. In 1920 at Election Precinct 6, York, Sumter Co., the same precinct as the Swains in 1900.

Grace Estelle Truett (14 Jan 1917 – 9 Nov 1992)

Ahnentafel #11 · his father’s mother’s mother (3 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Grace Estelle Truett was born on 14 January 1917 in Clarke County, Alabama, and by 1920 was living at Coffeeville. She married at least three times — to a Stephens in 1939, a Rivers in 1947, and later a Roberts — and appears in Social Security records under the name “Grace Tru Morgan,” suggesting a fourth marriage as well. She died on 9 November 1992 and is buried at Tuscaloosa Memorial Park.

Evidence

Born Clarke Co.; in 1920 at Coffeeville. Married at least three times — Stephens 1939, Rivers 1947, Roberts — and appears in Social Security records as “Grace Tru Morgan,” implying a fourth. Buried Tuscaloosa Memorial Park.

Gifford Herman Tuttle (7 Oct 1903 Indianapolis – Dec 1974)

Ahnentafel #12 · his mother’s father’s father (3 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Gifford Herman Tuttle was born on 7 October 1903 in Indianapolis and died in December 1974. He married Helen May Knott on 2 April 1923 in Rush County. The family lived in Terre Haute from 1910 to 1940 before moving to Evanston, Illinois, by about 1945. His own name almost certainly honors Gifford H. Spear, the stepfather who raised his father Dennis after Dennis's own father died young. A photograph survives of him with Helen in the early 1970s, the two of them hand in hand and wearing flowers, most likely at their fiftieth anniversary.

Evidence

Photographed with Helen c.1973, probably their golden wedding [TUTTLE-87]. m. Helen May Knott 2 Apr 1923, Rush Co. Terre Haute 1910–1940, Evanston by about 1945. Named almost certainly for Gifford H. Spear, his father’s stepfather — the man who raised Dennis after Richard B. Tuttle died.

Helen May Knott (b. 12 Jun 1905, IN – d. 24 Apr 1977, Evanston, IL)

Ahnentafel #13 · his mother’s father’s mother (3 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Helen May Knott was born on 12 June 1905 in Indiana and died on 24 April 1977 at Evanston Hospital, Illinois, at the age of seventy-one. Her parents are named on her 1923 marriage application to Gifford Tuttle, and again decades later in her son Robert Dale's obituary. Her death notice named her surviving sons, Robert D. of Evanston and Richard C. of Cincinnati, and her sister Mildred Cooper of Cedar Rapids. Three photographs survive of her childhood in Terre Haute about 1911 to 1913: one on the lawn beside her big sister Mildred, seven years her elder, which their mother sent as a postcard inscribed “My too sweet dauters, Mildred and Helen”; and two of Helen with her friend Grace, both girls in wide straw hats. The friend was almost certainly Grace Estella Schomer, born six months after Helen in December 1905, the daughter of the man who ran Terre Haute's dancing school — a child performer the local paper once called “the miniature Pavlowa.” She lived to be ninety-seven. A hand-tinted studio portrait shows her in her late thirties, and another photograph catches her with Gifford in the early 1970s, near their fiftieth anniversary.

Evidence

Hand-tinted studio portrait c.1940–45 [KNOTT-24]; with Gifford c.1973 [TUTTLE-87]. Childhood photographs c.1911–13: with her sister Mildred, on a postcard inscribed and signed by their mother [KNOTT-22]; and twice with her friend Grace Shomer [KNOTT-21, KNOTT-23, KNOTT-26]. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘part’ → ‘ok’, 6 Aug 2026, AT ALEC’S CHALLENGE [AUDIT-01]. The colour grades WHO THIS PERSON IS AND WHOSE CHILD THEY ARE — not whether every fact about them is known. Loose ends belong in Outstanding, below, and this node has some. They do not make the ancestor uncertain. Named with both parents on the 1923 marriage application, and again in her son Robert Dale’s 2007 obituary — doubly sourced. The Tuttles were lifelong members of St. Luke’s Episcopal, Evanston, where Robert Dale was interred.

Outstanding: Death not documented. Enquiry sent to St. Luke’s for her death and the Tuttle burials from ~1945. Awaiting reply.

⭐⭐ DEATH CLOSED (probable), 7 Aug 2026 [KNOTT-07]: Wilmette Life, 5 May 1977 — died 24 Apr 1977, Evanston Hospital, age 71; sons Robert D. (Evanston) and Richard C. (Cincinnati); sister Mildred Cooper of Cedar Rapids. Locks the SSDI candidate (b. 12 Jun 1905). Primary-page capture queued.

Alan C. Frost (b. Feb 1903, Paterson NJ – unknown)

Ahnentafel #14 · his mother’s mother’s father (3 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Alan C. Frost was born in February 1903 in Paterson, New Jersey. He managed a dime store in Mansfield, Ohio, by 1940, and had moved to Marion, Indiana, by 1950. By October 1964 he was living at 316 West Dartmouth in Flint, Michigan — a chapter of his life discovered only through his wife Velma's cremation certificate. His own death has not yet been found. The earliest picture of him is a studio photograph taken about 1903 or 1904, when he was still an infant in a long white gown, seated between his older sister and brother — Katherine and Harry, each named for a parent.

Evidence

Photographed as an infant c.1903–04 with his siblings Katherine and Harry [FROST-30]. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘part’ → ‘ok’, 6 Aug 2026, AT ALEC’S CHALLENGE [AUDIT-01]. The colour grades WHO THIS PERSON IS AND WHOSE CHILD THEY ARE — not whether every fact about them is known. Loose ends belong in Outstanding, below, and this node has some. They do not make the ancestor uncertain. 1940 census Mansfield OH (dime-store manager); 1950 Marion IN; and by Oct 1964 at 316 West Dartmouth, Flint, Michigan — a state nobody knew about, found on Velma’s cremation certificate.

Outstanding: Death unknown; a Michigan search returned nothing. Enquiry submitted to White Chapel Memorial, Troy MI, asking whether he is interred with Velma.

Velma Gladys Roush (21 Jun 1907 Mason Co. WV – 3 Oct 1964 Flint MI)

Ahnentafel #15 · his mother’s mother’s mother (3 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Velma Gladys Roush was born on 21 June 1907 in Mason County, West Virginia, the daughter of John G. Roush and Bertha E. Roush, as recorded on the original birth register. She was five feet tall, ran away from home at sixteen, and her school yearbook remembered her as “small but oh my!” She died on 3 October 1964 in Flint, Michigan, of a perforated duodenum after years of high-dose aspirin taken for arthritis. She was, for years, the hardest figure in the family to trace, until a cremation certificate found in a family photo album, together with her granddaughter Cynthia's own memory of her, finally identified her. Two photographs of her survive: a studio portrait, and a snapshot of her laughing outdoors in a plaid coat in her fifties.

Evidence

Photographed c.1957–64, outdoors in a plaid coat [FROST-31]. The hardest wall in the project until 5 Aug 2026 — broken by a cremation certificate in a family photo album plus Cynthia’s memory of her grandmother. Five feet tall; ran away from home at sixteen; “She’s small but oh my!” in her yearbook; died of a perforated duodenum after years of high-dose aspirin for arthritis.

Outstanding: Her 1931 first marriage to Alfred Hesterberg and her marriage to Alan Frost both need confirming.

⭐ Primary birth register read at native resolution, 7 Aug 2026: WV VRR id 2981877 (assessor’s register, Graham District, 1907, p. 37 line 44) — b. 21 June 1907, father John G. Roush, mother/informant Bertha E. Roush. A primary record for the parent links, independent of the tree-based citation.

Thomas Jefferson Bell (b. 1875×1878 – d. 30 Aug 1921, near Louisville, Barbour Co., Alabama)

Ahnentafel #16 · his father’s father’s father’s father (4 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Thomas Jefferson Bell farmed near Louisville in Barbour County, Alabama. He married Alice May Bradley on 13 August 1895, when she was eighteen, and they had nine children — seven still living when the 1920 census found them together, and two already buried at Bethlehem Baptist churchyard.

He died on 30 August 1921 and was laid beside those two children. He left no estate: the county's probate minutes for the year after his death record no administration in his name, and the clerk's index to seventy years of administrators' records contains no Thomas J. Bell at all. There was simply nothing to divide.

So when his children finally received something, it came through his own father's estate instead. In April 1925 a Barbour County court divided one deceased heir's share seven ways among Tom's children, naming each of them — Ellie, Ray, Ruth, William, Ethel, little Juanita, and “Henry Bell, Birmingham.” That decree is the court's own answer to the question of who his children were, and it is the document that finally settled this family's longest-standing puzzle.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘probable’ → ‘documented’, 12 August 2026, on the EIGHTH adversarial review [BELL-117] — after seven refusals. What changed: the rival reading of “Mrs. M. K. Norton” died with Katie Bradley in April 1924; Mack Norton’s own 1954 obituary calls Alice’s children Bells; and the 1925 probate decree was re-read as what it is — a court’s adjudication of who Thomas Jefferson Bell’s children were, paying seven named persons exactly one seventh each of a deceased heir’s share, with “Henry Bell, Birmingham” among them.

⭐ Corroborated from the Norton side [BELL-108]: his widow Alice remarried Mack K. Norton, whose 1954 obituary calls her sons T. R. and W. W. Bell — the same sibling set Thomas Ray Bell Sr.’s own obituary gives twenty years later, from the opposite side of the family. Mack joined Bethlehem Baptist near Clayton, this family’s church and burying ground, where Tom lies and where Alice is buried under the name Bell, not Norton.

Buried Bethlehem Baptist Church Cemetery; Find a Grave memorial 138198515 links him to John T. Bell and Sallie Cox as parents — corroboration of a hypothesis, not evidence [BELL-84]. ⚠ That memorial gives d. 27 Aug 1920 against the Clayton Record obituary's 30 Aug 1921; one is wrong by a year and the conflict is unresolved. Identified as John Henry Bell's father at probable — the chain runs through his widow, not through a document naming him [BELL-76, BELL-77]. Married Alice May Bradly 13 Aug 1895, Barbour Co. Direct Marriage Index vol. 13 p. 63. Obituary, Clayton Record 2 Sep 1921: d. 30 Aug 1921, son of John Bell Sr., buried Bethlehem “where two of his children are buried.” 1920 census, Louisville: Thomas J 44, Alice 42, Henry 18, Ray 16, William 12, Ethel 10, Juanita 2.

Outstanding: No record yet names John Henry Bell's father. His own age is unsettled — 44 in Jan 1920 (b. ~1875) against “about forty-three” in the Sept 1921 obituary (b. ~1878); the chart's old “1874” was supported by nothing. The Barbour sweep for competing Henry Bells is now RUN [BELL-82]: across the 1900, 1910 and 1920 censuses there is exactly one Henry Bell born 1899×1903 in the county, and he is Tom and Alice's son. No rival exists.

Alice May Bradley (8 Oct 1876 – 29 Dec 1956, Montgomery Co., Alabama; m.(2) Mack K. Norton)

Ahnentafel #17 · his father’s father’s father’s mother (4 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Alice May Bradley was born on 8 October 1876 in Barbour County, Alabama, the daughter of Henry Bradley and Elvira Loveless. Her father died young: in October 1891 a county court appointed a guardian for Alice, then fourteen, and her sister Katie, twelve, noting that the minors had no father living.

She married Thomas Jefferson Bell on 13 August 1895, at eighteen, and had nine children. Widowed in 1921, she married Mack K. Norton — and there is a quiet symmetry in it, because Mack's first wife had been her own sister Katie, who died in 1924. Two Bradley sisters each married a Bell, each was widowed, and the same man married them in turn.

By 1935 she was living with Mack at 118 East North Street in Dothan, and it was that address her son wrote on his draft card in 1942 when the form asked who would always know where he was. Mack died in 1954; his obituary called her children his step-sons and step-daughters, naming them as Bells, which is how the family was finally proved from the outside.

She outlived her husband by thirty-five years and died at Montgomery on 29 December 1956, aged eighty. They brought her home to Bethlehem, in Barbour County, and buried her beside him under the name Bell — not Norton.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘probable’ → ‘documented’, 12 August 2026, on the EIGHTH adversarial review [BELL-117] — after seven refusals. What changed: the rival reading of “Mrs. M. K. Norton” died with Katie Bradley in April 1924; Mack Norton’s own 1954 obituary calls Alice’s children Bells; and the 1925 probate decree was re-read as what it is — a court’s adjudication of who Thomas Jefferson Bell’s children were, paying seven named persons exactly one seventh each of a deceased heir’s share, with “Henry Bell, Birmingham” among them.

⭐⭐ The Norton question is settled [BELL-108]. Mack K. Norton’s own obituary (The Clayton Record, 22 Oct 1954) names his wife as “Mrs. Alice May Norton of Dothan” and her children as step-sons T. R. Bell of Bessemer and W. W. Bell of Jacksonville, with four step-daughters whose married names and towns match Thomas Ray Bell Sr.’s own 1974 obituary twenty years later. So the “Mrs. M. K. Norton, 118 North St., Dothan” whom John Henry Bell named on his 1942 draft card is Alice — and the competing reading, that she was Katie the aunt, is dead: Katie Bradley Norton died 11 April 1924, eighteen years before that card.

⭐ And Katie was Alice’s sister. Both women’s state death records give the same parents, Henry Bradley and Elvira Loveless. Two Bradley sisters each married a Bell and each was widowed; Mack Norton married Katie in 1912 and, after she died, married Alice. Mack joined his wife’s first husband’s church at Bethlehem and was buried apart from her.

⭐ Named with Tom as a parental couple on a state vital record: their daughter Ruth's Texas death certificate gives father Tom Bell, mother Alice Bradley [BELL-91], and their son Thomas Ray's Alabama death record gives Thomas Jefferson Bell and Alice Bradley [BELL-95]. Two children, two states, two vital records. Identified at probable. Maiden name attested in the Barbour Co. marriage index: T. J. Bell × Alice May Bradly, 13 Aug 1895 [BELL-76]. 1940 census, Dothan, ED 35-8, sheet 3B, house 118 — Mack K. Norton head, Alice M. wife; supplementary line 55 gives age at first marriage 18, nine children born, and “married more than once: Yes”, which orders the two marriages Bell-then-Norton [BELL-77]. 1935 residence: same house — seven years before John Henry gave that address on his draft card. Died 29 Dec 1956, Montgomery (Alabama Death Index, cert. #26068); buried under the name Bell at Bethlehem, the cemetery Tom's 1921 obituary named [self-correction #32].

Outstanding: ⭐ ADPH certificate #26068 names HER parents — and after the 1895 licence turned out to carry no parents' line [BELL-81], it is now the ONLY live route to the Bradley generation. Her own obituary, Clayton Record late Dec 1956, would list surviving children and is the document that would take both these nodes to documented. No marriage record for Alice × Norton exists after four bounded searches; the remarriage is proven by household, not by a return. Birth year wobbles: 1877 by the 1891 guardianship, 1876 by Find a Grave, whose provenance is unverified.

Dennis Clark Tuttle (25 Jan 1884 Peoria IL – 22 Aug 1975)

Ahnentafel #24 · his mother’s father’s father’s father (4 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Dennis Clark Tuttle was born on 25 January 1884 in Peoria, Illinois, and died on 22 August 1975. He worked forty-three years as a freight car repairman for the Pennsylvania Railroad, retiring onto its Roll of Honor, and was a Freemason, serving as Prophet of the Kerman Grotto in Terre Haute. He registered for the “Old Man's Draft” in April 1942 and, in his sixties, drew twenty dollars a week from Indiana unemployment during a lean stretch. He was married for sixty-seven years, and his own wallet, carried through much of that life, survives among the family's papers.

Evidence

Freight car repairman, Pennsylvania Railroad, 43 years; retired onto the PRR Roll of Honor; Railroad Retirement Board number 717-03-2620. A Freemason — Prophet of Kerman Grotto, Terre Haute. Registered in the “Old Man’s Draft,” April 1942. Drew $20 a week from Indiana unemployment in his sixties. His wallet survived: TUTTLE-02 to TUTTLE-10. Married 67 years.

Outstanding: Grand Lodge of Indiana Blue Lodge record — free, and Masonic bodies answer descendants.

Leona Marie Alexander (16 Feb 1883 Clinton IN – 13 Sep 1970)

Ahnentafel #25 · his mother’s father’s father’s mother (4 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Leona Marie Alexander was born on 16 February 1883 in Clinton, Indiana, and died on 13 September 1970. She married Dennis Tuttle on New Year's Eve, 1902 — their son Gifford arrived nine months and six days later, which is how the date of the marriage was pinned down against a family tradition of 1903. She was a life member of the Daughters of Mokanna, Kerman Caldron No. 19, and is buried alongside Dennis at Highland Lawn Cemetery. Her obituary records her as a member of St. Stephen's Episcopal Church in Terre Haute, where her funeral was held — the earliest sign of a family that had been Congregational for six generations turning Episcopalian.

Evidence

A member of St. Stephen's Episcopal Church, Terre Haute, where her funeral was held in Sept 1970 — the earliest evidence of this family's Episcopal affiliation, and it is in Terre Haute, not Evanston [TUTTLE-90]. m. Dennis on New Year’s Eve 1902 — not 1903; Gifford was born nine months and six days later. Life member of the Daughters of Mokanna, Kerman Caldron #19, 1964. Buried with Dennis at Highland Lawn.

John T. Bell was born on 10 June 1841 and died on 6 November 1915 near Louisville, in Barbour County, Alabama. He is believed — pending final confirmation of the Barbour County identification — to be the father of Thomas Jefferson Bell, and so a link further back in Alec's own line.

Ahnentafel #33 · his father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (5 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Sarah Cox married John T. Bell on 7 March 1861 in Barbour County, Alabama. He was twenty; the war began the following month.

They raised a large family — at least eight sons and two daughters survived to be named in his will twenty-five years after her death — and one of those sons was Thomas Jefferson Bell. In April 1888 she signed her name beside her husband’s on a deed selling a right of way to the Eufaula & East Alabama Railway, giving up her dower in about forty-six acres of it.

She died in 1910, five years before John, and by the time his will was probated no widow was named.

Ahnentafel #54 · his mother’s father’s mother’s mother’s father (5 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Erwin R. Lamb was born on 19 March 1840 in Wayne County, Indiana, and later lived at Newport, in Vermillion County. He was the son of Elias Lamb, who had married Susannah Bish in 1837, and grandson of Joseph and Lydia (Adamson) Lamb, Quakers who had moved from Randolph County, North Carolina, to Wayne County, Indiana, in April 1829. Erwin is believed to be the father of Martha Ellen Lamb — her death certificate names him, the 1880 census places her in his household, and an 1888 biographical sketch of the family lists her among his children — though a small conflict in the recorded dates keeps the identification at “probable” rather than fully settled.

Son of Elias Lamb (b. 1814; m. Susannah Bish 1837) and grandson of Joseph & Lydia (Adamson) Lamb, Quakers of Randolph Co., NC → Wayne Co., IN, Apr 1829. The family’s Wayne→Vermillion move ~1870 matches Martha’s 1865 Richmond (Wayne Co.) birth exactly.

Moses B. Roush was born in 1842 and served as a private in Companies A and B of the 28th Ohio Infantry, known as Ohio's “2nd German Regiment,” enlisting on 23 July 1864 and discharged on 6 July 1865. He filed for an invalid pension in 1880 based on his service. He married Sarah Duckworth on 1 September 1870 in Orange Township, Meigs County, Ohio. He died on 23 August 1925 in Mason County, West Virginia; his death certificate is the record that named both of his parents.

Evidence

Sarah Duckworth 1 Sep 1870, Orange Twp., Meigs Co., Ohio [ROUSH-20, ROUSH-28, ROUSH-30].

Lewis J. Nease (20 Nov 1854 – 17 Feb 1917, Waggener District, Mason Co., WV)

Ahnentafel #62 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s mother’s father (5 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Lewis J. Nease was born on 20 November 1854 and died on 17 February 1917 in the Waggener District of Mason County, West Virginia. For years the family record carried the wrong name in this place — a mistaken “J. W. Nease” — but the puzzle was solved by reading two records closely: his daughter Bertha's 1897 marriage license, on which he gave the information as “L. J. Nease,” and his own 1917 death register entry, which recorded his exact age at death and worked back precisely to his 20 November 1854 birth date, matching the family genealogy published in 1928. The 1900 census confirms his marriage to Martha (Love) in 1877, and county birth records from the 1890s likewise name him and Martha as parents. His own parents are not yet known.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THIS NODE WAS “J. W. NEASE” UNTIL 7 AUGUST 2026. IT IS NOW SETTLED [ROUSH-53]. Two threads had closed this slot with two different men and both closures were still on disk. Three record images were read at native resolution and the answer is Lewis J. Nease.

The decider — his daughter’s own marriage licence, Mason County, 22 February 1897 (WV VRR marriage Id 12211313): “The name of the party giving the foregoing information is L. J. NEASE of Mason County, State of West Virginia.” The looping capital L is unmistakable beside the J of “John Roush” written three lines above in the same hand — and the party who gives a bride’s age and birthplace to the clerk is her father.

And his own death register entry dates him exactly. WV VRR death Id 5286043, Register of Deaths p. 120 line 6: “Nease, Lewis — Waggener — married — 62 [yrs] 2 [mos] 28 [days] — Laborer — Heart trouble.” Work the age backwards and it lands on 20 November 1854 — to the day, the birth date the 1928 Roush genealogy gives Lewis Nease. An age in days on a register and a date in a book, agreeing exactly.

Corroborated four more ways: the 1900 census (Lewis Nease, married 23 years ⇒ 1877, wife Martha born Ohio, one page from Bertha’s own household); the county birth index for 1891 and 1895, both naming Lewis Nease and Martha as parents; and the 1928 Roush genealogy pp. 416–417, whose child list matches the 1900 census nine names out of ten.

⚠ The 1878 birth register really does read “J. W. Nease”, and so does the county’s later typed copy of it — but those are ONE source recorded twice, not two witnesses. Why the assessor wrote the wrong initials is left open rather than smoothed over.

⛔ James W. Nease (1852–1906) is retired as a candidate [ROUSH-53]: no death record anywhere in the West Virginia index for Mason County 1901–1911, and no James Nease anywhere in the 1928 Roush genealogy — a book that reconstructs these very households.

Outstanding: his parents. His 1917 death register carries ditto marks running down from “not given” in both parent columns.

Martha Jane Love (30 Jan 1860, Ohio – 12 Aug 1941, Mason Co., WV)

Ahnentafel #63 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s mother’s mother (5 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Martha Jane Love was born on 30 January 1860 in Ohio and died on 12 August 1941 in Mason County, West Virginia, at the age of eighty-one. The 1928 Roush family genealogy records her marriage to Lewis Nease on 12 April 1877, which fits exactly with the 1900 census and makes her daughter Bertha, born the following year, the eldest of the couple's children. Her own death record confirms her birth date and age precisely; she was buried, a widow, at Broad Run cemetery two days after she died.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ WAS “Martha Nease — married name only” UNTIL 7 AUGUST 2026 [ROUSH-53]. Her maiden name is LOVE, from the 1928 Roush genealogy (“Lewis Nease… Married Martha Love Apr. 12, 1877”) — a marriage date that matches the 1900 census’s “married 23 years” exactly, and puts Bertha, born 1878, as the first child.

Her own death record (WV VRR Id 5290412): died 12 August 1941 at Mason, aged 81y 6m 13d, born 30 January 1860 in OHIO, a widow, buried Broad Run, 14 August 1941 — the same cemetery as the Roushes. The age arithmetic checks out to the day.

⚠ Correction logged: an earlier note cited this record as giving her husband as “Lewis Nease.” The index does not carry a spouse field value — do not cite it that way until the image itself is read.

John (F.) Bell (13 Jan 1807 South Carolina – 21 Nov 1890, Barbour Co., Alabama)

Ahnentafel #64 · his father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

John Bell was born about 1807 in South Carolina and had moved to Barbour County, Alabama before 1837, where all of his children were born. The 1850 census finds him at forty-three, farming, with his wife Laney — also South Carolina born — and five children at home.

Two of those children matter to this family: William, aged eleven, who would die a Confederate pensioner in 1924 leaving an estate that scattered across his nieces and nephews; and John, aged nine, who would become John T. Bell and father Thomas Jefferson Bell.

His son’s obituary says he came to Alabama from Darlington, South Carolina, about 1830, and settled in the neighbourhood where the family stayed for the next century. He is the point at which this line leaves Alabama — and Darlington District is where everything before him has to be looked for.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ Re-graded probable → documented, 12 Aug 2026 [BELL-132], on adversarial review. The link is carried by four independent instruments: his son William’s 1924 death record naming the father John F. Bell; the 1891 partition, whose guardian-ad-litem notice calls the Farrior children “minor heirs of John Bell deceased” and so records descent rather than mere co-ownership; the 1925 estate dividing per stirpes with John T. Bell’s line in the same generational row as his sister’s; and ⭐ John T. Bell’s own 1915 obituary — “His parents moved to Alabama from DARLINGTON, S.C., about 1830 and settled in the neighborhood where he lived at his death.”

⭐ His farm is named [BELL-129]. In November 1891, the year after he died, his heirs petitioned to sell 604 acres in Barbour County for division — land described as containing “the John Bell homestead and out houses” and “the John Bell old home place.” It went in equal thirds to W. W. Bell, Jno. T. Bell and the five children of a daughter who had married a Farrior. The boundaries name the neighbours, and they explain a good deal: the Daniel Norton land lay to the south, which is why two Bell wives later married a Norton; the old William Beasley place too, and his son William was buried in the Beasley family cemetery.

Head of a Barbour County household in the 1850 census — John Bell, 43, born South Carolina — with his wife Laney, 42, also South Carolina, and children Mary 13, William 11, John 9, Kervin 4 and Laney 2 [BELL-123]. His son William W. Bell’s 1924 death record names his parents as John F. Bell and Viney Bell, sixty-four years later and independently.

Outstanding: ⚠ Probable rather than documented for one specific reason: the step from “John Bell, aged 9” in this household to the John T. Bell who died in 1915 rests on name, age and county, and Barbour County held more than one Bell family. His own dates, his wife’s maiden name, and where in South Carolina he came from are all unknown.

⭐ 13 Aug 2026 [BELL-151] — the 1870 census, never read before, puts him and his son on adjacent lines of one page. Township 10 Range 25, Barbour County, page 183, enumerated 8 August 1870, post office Louisville: John Bell, 63, farmer, born South Carolina, with Laney, 64, and the two grown children still at home, Mary 29 and Archibald 22. ⭐⭐ The very next dwelling is John T. Bell, 27, farmer, with Sarah, 27, and their children John 8, Mack 4, William 2 and Sarah 1 — the four eldest of the eight sons and daughters a probate court would name in 1915. ⭐ And the dwelling before his is Martha Beasley: the 1891 partition bounds the 604 acres “on the south by the old William Beasley place,” and his son William W. Bell would be buried in Beasley Family Cemetery. The neighbour on the schedule is the neighbour in the deed.

⭐ 25 Aug 2026 [BELL-529]: his later life recovered — ALIVE at 72 in the 1880 census (Clayton Beat 7), widowed (so Laney died 1870×1880), declaring both parents born in South Carolina — the necessary condition on node 128, sworn in his own household. On 1 August 1890, aged 83, he divided the home place by deeds of gift — 203⅓ acres each to son Curvin A. and daughter Mary O., reserving “the ownership and comfort of said above described lands during my natural life time” — which is why he has no probate file; died 1890×1900. A second John-and-Lany deed surfaced (to Wm M. Bounds, 1858, acknowledged 1889), and the 1890 deed fixes the home place exactly: “the big gate leading to the house” on the Clayton–Louisville road, Sections 11–14, T.10 R.25.

Laney Bell (b. about 1806 in South Carolina; died between August 1870 and June 1880, Barbour Co., Alabama)

Ahnentafel #65 · his father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (6 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

She was born in South Carolina about 1806 and came to Alabama as a young wife. In August 1840 she is a tally mark in a column — one woman aged thirty to forty in John Bell’s house in Barbour County, with a baby boy and a little girl. In 1850 she has a name, Laney, and five children. In 1860 she is fifty-three, and the family is prosperous: her husband returns four thousand dollars in land and ten thousand in personal estate. In 1870 she is sixty-four, keeping house, with her grown daughter Mary and her son Archibald still at home, and her son John T. Bell farming the next place along with a wife and four small children.

Then she is gone. By June 1880 her husband is enumerated as a widower, living with a lodger and a girl of fourteen, his children grown and scattered. A gravestone in the Beasley family’s little burying ground is said to give the date as 17 June 1873, though no one has photographed it.

Fifty-one years after she died, her eldest son’s death certificate called her Viney. Three census-takers had called her Laney. Nobody now alive knows which she answered to.

Evidence

⭐⭐ Her name is LANEY, and as of 13 Aug 2026 that is a manuscript reading rather than an index transcription [BELL-149, BELL-151]. Three census images have now been rendered at native resolution and read by eye — 1850 (aged 42), 1860 (53) and 1870 (64) — in three different hands, all writing Laney. ⭐ The 1860 enumerator wrote “Post Office Louisville” in his own header and “S. Carolina” in the birthplace column of the same line: the capital in her name is his L, not his C. ⭐ The FamilySearch index reads her 1870 age as 44; the manuscript reads 64, formed like the 6 in her husband’s 63 on the line above, and that correction has been checked twice.

⭐ She is in the household before the children were born. The 1840 Barbour schedule, whose female columns were unread until now, tallies one female aged 30–40 beside John at 30–40, with one boy and one girl under five — William, born February 1839, and Mary. ⭐ Her death is dated by record: the household is intact in August 1870 and John Bell is enumerated widowed in June 1880.

⚠ Held at probable on a second adversarial review, 13 Aug 2026 [BELL-151], and the reason is precise. Everything above establishes who she was. Nothing states that she was John T. Bell’s mother in words — the schedules of 1850, 1860 and 1870 carry no relationship column, so four enumerations are one kind of evidence four times over. And the single record in this family that does name a mother — William W. Bell’s 1924 Alabama death record, certificate 4736 — calls her Viney. ⚠ Viney is a clipping of Lavinia; Laney is a clipping of Elaine or Delaney; no one name yields both, so the easy reconciliation does not exist. The live alternative is two wives, and it requires no misreading of anything.

Outstanding: ⭐ She has a grave: Find a Grave memorial 68717758, “Laney Hurst Bell,” died 17 June 1873, BEASLEY FAMILY CEMETERY, Barbour County — the same small burying ground as her son William W. Bell, on the farm that bounded theirs. ⚠ There is no photograph, the entry is contributor-made, and the surname Hurst is a hypothesis only — it is also the mother’s surname on the death record of a different C. A. Bell, so it may be a compiler’s error carried across. It is not on this chart as her name. ⭐ What would settle her: a photograph of the stone; or Alabama certificate 475 of 1915 for John T. Bell, the only document that would name his own mother; or certificate 4736 of 1924, to see whether Viney is the informant’s word or the indexer’s.

Ahnentafel #72 · his father’s father’s mother’s father’s father’s father (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

John Eakins Sr. was born about 1795 in New Hanover County, North Carolina, and was still living there after 1880. As a young man he took on the guardianship of his own younger siblings at the February 1819 court term. He is documented in the 1850 census and, later, in a sworn affidavit he gave in 1863, which survives inside his son's Confederate service file.

Two of his sons served in the 18th North Carolina Infantry during the Civil War; John J. B. was killed at Sharpsburg and Owen J. at Chancellorsville.

Evidence

1850 census [BELL-07]; his own sworn 1863 affidavit survives inside his son’s Confederate service file [BELL-04]. As a young man he took over as guardian of his own younger siblings at the February 1819 court term.

William Payne — possibly a Parrish — is believed to have been born about 1791 in Virginia. Whether he was Sarah's father or her stepfather is not yet settled, so his place in the family is recorded as a candidate rather than a certainty.

Evidence

Henry H. Stephens (8 Aug 1822 North Carolina – Oct 1879, Gaston, Sumter Co., Ala.)

Ahnentafel #80 · his father’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Henry H. Stephens was born on 8 August 1822 in North Carolina and died in October 1879 in Gaston, Sumter County, Alabama. He is the best-documented man on this line, traced through census records from Sumter County, Alabama in 1850, to Newton County, Mississippi in 1860, and back to Cuba, in Sumter County, Alabama, by 1870. In 1850 he was a young farmer living just six houses from his father, Moore Stephens — the first record to tie the two men directly together — and his household included his wife Sarah and infant son Calvin.

Evidence

The best-documented man on this line — 16 sources — with a trail that reads as one continuous migration: 1850 Sumter Co. AL → 1860 Newton Co. MS → 1870 back at Cuba, Sumter Co. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ 6 Aug 2026 — the 1850 census read directly: Henry Stephens, 27, born NORTH CAROLINA, Sumter county, household 1214 / house 1184, with Sarah, 17, born North Carolina and Calvin, 1; boarder Redden Marshall, 23, N.C. ⭐⭐⭐ His father MOORE STEPHENS is in household 1220 / house 1190 — SIX HOUSES AWAY. This is the first census evidence tying node 80 to node 160 directly; everything before it was tree-derived [STEPHENS-16].

Outstanding: ⭐⭐ CALVIN STEPHENS, b. c.1849 — an elder brother of Caswell, and he was not in these notes at all. Follow him into 1860, or find that he died young. ⭐ Next: Henry and Moore in the 1860 Sumter Co. census; and the Sumter Co. 1850 slave schedule for Henry.

Sarah Ann Jones (b. about 1833 in North Carolina; m. 4 June 1846, Sumter Co., Alabama)

Ahnentafel #81 · his father’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s mother (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Sarah Ann Jones was born about 1833 in North Carolina and married Henry H. Stephens on 4 June 1846 in Sumter County, Alabama, when she was only about thirteen. She was the mother of Caswell Stephens, born in January 1852, and possibly of his brothers Calvin and John as well. A Daniel Jones, who died around 1869 in Sumter County, is a promising but not yet confirmed candidate for her father; her own sons were bonded as his minor heirs after her death.

Evidence

m. 4 Jun 1846, Sumter Co.; mother of Caswell (b. Jan 1852). Henry’s second wife was Elizabeth Jones, m. 1854. ⭐⭐⭐ 6 Aug 2026: the 1850 census gives “Sarah Stephens, 17” — born NORTH CAROLINA, which matches her recorded 1833 birth exactly and adds a birthplace that was not previously recorded. She married at about thirteen and had Calvin at about sixteen [STEPHENS-16].

Outstanding: ⭐⭐ Her North Carolina birth opens the JONES line. Look for a Jones household of North Carolina origin in Sumter Co., Alabama, 1850 — that is her parents, and a whole unopened generation. Also: boarder Redden Marshall, 23, b. N.C., in her house — possibly kin.

10 Aug 2026 [STEPHENS-33/-VERDICT]: a Daniel Jones, d. ~1869 Sumter Co., is a named, well-evidenced LEAD for her father — her sons Caswell, Calvin, and John were bonded 31 Dec 1869 as his “minor heirs” (per-stirpes through their dead mother) — but the record is generation-blind (father vs. childless brother), so the adversary refused a node. The decider: Daniel’s own 1869 administration/distribution (same volumes) — a “daughter Sarah” recital confirms slot 162 at part; a sibling-heir recital kills it.

John Wesley Truett (20 Oct 1833 – 17 Apr 1914)

Ahnentafel #88 · his father’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

John Wesley Truett was born on 20 October 1833 and died on 17 April 1914. He served as a private in Company D of the 32nd Alabama Infantry during the Civil War, enlisting on 15 October 1862 at Coffeeville, and was hospitalized within weeks with chronic rheumatism that kept him listed as absent for the rest of the war. A voucher he signed himself at Dalton, Georgia, on 30 May 1863 is the oldest surviving signature of a direct ancestor in this family's records, and he was later enumerated, at age 73, in the 1907 Alabama Census of Confederate Veterans.

Evidence

Private, Co. D, 32nd Alabama Infantry (CSA). Enlisted 15 Oct 1862 at Coffeeville — not May 1862 at Mobile — was in hospital at Dalton, Georgia within 23 days with chronic rheumatism, and is marked absent on every muster roll in the file. Page 11 is a voucher he signed himself at Dalton on 30 May 1863: the oldest signature of a direct ancestor recovered in this project. Enumerated in the 1907 Alabama Census of Confederate Veterans, aged 73.

Outstanding: His 1907 Confederate pension application at ADAH would add unit detail and a physical description.

Ahnentafel #89 · his father’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s mother (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Emily Caroline Ward was born on 25 December 1836 in Henry County, Alabama, and died on 30 November 1922 in Levy County, Florida. In 1917, at the age of 80, she moved with the family to Hardeetown in Levy County, Florida, where she died at Chiefland and was buried in Hardeetown Methodist Cemetery. A photograph of her survives, captioned with her husband's and son's names.

Clark Strong Tuttle (b. 28 Apr 1811 Jefferson Co. NY – 1870s)

Ahnentafel #96 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Clark Strong Tuttle was born on 28 April 1811 in Jefferson County, New York, and died in the 1870s. He was a prosperous miller at Otter Creek Township in Vigo County, Indiana, owning real estate worth $9,500 in 1850 and $14,000 by 1860, and he served as a Vigo County commissioner beginning in October 1858. He married twice: his first wife, known from records only as "H. M.," was born in New Jersey and died between the autumn of 1850 and July 1851, when Clark married Nancy A. Harbert.

Evidence

A MILLER, and a well-off one — read from two census images 6 Aug 2026. Otter Creek Township, Vigo County: 1850, miller, $9,500 real estate; 1860, miller, $14,000 real estate and $9,215 personal, with three hired hands and a servant living in and every school-age child at school [TUTTLE-13, TUTTLE-14]. Vigo County commissioner from October 1858. ⚠⚠ HE MARRIED TWICE. His first wife, “H. M.” Tuttle, aged 37, born NEW JERSEY, is in the 1850 household with Hannah (9), Frank (2) and Aula (1); she died between the autumn of 1850 and 11 July 1851, when he married Nancy A. Harbert.

Outstanding: ⚠ Any note treating Hannah, Frank or Aula as Nancy’s children is wrong. Find “H. M.” — born New Jersey c.1813, dead 1850×1851, Vigo County. ⚠ The family is NOT in Vigo Co. in 1870 (three searches); Dennis C. Tuttle was born at PEORIA, ILLINOIS, so look there.

Elias Lamb (24 Sep 1814 NC – 31 Aug 1890, Vermillion Co., IN)

Ahnentafel #108 · his mother’s father’s mother’s mother’s father’s father (6 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Elias Lamb was born on 24 September 1814 in North Carolina and died on 31 August 1890 in Vermillion County, Indiana. According to an 1888 county history, he was the son of Joseph and Lydia Lamb, married Susannah Bish on 23 March 1837, and celebrated his golden wedding anniversary in 1887. He left the Society of Friends for the United Brethren church in 1841, and his son Erwin's own biographical sketch, printed in the same volume, confirms him as Erwin's father.

Evidence

Susannah Bish 23 Mar 1837; golden wedding 1887) and the children list naming “Azel E., Irvin R.…” — with Erwin’s own sketch (“a son of Elias Lamb”) in the same volume. Benjamin “Bennie” Roush (30 Aug 1816 – 5 Mar 1897)

Ahnentafel #120 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Benjamin "Bennie" Roush was born on 30 August 1816 at Graham Station, Mason County, West Virginia, and died on 5 March 1897. He married Sophia Altman on 23 May 1839 in Orange Township, Meigs County, Ohio, and farmed there before later moving to Syracuse, Ohio, and then back to Wagner District in Mason County, West Virginia. He is buried at Broad Run Cemetery in Mason County, beneath a stone reading "FATHER & MOTHER."

Evidence

Born at Graham Station, Mason Co., West Virginia. m. Sophia Altman 23 May 1839, Orange Township, Meigs County, Ohio — read from the Justice of the Peace’s own return. Farmed in Sutton Township, Meigs Co. (1850, 1860), Syracuse (1870), and Wagner District, Mason Co., W.Va. (1880). Buried Broad Run Cemetery, Mason Co.: “FATHER & MOTHER / BENJAMIN ROUSH / BORN AUG. 30, 1816 / DIED MAR. 5, 1897.” [ROUSH-20, ROUSH-36, ROUSH-36b].

Outstanding: Nothing above him has been worked. Roush = anglicised RAUSCH; the printed Ohio-valley Roush genealogies should be tested against the tree-only “John Blackbird Roush.”

Sophia Altman was born on 1 June 1823 in Ohio and died on 4 July 1898 in Meigs County, Ohio. She was only fifteen years and eleven months old when she married Benjamin Roush in May 1839. James Duckworth (b. about 1813/18 in Lancashire – d. between 1874 and 1880, Meigs Co., Ohio)

Ahnentafel #122 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s mother’s father (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

James Duckworth was born about 1813 or 1818 in Lancashire, England, and died between 1874 and 1880 in Meigs County, Ohio. He worked as a collier — a coal miner — and married Ann Collins on 21 January 1839 at St Mary's Church in Bury, Lancashire, signing the register with a mark, as did his bride. He emigrated to the United States around 1850 or 1851, settling first in Pennsylvania and then in Meigs County, Ohio, where in 1874 a court inquest found him insane and committed him to the Lunatic Asylum of the State of Ohio at Athens.

Evidence

A COLLIER. “James Duckworth, of full age, Bachelor, Collier, of Wood Head Hill” — m. Ann Collins 21 January 1839 at St Mary, Bury, Lancashire, after banns, by J. F. Amos, curate; both signed with an ✗ mark; his father entered as GEORGE DUCKWORTH, HATTER [ROUSH-34]. Emigrated c. June 1850 × 1851; Pennsylvania, then Meigs County, Ohio. ⚠⚠ Meigs County Probate Journal p.288, inquest of 24 April 1874: “In the Matter of the Insanity of James Duckworth.” Found insane, of less than two years’ duration, legal settlement Sutton Township, entitled to the Lunatic Asylum of the State of Ohio at ATHENS; witnesses Wm Duckworth, Henry and August Bartels, David Thomas; medical witness Dr Isaac Irwin; Judge Stanbery [ROUSH-38].

Outstanding: ⭐ The Athens asylum admission and death registers, 1874–1880, would give his death and burial — and would close the story. There is no estate because a man sent to a state asylum leaves no will.

Ann Collins (b. after 21 Jan 1818 in Lancashire – living after 1860)

Ahnentafel #123 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s mother’s mother (6 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Ann Collins was born after 21 January 1818 in Lancashire, England, and was still living after 1860. She was a minor of Freetown, in Bury, when she married James Duckworth in 1839, which is why her father's name was recorded in the marriage register at all; she, too, signed with a mark. Her maiden name was recovered from the 1924 Ohio death certificate of her eldest daughter, Mary Duckworth Treadwell, which named her mother as "Ann Collins, England."

Evidence

A MINOR at her marriage — which is why her father is named in the register at all. Of Freetown, Bury. Signed with an ✗ mark. Her maiden name was recovered on 6 Aug 2026 from the 1924 Ohio death certificate of her eldest daughter Mary (Duckworth) Treadwell, which reads “MOTHER: ANN COLLINS, England” [ROUSH-33, ROUSH-34].

Outstanding: Her baptism at Bury c.1818–21, father Adam Collins. ⚠ Bury is 9 miles north of Manchester — the “Manchester” of the Ohio death certificates is the region, not the parish.
