Archibald "Archy" Bell (b. about 1770–1775; d. before 22 October 1839, Sumter District, South Carolina)

Ahnentafel #128 · his father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (7 generations back) · Hypothesis — a candidate, not tied in

Archibald Bell — “Archy” to the clerks who wrote down his name — was born about 1770 to 1775, and if the reconstruction holds he was a son of William Rasor Bell, a planter of Salem in Sumter District who died leaving nine sons and a widow named Mary.

He farmed on both banks of Lynches Creek, the water that divides Sumter District from Darlington. He bought land in Darlington in 1806 and sold a hundred and twenty-seven acres there in 1817, between Bay Branch and Newmans Swamp; he was witnessing deeds across the creek in Sumter as early as 1812. His wife Martha released her dower twice, in 1809 and 1817, before a justice named John Huggins — whose family would later marry into his.

Martha died, and he married Margaret, and had five more children with names that read like a roll-call of his neighbours: Benjamin Sumter, Ezekiel Dixon, Hugh McCallum, Columbus Campbell. He was sued in 1830 for a witness fee. He was still buying land from Robert Severance in 1836, at sixty. He died in the autumn of 1839, weak in body but of sound mind, leaving two hundred and seventy-five acres, a set of blacksmith’s tools, thirteen geese, and a boy named Limus whom he directed to be sold to pay his debts.

Whether the John Bell he named first among his older children is the John Bell who went to Alabama about 1830 is the question this family has not yet answered.

Evidence

⭐⭐ A candidate with a documented life and no document tying him to us. He is graded hypothesis deliberately — but for a narrower reason than this node used to give. ⭐ Archy Bell’s own will names a son JOHN BELL: “the remainder I will & bequeath to my beloved children to be equally divided between them, viz, John Bell, Celey Amoson, Harriett Bell, Martha Bell, William Wesley Bell, and Jane Bell” — verified in the signed original, the clerk’s certified copy and the recorded will book [BELL-430]. ⚠ What is NOT documented is that this son John is OUR John F. Bell of Barbour County, Alabama. The open question is an identification, not a relationship — and a “John Bell Senr” was witnessing deeds in Darlington in 1820, so uniqueness cannot be assumed. What follows is the case, which is now a strong one.

⭐ He left a will [BELL-147]. Made 4 October 1839 and filed at Sumter District on the 22nd, it leaves his wife Margaret the 275-acre plantation for her life or widowhood, then to five children — Benjamin Sumter, Margaret Louisa, Ezekiel Dixon, Hugh McCallum and Columbus Campbell Bell. An 1846 partition establishes that those five are the children of a second marriage. The residue he leaves to six more: ⭐ “John Bell, Celey Amoson, Harriet Bell, Martha Bell, William Wesley Bell, and Jane Bell.”

⭐ That earlier family is dated, and it is dated to the right decade. William Wesley Bell is in the 1850 Darlington census aged 30, born 1820 [BELL-160]. So the six were born roughly 1805×1820 — the window in which John Bell was born, 13 January 1807, and the window of Archibald’s marriage to Martha, who released dower in 1809 and 1817.

⭐⭐ And his household held a boy of exactly the right age, twice [BELL-159]. The 1820 Sumter schedule (page 115, indexed “Arel Bell”) tallies one male aged 10 and under 16 — John was thirteen. The 1830 schedule (page 66, indexed “Achable Bell”) tallies one male aged 20 and under 30 — John was twenty-three, and his family left for Alabama “about 1830.” Archibald himself is 45-and-upwards in 1820 and 50–60 in 1830. ⚠ But the bracket is not exclusive: of the seven Bell households on the 1830 Sumter schedule, the elder John Bell’s (p. 93) also holds an anonymous male 20–30 [BELL-166] — the census cannot choose between the two roofs.

⭐ The neighbours run for twenty-six years. Robert Severance is the household directly below him in 1820, sells him land in 1836, and his estate bounds Archibald’s farm in 1846. William Copeland is on the 1830 page and witnesses the will. The Amosons are on both pages, and a daughter married one of them.

Outstanding — and these are the reasons the grade has not moved [BELL-148, BELL-162]: ⛔ No instrument names John Bell as his son, and none places the will’s John Bell in Alabama. The estate packet was opened in August 2026 and contains no legatee receipts at all. ⛔ Two other Sumter Bell households in 1820 — Thomas Bell, on the line immediately above him, and John Bell — also held a boy of John’s age, and if those men are his brothers then the rivals are his nephews, sharing every neighbour and every migration route. ⛔ And the rival persists: in 1830 the elder John Bell’s household (the probable “John Bell Senr” of the 1839 bond) still holds a male 20–30, while Thomas Bell’s household has vanished from the schedule entirely, leaving his boy unlocated [BELL-166]. ⚠ The Darlington-vs-Sumter question has narrowed sharply: beyond the shared “Archy”/“Archibald” usage and the three surnames that cross the creek with him (Huggins, Chaney, Newman), the February 1839 resurvey of his own home plantation — the Sumter estate tract itself — is headed “South Carolina, DARLINGTON DISTRICT” by the deputy surveyor [BELL-170]. His home paperwork crossed the line; a single instrument equating the two men in words is still wanting. ⚠ The 1820 and 1830 tallies also leak by one boy, which is unexplained. ⭐ And the exclusion now runs one way [BELL-172]: no John Bell of the will’s generation is documentable in Sumter or Darlington District in 1840 or 1850 — the one right-age Sumter candidate resolves to James P. Bell by his own will — so the will’s John left the home districts, and ours is the only located right-age John with any documented tie to them.

⭐ 22 Aug 2026 [BELL-484]: the Darlington Archibald (wife Martha, 1809–1820) and the Sumter Archy (wife Margaret by 7 Oct 1829) are now one man stated in the record — “Archd Bell of the district of Sumpter” buying the Darlington tract on 4 May 1810, and “Archey Bell … Margaret Bell the wife of the within named Archibald Bell” on one 1829 deed. His Boykin land came “sold to said A. Bell by General Cantey” — a purchase, not a descent from William Rasor Bell. He witnessed for Jonathan Newman of Sumter District in 1805 and beside a John Bell Senr in 1820 — association, never kinship. Still no document ties him to W.R.B.’s son Archibald; the estate bundle (Sumter Box 10 Pkg 3) was opened the same week — nine leaves, no division, no receipts [BELL-485].

⭐ [BELL-485]: his own estate packet is found (Sumter Bundle 113 Pkg 15): the original signed will of 4 Oct 1839 names wife Margaret, five younger children (Benjamin Sumter, Margaret Louisa, Ezekiel Dixon [#238 — not “Nixon”], Hugh McCallum, Columbus Campbell) and six older (John, Celey Amoson, Harriett, Martha, William Wesley, Jane) — and no father or brother; the sale bill shows the family buying its household back; J. W. Stuckey was guardian ad litem for the three youngest in 1846–47. William Rasor Bell’s own bundle is nine leaves with no division or receipts, the Ordinary’s books of 1809–39 were never filmed, and Sumter equity 1795–1870 holds no Bell partition — so the document that would tie 128 to 256 is not online anywhere now searched.

⭐⚠ 24 Aug 2026 [BELL-502–503, #229]: Archy is now documented holding and mortgaging (1815, to Zachariah Cantey) the 345-acre Lynches Creek tract whose 1785 survey was laid out for William Rasor Bell — the first document putting him on node 256’s ground — but a seventh adversary pass [BELL-512] kept the link at hypothesis: W.R.B. never owned the tract (a locative recital, not a title — and the recital’s companion word is now settled as HARRISON at the ink, an inherited 1780s adjoiner call, not a plat call [BELL-517]), Archy was a serial open-market buyer, and land never conveyed keeps its dead owner’s name for decades.

⛔ 25 Aug 2026 — the online case is CLOSED at the structure, not the query [BELL-514, BELL-517, BELL-518, BELL-521, BELL-522]: the Kershaw conveyance indexes 1791–1824 hold no Bell of ours in either direction (and Book A only opens in 1791, so 1786×91 is unanswerable there); no suit in five volumes of Sumter judgments 1804–26 ever touched the estate — proven in a record class where the Ordinary demonstrably sued his own administrators; the administrator Charles Williams (“of Georgetown district,” Durant in-laws, a rule-41 defaulting guardian in 1821) is dense in Sumter’s equity record 1819–27 with zero Bell connection; and the fiduciary volumes that would carry the one settling line — a distributee entry naming Archibald — were never filmed by anybody: they are SCDAH’s unfilmed Returns Books L 43049 (1816–1916) and Journals L 43051, paper in Columbia. The decisive documents are now all offline and named; the grade holds where seven adversaries left it.

Martha Bell (wife of Archibald Bell by October 1809; living February 1817, Darlington District, South Carolina)

Ahnentafel #129 · his father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (7 generations back) · Hypothesis — a candidate, not tied in

Martha Bell appears twice, briefly. In October 1809, and again in February 1817, her husband Archibald sold land in Darlington District, South Carolina, and each time she came before a justice of the peace — the same man both times, John Huggins — who examined her privately and separately from her husband, as the law required, to be sure a wife was not signing away her dower under his eye. She declared she did it freely and voluntarily, without compulsion, dread or fear of any person whomsoever. In 1809 she made her mark.

That is all we have of her, and whether she was John Bell’s mother is a hypothesis resting on her husband’s.

Evidence

⭐ “Martha Bell, the wife of the above named Archibald Bell” appeared twice before a Darlington District justice of the peace — 17 October 1809 and 24 February 1817 — and, examined privately and separately from her husband as the law required of a married woman, released her dower in land he was selling. In 1809 she made her mark. ⭐ The 1809 record, found 13 Aug 2026 [BELL-150], moves her eight years earlier than this file had her, and closes the gap between her documented marriage and John Bell’s birth in January 1807 to under three years.

⚠ She is the weaker of these two nodes and must be judged separately from her husband [BELL-148, BELL-162]. For Martha to be John Bell’s mother, three things must all hold: that the Darlington Archibald and the Sumter Archy are one man; that Martha was the first of his wives; and that the earlier family in his will is hers.

Outstanding: ⛔ No source anywhere states that Martha was the mother of anybody. ⛔ Every document naming her is a Darlington instrument, while every document of the will and the estate is Sumter — so she depends entirely on a merge that is still undocumented. ⚠ His 1839 will names a wife Margaret, and “children by his second marriage” establishes at least two marriages, not exactly two; a Martha who was wife number two of three, and stepmother rather than mother to the older children, fits every record equally well. ⚠ The 1830 Sumter schedule shows the woman of that house aged 30–40, who would have been only nine to nineteen in 1809 — so she is probably Margaret, and Martha is probably already dead. Her maiden name and her dates are unknown.

⭐ [BELL-484]: Martha living 20 Sept 1820 (dower, Sumter); Margaret is Archibald’s wife by 7 Oct 1829 (dower, Sumter) — the change falls 1820×1829, not c.1830.

Joseph Eakins (d. early 1805, New Hanover Co., NC)

Ahnentafel #144 · his father’s father’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father (7 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Joseph Eakins died in early 1805 in New Hanover County, North Carolina. In 1790 he bought 400 acres on Moores Creek, described in the deed as a "Gentleman," sold that land in 1793, and in 1796 received a 210-acre North Carolina state land grant signed by Governor Samuel Ashe. After his death, his widow's year's provision was allotted in March 1805 and his goods were sold at auction that April for $1,657.57. The Eakins family cemetery still stands on land he once owned, with burials dating from 1797 to the present day.

Evidence

Proven by his own estate file. In 1790 he bought 400 acres on MOORES CREEK as “Gentleman”; sold to Jacob Moore 1793; and in 1796 received a 210-acre North Carolina state land grant signed by Governor Samuel Ashe. His widow’s year’s provision was allotted March 1805 and his goods sold at auction that April for $1,657.57. The Eakins family cemetery still stands on that land, its oldest burial 1797 and its most recent 2019.

Outstanding: He reached New Hanover about 1788–90 from somewhere else — and that somewhere, not New Hanover, is where Gen 9 will be found. The tree’s Irish origin (Joseph Elijah Eakins & Isabella Wauchope) was tested and fails outright.

Ahnentafel #145 · his father’s father’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s mother (7 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Her father is believed to have been William "Lost Billie" Pigford, whose 1801 will names a daughter, "my daughter Elizabeth Aiken."

Evidence

Four Pigfords administer the estate and raise the children, and a Pigford sells the family the adjoining land in 1834 — strong, but the marriage itself is not documented.

Moore Stephens (b. c.1796 North Carolina; m. 3 Sept 1817 Orange Co., NC; of Sumter Co., Alabama 1850)

Ahnentafel #160 · his father’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (7 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Moore Stephens was born about 1796 in North Carolina and was still living in Sumter County, Alabama, in 1850, later moving on to Newton County, Mississippi. He married Rebecca Moize on 3 September 1817 in Orange County, North Carolina, and the two were still together, and still recorded as North Carolina-born, in both the 1850 and 1860 census. He was one of ten heirs at law of Mary Thorn's 1846 Greene County, Alabama estate, alongside nine named siblings — William, Lewis, Charles, and James Stephens, and sisters Nancy Barnett, Mary Barnett, Lucinda Ball, Elizabeth Beavers, and Esther Hitt.

After his sister Esther's death, he and Rebecca raised her two orphaned children alongside their own, naming a daughter Easter in her memory. The 1850 slave schedule for Sumter County records that Moore held one enslaved person, an unnamed Black girl about seven years old.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ FOUND IN THE 1850 CENSUS, 6 Aug 2026: Moore Stephens, 54, born NORTH CAROLINA — Sumter county, Alabama, household 1220, with REBECCA, 50, born North Carolina (alive at 50 — his 1817 bride), Benjamin W. 21, James M. 18, John F. 16, Mary A. R. 13, Charles L. 12, Easter C. 10, William K. 6, and Rebecca J. and Richard HITT, both 10 — his dead sister Esther Hitt’s orphans, the “Representatives of Esther Hitt” of the 1846 distribution, being raised in his house. He named a daughter Easter (Esther) for that sister [STEPHENS-15]. ⭐⭐ Sumter County explains the cost bill’s “Heirs of SUMTER CO., ALA.” — and explains why he alone of the brothers bought nothing at his mother’s sale: he did not live there. ⭐⭐ His marriage bond found 6 Aug 2026: Orange County, North Carolina, Marriage Bonds index p.379 — “Stephens, Moore ✗ | Rebecca MOIZE | 3 Sept. 1817 | bondsman CHARLES ✗ STEPHENS, witness Joseph A. Woods”. Both men signed with a mark [STEPHENS-05]. ⭐⭐⭐ And his whole family found the same night: Greene County, Alabama, Orphans’ Court, 12 October 1846 — he is one of TEN heirs at law of MARY THORN, deceased, taking a distributive share of $107.50, receipted at $95 [STEPHENS-06]. His nine siblings are named: William, Lewis, Charles and James Stephens; Nancy Barnett, Mary Barnett, Lucinda Ball, Elizabeth Beavers and Esther Hitt. ⭐ Charles Stephens is his BROTHER, not his father — co-heirs in equal shares are siblings, and the “Gen 9 candidate” label on Charles is withdrawn.

Outstanding: ⭐ The migration is now dated: Orange Co., N.C. (Sept 1817) → Greene Co., Alabama (Aug 1824), window 1819×1824 — and corroborated 6 Aug 2026: his brother James is recorded in the 1850 census as born NORTH CAROLINA, c.1801 [STEPHENS-14]. ⚠⚠⚠ The estate he inherited from was two-thirds a human being. Mary Thorn’s sale of 29 Apr 1844 realised $1,075.86, of which $700 was the price of the woman JANE, bought by his brother James [STEPHENS-13]. Moore’s share was $107.39; about $70 of it was Jane. ⛔ Moore himself does not appear in the 1850 slave schedule index — held open, since he has not been found in the 1850 population census either. ⚠⚠⚠ VERIFIED FROM THE IMAGE, same night [STEPHENS-17]: Sumter Co. 1850 slave schedule, right-hand half, line 31 — “MOORE STEPHENS” in a clear hand (the index’s “Moon” is a transcription error). He held ONE enslaved person: a Black FEMALE, age indexed 7 (the age cell alone is faint). Born c.1843; about twenty-two at emancipation; not named, because the schedule was designed not to name her. ⭐ She is the likeliest person in this family to be findable BY NAME in the 1870 census of Sumter County. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ 1860 FOUND [STEPHENS-18]: MOORE STEVENS, 66, born North Carolina — NEWTON COUNTY, MISSISSIPPI, p.117, with Rebeca 55, Mary 23, Emeline 21, Luis 18, Rebeca 18, William 15. He left Sumter Co., Alabama for Mississippi in the 1850s — and node 80 already has HENRY H. STEPHENS in NEWTON CO. MS in 1860 too. Father and son moved together again (in 1850 they were six houses apart). ⭐⭐ It also frames the Mary Thorn cost bill’s “Heirs of Tishomingo County, Mississippi” — the westward pull on this family is now documented. ⚠ An FS tree calls him “1796–1860” — unsourced, NOT entered, but worth testing against a Newton Co. probate or the 1860 mortality schedule, which would give him a death date for the first time. ⚠ Two children (Luis 18, Rebeca 18, b. c.1842) are not in the 1850 household — unresolved; this family has taken in others’ children before.

Rebecca Moize (b. c.1800 North Carolina; m. 3 Sept 1817 Orange Co., NC; living Sumter Co., Ala. 1850)

Ahnentafel #161 · his father’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (7 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Rebecca Moize was born about 1800 to 1805 in North Carolina. She married Moore Stephens on 3 September 1817 in Orange County, North Carolina, at about seventeen years old, and was still recorded beside him, and still North Carolina-born, in both the 1850 census in Sumter County, Alabama, and the 1860 census in Newton County, Mississippi — forty-three years and two states after her wedding. The Moize family remained settled in Orange County for generations afterward, and her own parents have not yet been identified.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ ALIVE AND FOUND, 6 Aug 2026: “Rebecca Stephens, 50, born North Carolina” in Moore Stephens’s household, Sumter county, Alabama, 1850 [STEPHENS-15]. Married at about seventeen and still his wife thirty-three years later, a state away. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND FOUND IN 1860 [STEPHENS-18]: “Rebeca Stevens, 55, born North Carolina,” Newton County, Mississippi, still beside Moore. Married in Orange Co., N.C. at about seventeen and still his wife forty-three years later, two states away. Her death is now after 1860, not merely after 1850. ⚠ The two censuses give her birth as c.1800 and c.1805 — census ages drift; recorded as b. c.1800–1805. Bride on the 1817 Orange County marriage bond [STEPHENS-05]. ⚠ The bond does NOT name her father — the bondsman carried the groom’s surname, not hers. ⭐ MOIZE is a settled Orange County surname: the family is still in Lebanon Township, Orange County in 1880, and Moize deaths registered at Durham into the 1950s still give “Orange County, N.C.” as birthplace.

Outstanding: Her parents are unnamed. Look for a Moize groom of the 1780s–1810s in the same Orange County bond index — he would be her father or brother. ⭐ Also: the Greene Co. cost bill shows “Chas. Stephens & wife” and three other Stephens wives relinquishing dower — if Moore is in the unread part of that list, the deeds name Rebecca.

Daniel Jones (b. ~1806 North Carolina; d. by Dec 1869, Sumter Co., Alabama)

Ahnentafel #162 · his father’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father (7 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Daniel Jones was born about 1806 in North Carolina and died by December 1869 in Sumter County, Alabama. He is believed to have been the father of Sarah Ann Jones, based on the bonding of her sons Caswell, Calvin, and John as his "minor heirs" in December 1869 — an inheritance only possible through a lineal, and by then deceased, child. He lived two doors from the Stephens family in the 1850 census and repeatedly stood as co-surety with Moore Stephens in the 1850s, reflecting a close and long-standing connection between the two families.

Evidence

PROBABLE, adversary-confirmed 10 Aug 2026 [STEPHENS-33/-34/-35/-VERDICT]. Father of Sarah Ann Jones (81). The hard link: her sons Caswell, Calvin, John bonded 31 Dec 1869 as his “minor heirs” — per-stirpes inheritance possible only through a lineal predeceased link, their dead mother. Converging web: 1850 household 1216 (Daniel 44 NC · Susan 35 NC · Elizabeth 13 · James 12 · Margaret 10 · Mary 6) TWO DOORS from Henry’s 1214 [STEPHENS-35a]; repeat co-surety with Moore Stephens (160) 1853–54; adult surety by 1839; the lone adult Daniel Jones in 1850 Sumter; the NC POA sibling set (Daniel, Elijah, David, Sarah; brother Joel d. ~1843 Sumter). Sororate: daughter Elizabeth = Henry’s 1854 second wife (inference — age+name+two doors).

Outstanding: the decider — his own 1869 administration/distribution (Probate Records 1868–74 / Estate Case Files; “daughter Sarah, deceased” recital upgrades, different heirs falsify); Henry’s 1860 household (never pulled — Caswell’s maternity primary-check); the Elizabeth-identity document; post-1850/adjacent-county same-name residue.

Susan Jones, whose maiden name is not known, was born about 1815 in North Carolina and was living in 1850 as the wife of Daniel Jones. Whether she was the mother of Sarah Ann Jones, who was born around 1833 when Susan would have been only about eighteen, is not established, and Daniel may have had an earlier wife instead. She remains a candidate rather than a confirmed ancestor.

Syntha / Cyntha Ward (b. about 1813 in South Carolina – d. between 1880 and 1900)

Ahnentafel #179 · his father’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s mother’s mother (7 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Syntha, also recorded as Cyntha, Ward was born about 1813 in South Carolina and died sometime between 1880 and 1900. As William H. Ward's widow, her dower share was allotted on 18 September 1869 in Henry County, Alabama, and she was later described in a sworn deposition as "Mrs Cyntha Ward the widow of deceased." By 1880 she was living, widowed, in the household of her son Isaiah, and that census recorded both of her own parents as having been born in North Carolina.

Her maiden name, despite appearing in several records connected to her husband's estate, has never been given, and remains unknown.

Evidence

Widow of William H. Ward. ⭐ Her dower was allotted 18 September 1869, Henry County — notice by publication in the HENRY COUNTY REGISTER, J. H. Simonton guardian ad litem for the minors [TRUETT-24]. Named as “Mrs Cyntha Ward the widow of deceased” in Abijah Wiggins’s deposition sworn 11 Nov 1870 at Abbeville [TRUETT-22]. In 1880 she is 66, widowed, keeping house in her son Isaiah’s household; that schedule gives both her parents as NORTH CAROLINA-born — Generation 9, placed but unnamed.

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ HER MAIDEN NAME IS THE PROJECT’S FOUR-TIME NEGATIVE — the dower proceeding, the 1870 petition, the Wiggins deposition and the estate file all decline to give it. PARKED. It will come from a Bible, an obituary, or a Ward estate in South Carolina.

Colonel Gershom Tuttle III (11 Jun 1769 – 5 Sep 1823)

Ahnentafel #192 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (7 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Colonel Gershom Tuttle III was born on 11 June 1769 and died on 5 September 1823. According to the 1883 Tuttle genealogy, he moved from Bristol, Connecticut, to Paris in Oneida County, New York, about 1790, and then around 1800 to Jefferson County, New York, where he opened a 275-acre farm near what became Watertown. He was one of the town's first highway commissioners, helped build the first grist mill in the town of Rutland in 1806, and opened a tavern on the main highway to the lake country that became famous during the War of 1812, in which he served as Lieutenant Colonel of the 76th New York Militia, a regiment that bore his name.

He married Pamelia Strong Clark on 8 July 1788 in Farmington, Connecticut, and the family later removed to Vigo County, Indiana, in 1816, where he served as county commissioner from 1820. He was described by his contemporaries as a man of excellent character and genial temper, fond of a joke and a capital storyteller, and he is buried at Otter Creek Union Cemetery, North Terre Haute, in Vigo County, Indiana.

Evidence

⭐ RENAMED 6 Aug 2026 at Alec’s correction: he was on this chart as “Gershom Tuttle Jr.”, which was wrong once the line ran deeper. There are THREE Gershoms in a row and “Jr.” belongs to none of them: I = Ensign Gershom, b. 1714, d. 1777 (node 768); II = Gershom the patriot, b. 1738, d. 1818 (node 384); III = this man, the Colonel of the War of 1812, b. 1769, d. 1823. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ A LIFE, FROM THE 1883 TUTTLE GENEALOGY p.511 — entry no. 92514 [TUTTLE-29]: “rem. from Bristol, Conn., to Paris, Oneida Co., N.Y., abt. 1790; thence abt. 1800 to Jefferson Co., N.Y., and opened a farm of 275 acres abt. five miles from the site of Watertown, in the town of Rutland.” One of the first commissioners of highways; in 1806, with Seth Bailey, he BUILT THE FIRST GRIST MILL IN THE TOWN; 1808–1816 commissioner for loaning moneys of the state; 1810 a subscriber to the Black River Navigation Co. He traded, made potash, and opened a TAVERN on the great highway to the lake country — “the tavern became famous” in the War of 1812. Described as “a man of excellent character, genial temper, very fond of a joke, a capital story teller.” ⭐⭐⭐ THE MILL DESCENDS: his son Clark Strong Tuttle (node 96) was a MILLER at Otter Creek, Indiana — father builds Rutland’s first grist mill 1806, son is milling in Indiana forty years on. ⭐⭐ And it independently confirms the ONEIDA COUNTY stage that TUTTLE-15 had only from stray deed hits. m. Pamelia Strong Clark 8 Jul 1788 at Farmington, Connecticut; removed the family to Vigo County, Indiana in 1816; county commissioner from 1820; Lieutenant Colonel of the 76th New York Militia in the War of 1812 — the regiment bore his name. ⭐ Buried OTTER CREEK UNION CEMETERY, North Terre Haute, Vigo County (Find a Grave 123311856) — ⚠ no grave photograph exists. ⚠ His wife Pamelia is at Southview Cemetery, Brown County, about ninety miles away: two separate visits.

Outstanding: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE ROUTE PAST GERSHOM TUTTLE SR. IS NOW KNOWN [TUTTLE-29]. The 1883 genealogy numbers by descent — each digit is a child’s position. This man is 92514; so 9251 = the patriot (node 384), 925 = GERSHOM TUTTLE SR. (node 768, the wall), 92 = his father (a generation we do not have), 9 = his father, a child of William Tuttle the immigrant of 1635. ✅✅ CONFIRMED THE SAME NIGHT [TUTTLE-30] — ALL FOUR RUNGS EXIST AND ALL FOUR ARE THE RIGHT PEOPLE: 9251 = Gershom b. 1738, d. 5 Jan 1818 (image 616); 925 = Gershom Sr., b. 11 Aug 1714, Cheshire → Bristol (image 615); 92 = Dea. Timothy Tuttle of Cheshire (image 574); 9 = Simon Tuttle, bap. New Haven 28 Mar 1647 (image 560), the ninth child of William Tuttle the immigrant. The entry number really was a pedigree. ⭐ Page offset known from the SAR screenshot: printed p.511 = archive.org image 615 of 838; the earlier entries lie BEFORE it. His 29 Aug 1823 Vigo Co. will is still to be obtained. ⭐ Otter Creek Township is now independently documented as the family seat — his son Clark was milling there in 1850 and 1860.

Pamelia Strong Clark (b. 4 Nov 1770 Farmington, Conn. – 15 Apr 1851 Nashville, Ind.)

Ahnentafel #193 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (7 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Pamelia Strong Clark was born on 4 November 1770 in Farmington, Connecticut, and died on 15 April 1851, by one account, or 15 November 1851 by another — sources disagree on the exact date — near Nashville, Indiana. She married Gershom Tuttle on 8 July 1788 in Farmington; according to the 1883 Tuttle genealogy, her brother was a United States Army officer who died while commanding the U.S. arsenal at Troy, New York, and her nephew was General Newman Strong Clark of the Department of the Pacific.

She outlived her husband by twenty-eight years, following one of their children south, and is buried at Southview Cemetery in Nashville, Brown County, Indiana, ninety miles from where he lies. Her middle name, Strong, was carried on through her descendants for four generations, though a search of the standard genealogy of the Strong family has so far failed to confirm any direct Strong ancestry for her.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HER MAIDEN NAME IS CLARK. “STRONG” IS A MIDDLE NAME [TUTTLE-46, 7 August 2026]. G. F. Tuttle 1883, p. 512, in Col. Gershom’s own sketch: “He m. in Farmington, Conn., July 8, 1788, Pamelia Strong Clark of F., b. Nov. 4, 1770. Her brother was an officer of the U.S.A., and d. while in charge of the U.S. arsenal in Troy, N.Y. Gen. Newman Strong Clark of the Dept. of the Pacific, was her nephew. She d. at Nashville, Ind., April 15, 1851, a. 81.”

⛔⛔ THE STRONG DOOR IS SHUT. Dwight’s Descendants of Elder John Strong (1871) was searched in both volumes — no Pamelia Clark born 1770, no Tuttle marriage, no “Clark of Farmington.” The Elder John Strong descent that this chart has quietly assumed is not established and is now actively unsupported by the standard Strong genealogy. Her mother may still have been a Strong — that is a guess, not a finding.

⚠ Her death date is in conflict: Tuttle 1883 says 15 April 1851; Find a Grave memorial 16018171 says 15 November 1851. She is buried at Southview Cemetery, Nashville, Brown County, Indiana — ninety miles from her husband’s grave, because she outlived him by twenty-eight years and followed a child south.

Buried Southview Cemetery, Nashville, Brown Co., Indiana, with a headstone photograph. Her name is the “Strong Clark” carried down through Clark Strong Tuttle to Dennis Clark and Richard Clark — four generations of it.

Joseph Lamb (c.1782 North Carolina – 1855, Wayne Co., IN (æ73))

Ahnentafel #216 · his mother’s father’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father (7 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Joseph Lamb was born about 1782 in North Carolina and died in 1855 in Wayne County, Indiana, at the age of 73. Two independent nineteenth-century local histories, written sixteen years apart, agree that he settled on land near Perry, Indiana, in 1829, and name his children, including Ezekiel, Esther, Smith, Elias, Mournen, Joseph, and Ithamar. According to one of those accounts, his own parents were Joseph and Lydia Adamson Lamb, and the family were members of the Society of Friends before leaving North Carolina; the particular county of North Carolina they came from is not recorded by either source.

Evidence

⭐⭐ ADDED at DOCUMENTED, 10 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [KNOTT-13/-VERDICT]: two genuinely independent histories 16 years apart — Young 1872 (“Joseph Lamb, from North Carolina, settled, in 1829, on land adjoining Perry, where he died in 1855, at the age of 73. His children were, Ezekiel, deceased; Esther, Smith, deceased; Elias, Mournen, Joseph, Ithamar”) and the 1888 Elias-informed sketch (“his parents, Joseph and Lydia (Adamson) Lamb, left North Carolina… members of the Society of Friends”) — different informants, different details (Young names the deceased children), converging. County of NC origin NOT stated by either source [self-correction #25].

Outstanding: the 1855 estate file (heirs list; NC county); a Quaker certificate of removal.

10 Aug 2026 [KNOTT-14]: candidate parents lead-grade: Henry (M.) Lamb × Ann Dennis; b. 25 Mar 1782; the 1806 “Lidya Addison” bond (bondsman her brother Samuel) and the 1829 Center MM certificate — all one compiled source pending the minute books.

John Roush (22 Nov 1785 – 21 Jan 1861, Meigs Co., Ohio)

Ahnentafel #240 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father (7 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

John Roush was born on 22 November 1785 and died on 21 January 1861 in Meigs County, Ohio. He was the son of George Roush and Catharine Zerkel, and on 18 May 1808 he married his own cousin, Regina Roush, with Justice of the Peace Francis Watkins presiding. The couple settled in the Nease Settlement of Sutton Township, Meigs County, Ohio, moving there from Graham Station, Mason County, Virginia, sometime between the births of their children Benjamin, born in Virginia in August 1816, and Hannah, born in the Nease Settlement in May 1820.

They are buried in a family plot beside Regina's father, Jonas, though their graves are unmarked. Their son Benjamin's place in the family is recorded in a 1928 genealogy, though this last link has not yet been confirmed by an original record.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 7 August 2026 [ROUSH-54]. This is the rung that opens two Revolutionary patriots.

Son of George Roush and Catharine Zerkel. Married 18 May 1808 — “Francis Watkins, Justice of the Peace, pronounced the words that made them one” — his own cousin Regina Roush. A baptismal register printed in the 1928 genealogy records “John, Nov. 22, 1785. Bap. Wit. John Roush, wife Susannah, Jan. 5, 1786.”

He and Regina settled in the Nease Settlement, Sutton Township, Meigs County, Ohio — ⭐ the same few square miles where their great-great-grandson John G. Roush was born in 1877, and where the Roush and Nease families that later intermarried were founding neighbours. They lie in a family plot beside Regina’s father Jonas, with no stone marking their graves.

The move is dated by their children. Benjamin was born at Graham Station, Mason County, Virginia in August 1816; his sister Hannah was born in the Nease Settlement in May 1820. So John and Regina crossed from Virginia into Ohio between those two dates — which is exactly why Benjamin’s birthplace is Virginian and his siblings’ are not.

⚠⚠ GRADED PROBABLE ON PURPOSE. Everything above this couple is DAR-verified; everything below Benjamin is ours from records. The one link still resting on a single source is Benjamin as their son — the 1928 genealogy, p. 464: “5. BENJAMIN ROUSH, fifth child of John and Regina Roush, was born August 30, 1816.”

Outstanding — and this is the single most valuable document left on the Roush line: the Meigs County, Ohio probate death record for Benjamin Roush, 1897, or John Roush’s 1861 Meigs County estate file naming son Benjamin. Either one converts this node to Documented and the whole patriot descent with it. ⛔ Benjamin is not in the West Virginia death index — searched Mason Co. 1897 and Benjamin Roush statewide, all years. He died in Ohio.

Regina Roush (30 Nov 1788 – death not established)

Ahnentafel #241 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s mother (7 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Regina Roush was born on 30 November 1788, the daughter of the Revolutionary War patriot Jonas Roush and his wife Barbara Fox. She married her cousin John Roush on 18 May 1808. In her later years her father lived in her household in the Nease Settlement, a detail recorded independently both in a 1928 family genealogy and in Jonas's own Revolutionary War pension file. Her date of death was not known even to the compilers of that genealogy and remains unrecorded today.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 7 August 2026 [ROUSH-54]. Daughter of the patriot Jonas Roush and Barbara Fox; married her cousin John Roush on 18 May 1808. A Roush married a Roush — both of them grandchildren of the same Shenandoah Valley family, which is a pedigree collapse waiting to be drawn once the generation above is proved.

⭐⭐ The DAR has approved this exact person twice — on Jonas Roush’s ancestor record the approved lineages include “child REGINA — spouse JOHN ROUSH” (National Nos. 569934 and 1076887).

⭐ Her father spent his last years in her house. The 1928 genealogy says Jonas Roush “spent his last years in her home in the Nease Settlement” — and Jonas’s own Revolutionary pension file, independently, records that he removed to Meigs County, Ohio to live with a daughter. Two sources that never met, agreeing.

Outstanding: her death date. The 1928 book says plainly that it “has not been ascertained,” and the grave is unmarked.

David Altman (b. about 1788 in Pennsylvania – d. between 1850 and 1870)

Ahnentafel #242 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s mother’s father (7 generations back) · Hypothesis — a candidate, not tied in

David Altman is believed to have been born about 1788 in Pennsylvania and to have died sometime between 1850 and 1870. He farmed in Sutton Township, Meigs County, Ohio — the same township where the Roush family lived — and is recorded there in both the 1840 and 1850 census with a household that includes children named Jacob, Solomon, Mary, and Samuel. He is a leading candidate to be the father of Sophia Altman, whose birth year fits neatly into a gap between two of his other children, but no document has yet been found that states this connection directly.

Evidence

LEADING CANDIDATE, NOT ESTABLISHED. William Rasor Bell (planter of Claremont County by 1784, later of Salem, Sumter District; will 26 Dec 1803; d. about 1808–09)

Ahnentafel #256 · his father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (8 generations back) · Hypothesis — a candidate, not tied in

He appears in 1784 in Claremont County, already styled “Planter,” buying a neighbour’s entire personalty — an enslaved woman named Ann, the horses, the beds, nineteen cattle — and three years later giving every bit of it back to that neighbour’s children “for love, good will and affection,” which in the language of that century usually means family. He farmed the Black River country beside Frederick Bell and the Newmans, proved deeds across the line in Darlington, and on the day after Christmas 1803, “very sick & weak in body,” divided a plain farmer’s world — a plantation, stock, feather beds, a cow already called his daughter Elizabeth’s — among a wife, two daughters and nine sons, the second of whom was Archibald.

Evidence

⭐ The one link on this branch that is ASSERTED in a document: his will of 26 December 1803 — “I give & bequeath unto my beloved sons, John Bell, Archibald Bell, Thomas Bell, William Bell, James Bell, Drury Bell, Jesse Bell, Benjamin Bell, & Robert Bell…” — recorded 15 March 1809, executor Moses Gordon, witnesses Lewis Bell, Mary Stuckey and a Douglas [BELL-152, BELL-181]. Wife Mary took a life estate; the land was to be sold at her death or remarriage and divided among the nine sons — that sale, not yet found, would name the whole sibling generation in one instrument.

⭐ His documented life: buying Micajah Stokes’s whole personalty in 1784; settling it on Stokes’s children “for love, good will and affection” in 1787 (the language of kinship); land co-surveyed with Frederick Bell — a documented Revolutionary claimant — on the North Branch of Black River in 1787; co-signing a Darlington-proved deed with Frederick, Mary and John Bell in 1794 [BELL-182].

Outstanding: ⛔ This node is on the chart at Alec’s direction and stands or falls with node 128 — no document yet ties OUR John Bell to Archibald, so everything above Archibald is conditional on that link. ⛔ His own parents are unidentified; the leading frame is the Raccoon Swamp Bell clan of Albemarle Parish, Surry/Sussex Co., Virginia — narrowed hard on 14 Aug 2026: Rev. James Bell’s will (Sussex WB C p.310, proved 15 Oct 1778) was read off the will book and names six children — no William, no Frederick, no Lewis — ELIMINATING the patriarch’s heir-branch [BELL-193]. Hannah Bell’s will (WB B p.153, proved 17 Mar 1768) reveals the two places a father can still hide: an unnamed SON WHO DIED before 1765 (father of grandsons Silvanus & Zadock Bell), and an ABSENT son Benjamin — “provided he comes himself in person” — gone from Virginia by 1765 [BELL-194]. The patriarch’s own 1746 will, read the same day [BELL-196], names FIVE sons — John Jr., Burrell, Benjamin, Balaam, James — no William in the clan’s senior generation; if WRB belongs here it is as an unnamed grandson via BURRELL or BALAAM — the adversary-verified register work [BELL-197, BELL-199] closed John Jr.’s issue at two sons (Sylvanus & John, by PHOEBE STOKES — Micajah’s sister), and found the second dead son: Balaam Bell d. 1755, widow Mary, posthumous daughter Phoebe — his 1745×55 window fits WRB’s birth. Silvanus STOKES Junr witnessed the 1746 will — the Stokes intimacy begins in Virginia, a generation before WRB’s kinship-language settlement on Micajah Stokes’s children. Otherwise the descent runs outside the clan, e.g. Richard Bell of Lawne's Creek, d. 1734, whose sons include a William [BELL-183–185, BELL-191, BELL-196]. ⛔ The “Rasor” name — likely his mother’s or grandmother’s family — has no located source; no Rasor appears in the parish register. ⭐ His own Patriot case is ONE PRINTED INDENT — Book G No. 30, “Issued the 22d. April 1784 To Mr. William Bell for Eleven pounds Eight Shillings and Seven pence Sterling for two Horses impressed for Militia Use in October 1780.” It is the only William Bell indent in South Carolina unattributed to any man, and no DAR or SAR lineage claims it. ⚠ Nothing yet ties its claimant to him rather than to one of at least six other South Carolina William Bells, and the Chester “W:m Bell Sen:r” is not excluded. Frederick’s is now PROBABLE: three indents incl. 50 days at the Siege of Charleston in Capt. John Nelson’s company — a company three pensioners place in the Black River / Salem country of old Camden District [BELL-192, BELL-195].

⭐⭐ Where the case stood after the sweep of 19–20 August 2026 [BELL-382–406]. Book G No. 30 survived three separate attempts to take it off the board in four days and remains unseated and unexcluded. What strengthened: the printed text was transcribed from the surviving counterfoil itself — Wates’s asterisk/dagger marks are a survival map, Book G’s stubs go missing only from No. 251, and No. 30 is unmarked, 220 entries inside the intact run [BELL-385]. The evidence type is pre-approved with an exact precedent: DAR ancestor A008802 carries 46 approved lineages on a single stub-entry citation [BELL-392]. The rival Abbeville claimant is now excluded on his own itemised schedule, and the Chester man’s claims fell from three to two — one of them proved to be William Ball — so £11.8.7 no longer sits between his sums but above both [BELL-395]. What was closed off: no second October-1780 impressment claim exists anywhere in the twelve printed volumes [BELL-384, BELL-391]; his neighbours left six indents between them in 12,242, so no cohort argument can be built [BELL-394]; and the numbered Treasury returns were alphabetical work-batches, never geographic [BELL-396]. Book G No. 30 is cited by nobody in either lineage society, checked against the full source citation of every South Carolina Bell in both [BELL-392].

⭐ And his own paper trail was pushed back and sharpened. There is no South Carolina record of him before 1785, tested across seven name-forms — the apparent 1777/1778 hits are index frames carrying the 1785 judgment roll [BELL-400, BELL-406]. He has two 1785 surveys, not one: 150 acres on Bane Branch, 30 April, and 400 acres on Lynches Creek, 15 June — and both grants issued to Samuel Boykin, who paid the treasury fee, which was the local norm [BELL-400]. ⭐ His own plat survives and is cited to the page — Surveyor-General, Land Plats vol. 9Q p. 383 — and the Surveyor-General’s own index dates it 30 April 1784, a year before the grant’s recital. Volume 9Q is one of six Q-volumes never microfilmed; it is the arbiter of whether his South Carolina floor is 1784 or 1785 [BELL-406]. The Micajah Stokes transaction is four instruments over two days in April 1787, including a gift of land to a seventh child, Drury Stokes, not previously known [BELL-406]. ⚠ Origin, still hypothesis: a royal warrant of January 1773 lays out Duplin County, North Carolina land to Fredrick Bell “on the S. side of Bare Swamp joining his own & W:m Rasor Bells Line” — read at the ink, and it is a Bell, not a Ball. Frederick Bell is documented in Camden District, South Carolina on 8 May 1777 [BELL-404], so the brother’s crossing is proved; William’s is not. ⛔ The survey run on that same warrant fourteen months later names a different neighbour, so his entire North Carolina footprint is one clause in one clerk’s hand [BELL-397, BELL-402].

William Pigford Sr. (known in family tradition as “Lost Billie”; will proved March 1803, New Hanover Co., North Carolina)

Ahnentafel #290 · his father’s father’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father (8 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

William Pigford Sr., remembered in family tradition as "Lost Billie," is believed to have been a shipwrecked orphan who was bound out by court order to Benjamin Fussell in March 1740 or 1741, on the petition of Rebecca Perry. He grew into a landowner on both banks of Sill's Creek in New Hanover County, North Carolina, where he wrote his will on 27 March 1801; it was proved in March 1803. That will named nine children, including "my daughter Elizabeth Aiken." The identification rests on a later transcription of the will rather than the original document itself, so it is probable rather than fully proven.

The father of a large family of Stephens children lived in Orange County, North Carolina, by 1817, before the family moved to Greene County, Alabama, sometime between 1819 and 1824. Mary Thorn / Thorne (widow of James Thorn; d. shortly before 23 Jan 1844, Greene Co., Alabama)

Ahnentafel #321 · his father’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (8 generations back) · Hypothesis — a candidate, not tied in

Mary Thorn, also spelled Thorne, was the widow of James Thorn of Greene County, Alabama, and died shortly before 23 January 1844. She is believed to have been born a Stephens and to have been the mother, by an earlier marriage, of ten children — Moore, James, Lewis and William Stephens, and daughters Esther Hitt, Lucinda Ball and Elizabeth Bevers — who inherited her estate in equal shares. Among her possessions was an enslaved woman named Jane, sold for $700 in April 1844, easily the largest part of everything she owned. The tie between Mary and these ten Stephens children is a strong hypothesis rather than a settled fact, since no surviving document actually calls them her children.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ STEPHENS-21, folded into the chart 7 August 2026 — the strongest indication yet that she was a SECOND WIFE whose own next of kin were Stephenses.

The final settlement of James Thorn’s estate, Greene Co., Alabama Orphans’ Court Minutes, 14 October 1844 (p. 274), distributes his legacies to Jesse W. Thorn, Nazareth Thorn, James Thorn, William Henry Thorn and Elizabeth Crisp, plus the seven children of a predeceased son Kennedy Thorn. ⭐ Mary Thorn is not among the legatees — as widow she took a widow’s share, a different thing — and the Thorn sons formally released all claims against her estate, which was administered by a STEPHENS. Had she been their mother, her estate would have gone to them and no Stephens would have been appointed to it.

⚠⚠ A strong indication, not a proof. This node stays a hypothesis. ⭐ But it dates the document that should name JANE: Mary was James Thorn’s widow, so he died first, and Jane must have come to Mary out of his estate at the assignment of the widow’s share — a proceeding that itemises people by name and value. Greene County, probably 1841–1843.

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ SHE HAD A HUSBAND, AND HE IS NAMED — 7 Aug 2026 [STEPHENS-20]. Greene County Orphans’ Court Minutes 1839–1845, ark 3:1:3Q9M-C91H-HSD8-M, image 723, page 274, 14 October 1844: “This day came Jesse W Thorn and William H Thorn administrators with the will annexed of JAMES THORN deceased … and JAMES STEPHENS administrator of the Estate of MARY THORN deceased (WHO WAS THE WIDOW OF THE SAID JAMES THORN) also appearing in open Court … the said James Stephens administrator as aforesaid acknowledge satisfaction in full of THE SHARE OF THE SAID WIDOW, his said intestate, to the estate of the said James Thorn deceased, and the said Jesse W Thorn and William H Thorn … release and discharge all claim or claims which they have or hold against the Estate of the said Mary Thorn deceased.” ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND THIS BEARS ON THE VERY DOUBT WRITTEN INTO THIS NODE. The outstanding note has said for days: “NO document yet uses the word ‘children.’ A childless woman’s next of kin can be nephews and nieces.” She now turns out to be a woman who died bearing the surname THORN BY MARRIAGE, whose next of kin are all named STEPHENS. The most economical explanation is that STEPHENS was her FIRST married name and THORN her second — which would make Moore, James, Lewis, William, Esther, Lucinda and Elizabeth her CHILDREN by the first marriage, exactly as the ten equal shares and Esther Hitt’s per stirpes “Representatives” already suggested. ⚠⚠ STRENGTHENED, NOT PROVED. The alternative survives: Stephens could be her MAIDEN name and the Stephens heirs her brothers and their families. The node stays ‘hyp’ until a document says so. ⭐⭐⭐ AND THE TWO DOCUMENTS THAT WOULD SETTLE IT ARE NOW NAMED AND LOCATABLE: ① a marriage of MARY STEPHENS to JAMES THORN (Greene Co., Ala., or earlier in Orange Co., North Carolina, where Moore Stephens married in 1817); ② JAMES THORN’S WILL — the court calls his administrators “with the will annexed,” so a will exists, and a will names a wife and usually her children. ⭐ NEW NAMES: JESSE W. THORN and WILLIAM H. THORN, his administrators and very likely his sons. ⚠⚠⚠ AND IT REACHES JANE. Mary’s estate was paid the widow’s share of James Thorn’s estate. In Alabama in 1844 a widow’s share of an estate that held people WAS people. JANE — sold out of Mary’s estate on 29 April 1844 for $700 [STEPHENS-13] — may well have come to Mary out of JAMES THORN’S estate, and his WILL is the first document that could name her before the sale. ——— Earlier note: NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — and NOT yet proved. The Greene County Orphans’ Court cited Moore, James, Lewis and William Stephens, Esther Hitt, Lucinda Ball and Elizabeth Bevers as her NEXT OF KIN on 23 Jan 1844 [STEPHENS-07], and distributed her estate among TEN heirs in equal shares in 1846 [STEPHENS-06]. Three of the sisters are proved born Stephens by their own Greene County marriage records [STEPHENS-08], and Esther Hitt’s share went per stirpes to her “Representatives” — the treatment a court gives a predeceased CHILD. ⭐ She held promissory notes on Charles Stephens ($15.13, $32.40) and Lewis Stephens ($100) — she was banking her sons [STEPHENS-12]. ⭐ Her inventory of 27 Apr 1844 lists her whole world down to an 18-cent umbrella and a woman’s saddle — and “1 Negro woman JANE — 475.00,” worth more than everything else combined [STEPHENS-11].

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ NO document yet uses the word “children.” A childless woman’s next of kin can be nephews and nieces. She is drawn here as a HYPOTHESIS. The petition to sell land, or a Stephens × Thorn marriage, would settle it. ⚠⚠⚠ JANE, 6 Aug 2026 — FOUND: the account of sales reads “1 Negro Woman Jane ... James Stephens ... 700.00,” sold 29 April 1844 to the administrator, Moore’s own brother [STEPHENS-13]. She was 65% of the whole estate ($700 of $1,075.86), so roughly $70 of Moore’s $107.39 share was her price. In 1850 James Stephens held 13 people in Greene Co.; Jane is very probably the 38-year-old woman, but that schedule names no one [STEPHENS-14]. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HER NAME IS ON THE OUTSIDE OF THE FILE [STEPHENS-19]: the clerk endorsed the packet “1105 · Greene County, Ala. · 1 NEGRO WOMAN JANE · Inventory of Notes · Estate file · Thorn, Mary · 1844 · James Stephens, administrator.” Everything Mary Thorn owned was summarised in two lines on the wrapper, and the first line is Jane. ⛔⛔ The packet is now BOUNDED — images 591–600, all read. The HIRE BOND and any PETITION TO SELL LAND are NOT in the loose papers (601 starts an 1825 estate; 589 is an 1861 guardian bond). Both must be sought in the GREENE CO. ORPHANS’ COURT MINUTE BOOKS 1844–46. ⚠ And the land was sold at the ordinary sale (Andrew Norwood, $127.09) — so a separate petition may never have existed, in which case this node may not be movable off ‘hyp’ by that route at all. Still owed: the 1870 census of Greene & Sumter Cos. for Jane.

Elisha Truitt or Noel Truett (1786–1864 / 1769–1850 — disputed)

Ahnentafel #352 · his father’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (8 generations back) · Hypothesis — a candidate, not tied in

Two men have been proposed as the father of Rev. William Hampton Truett: Elisha Truitt, who lived from 1786 to 1864, and Noel Truett, who lived from 1769 to 1850. Elisha is thought the more likely candidate, since a grandson, John Wesley Truett, named his own son Elisha Dozier Truett in 1877, a naming pattern common when honoring a grandfather. The question remains unsettled and awaits South Carolina and early Alabama land and probate records.

Evidence

The tree offers two conflicting fathers for Rev. William Hampton Truett. One piece of real evidence points to Elisha: John Wesley Truett named a son Elisha Dozier Truett in 1877, and families of this period named children for grandfathers with great regularity.

Outstanding: Not settled — needs South Carolina and early Alabama land and probate records. “Dozier” is itself a surname and may carry another connection.

Gershom Tuttle II (22 Aug 1738, Farmington, Connecticut – 5 Jan 1818)

Ahnentafel #384 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (8 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Gershom Tuttle II, remembered as "the Patriot," was born 22 August 1738 in Farmington, Connecticut (in the New Cambridge parish, now Bristol), and died 5 January 1818. During the Revolutionary War he served as a teamster, conducting wagon trains of army provisions — an operation that hauled some 3,000 barrels of supplies, roughly 500 tons, from Connecticut into New York State. The Connecticut General Assembly paid him for this service in 1780, and he is recognized today as Daughters of the American Revolution Patriot Ancestor A131791.

He married Tabitha Mitchell at Deerfield, Massachusetts, on 22 December 1757. The couple later moved first to New Hampshire, then to Clinton in Oneida County, New York, and finally to Watertown, New York, where Gershom served as the first deacon of the town's first Congregational church, organized in 1803.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE DAR’S OWN RECORD, READ 7 AUGUST 2026 [TUTTLE-46]. Ancestor # A131791 — TUTTLE, GERSHOM — Connecticut, rank TEAMSTER — b. 23 Aug 1738 — d. 5 Jan 1818, WATERTOWN, NEW YORK — “Conductor of teams transporting supplies for the army.”

⭐⭐ This settles two long-running conflicts in this project. The birth year is 1738, not 1739 — the DAR and the Farmington birth record agree. And he died at Watertown, New York, not in Indiana; the Otter Creek burial belongs to his son the colonel. ⚠ One small gap survives: the Farmington register was read here as 22 August, the DAR says 23rd. Settle it from the register image before the SAR application is filed.

⭐ Eight of the DAR’s nineteen approved applications on this patriot descend through exactly our couple — “child GERSHOM, spouse PARMELIA STRONG CLARK” — including National No. 536770, which is the record copy already sitting in the frozen SAR Evidence folder [SAR_32]. The other nine descend through his son Solomon.

⚠ NUMBERED 6 Aug 2026 at Alec’s correction — he is GERSHOM II, between his father Ensign Gershom I (node 768, d. 1777) and his son Col. Gershom III (node 192, d. 1823). The 1777 headstone [TUTTLE-33] is his FATHER’S, not his — this man died 5 January 1818, forty-one years later. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HIS BIRTH RECORD FOUND 6 Aug 2026, AND IT NAMES A FATHER: “Gershom Tuttle, birth 22 August 1738, Farmington, Hartford, Connecticut — Father’s Name: GERSHOM TUTTLE” [TUTTLE-15]. ⚠ Our notes said 23 Aug 1739 Bristol; the record says 22 Aug 1738 Farmington. Bristol IS Farmington — Bristol was the parish of New Cambridge inside Farmington township until 1785, which is exactly how the 1757 Deerfield marriage styles him. The place is confirmed, not contradicted; the YEAR 1739 in our notes is probably wrong. THE PATRIOT. A conductor of teams. On 23 May 1780 the Connecticut General Assembly paid him £285.13.8 in wages and £60.13.11 for forage IN ADVANCE, and still owed him £200.2.8; he is listed first of nine conductors. The operation: 3,000 barrels of provisions — 500 tons — hauled from Connecticut into New York State, averaging 115 miles. DAR ancestor A131791. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ 6 Aug 2026 — ENTRY 9251 OF THE 1883 GENEALOGY, IN FULL [TUTTLE-30]: “Gershom Tuttle, b. Aug. 28, 1738; d. Jan. 5, 1818, in his 80th yr.; served, it is said, as Capt. in Rev. army through the war. He went first to NEW HAMPSHIRE, thence, about 1790 to CLINTON, ONEIDA CO., N.Y., afterward joined the settlement at WATERTOWN, JEFFERSON CO., N.Y., and with William Fellows, elected the FIRST DEACON of the first Cong. chh., organized June 3, 1803.” This is the entry that validated the numbering ladder and broke the wall at his father. ⚠ DATE CONFLICT, RECORDED NOT RESOLVED: the entry prints Aug. 28; his father’s child-list on the facing page prints “I. Gershom, b. Aug. 22, 1738”, and the Farmington town record says 22 August. The town record wins; the printed 28 is the book’s error and is logged, not dropped. ⭐ The New Hampshire stage is new, and Clinton/Oneida is now confirmed from a second direction.

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ A LIVE CONFLICT ON HIS DEATH PLACE. SAR evidence file 04 — the 1883 Tuttle genealogy p.511 — says he died “on his son’s farm,” Otter Creek, Vigo Co., Indiana. Find a Grave 85146147, citing our own DAR number, says Watertown, Jefferson Co., New York, burial UNKNOWN. SAR file 29 was assembled specifically to document the conflict, so it predates this project. ⚠ The same 1883 work misprints Clark Strong Tuttle’s county, so it is capable of this error. ⚠ Note too that Clark was a MILLER at Otter Creek, not a farmer. UNRESOLVED.

Tabitha Mitchell (b. 9 Feb 1736/7 – m. 22 Dec 1757, Deerfield; daughter of Joseph Mitchell and Mary Allis)

Ahnentafel #385 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (8 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Tabitha Mitchell was born 9 February 1736/7 and married Gershom Tuttle II on 22 December 1757 at Deerfield, Massachusetts. She was the daughter of Joseph Mitchell, a weaver, and his wife Mary Allis — and because Mary Allis's sister Lois had married Gershom's own father a generation earlier, Tabitha and Gershom were first cousins when they wed.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE DAR DOES NOT KNOW HER GIVEN NAME — AND WE DO [TUTTLE-46, 7 August 2026]. On DAR ancestor record A131791 the patriot’s wife is listed only as “X MITCHEL” — surname, no more. This project has her as Tabitha Mitchell, married at Deerfield 22 December 1757, with her parents Joseph Mitchell and Mary Allis and her grandfather Michael Mitchell of Wallingford, all from records. Alec is in a position to give the DAR something it does not have — worth saying to the registrar when the application goes in.

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HER PARENTS FOUND — AND GERSHOM II MARRIED HIS FIRST COUSIN [TUTTLE-41]. History of the Town of Sunderland, image 567: “MITCHELL, Joseph, m. Nov. 2, 1726, MARY, dau. WILLIAM ALLIS.” And from the Allis side, image 355: “Ch.: MARY, b. Feb. 18, 1705; m. Joseph Mitchell. … LOIS, b. Jan. 13, 1708; m. Oct. 17, 1737, Gershom Tuttle.” ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ MARY ALLIS AND LOIS ALLIS WERE SISTERS. Lois married Gershom Tuttle I. Her sister Mary married Joseph Mitchell. Twenty years later Lois’s son married Mary’s daughter. TABITHA MITCHELL WAS GERSHOM II’S FIRST COUSIN, and the 1757 Deerfield marriage stops being a mystery entirely — he married inside his mother’s family. ⭐ It also explains the Deerfield venue: the bride was local and the groom was styled “of New Cambridge” [TUTTLE-26] — the DEERFIELD hypothesis, preferred over the Farmington one in TUTTLE-24, is confirmed. ⛔⛔ AND IT HALF-CLOSES THE MAYFLOWER DOOR [MAYFLOWER-01]. Her MOTHER’S line is the ALLIS line already on this chart — Braintree freeman 1640, Massachusetts Bay, not Plymouth. The Mayflower question now rests on her FATHER Joseph Mitchell alone, whose origin the Sunderland book does not give. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HER BIRTH AND HER FAMILY FOUND — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-40]. Sheldon, History of Deerfield vol. 2, image 505, in the MITCHELL genealogy: “Ch.: Joseph, Mar. 3, 1726-7, in SUND[ERLAND]. TABITHA, Feb. 9, 1736-7; m. Dec. 22…” The marriage day and month match ours exactly — 22 December 1757 — and the birth of 9 February 1736/7 sits inside the c.1735–40 window TUTTLE-24 predicted for her when the Farmington Mitchel hypothesis was killed. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND THE FAMILY WAS AT SUNDERLAND. Her brother was born there in 1726-7. Sunderland is the town where the groom’s own parents married in 1737 [node 769]. Father and son married women from the same small Connecticut Valley town, twenty years apart. That is not coincidence; that is a family that kept its Massachusetts ties. ⚠ Her father is almost certainly JOSEPH MITCHELL of Deerfield — Sheldon has daughters Hannah (m. Zadock King 1751), Thankful, Mary (m. Samuel — of Ashfield, 1753) and Lydia all styled “dau. Joseph Mitchell”, plus the son Joseph. Recorded as a strong identification, not yet a proved one — the head-of-family paragraph has not been read. ⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND THIS IS THE ONE PLACE A MAYFLOWER LINE COULD ENTER THIS TREE [MAYFLOWER-01]. The Plymouth Colony Mitchells descend from EXPERIENCE MITCHELL, who came on the ANNE in 1623 and married JANE COOKE, daughter of FRANCIS COOKE, MAYFLOWER PASSENGER, 1620. ⭐ And Sheldon’s own pages carry the tell: “FULLER, John; m. July 12, 1774, EXPERIENCE MITCHELL of Ash[field]” — the given name “Experience” running in this very Mitchell family in this very valley is the classic marker of descent from him. ⚠⚠ A MARKER IS NOT A DESCENT. Mitchell is a common name and no link is shown. Nothing about the Mayflower goes on this chart until Joseph Mitchell’s own father is documented. ——— Earlier note: Married at Deerfield, Massachusetts — “TUTTLE, Gershom of New Cambridge, C[onn.] … Mitchel, Dec. 22, 1757.” ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ 6 Aug 2026 — THE FIRST CHECKABLE LEAD ON HER FAMILY [TUTTLE-23]: the complete signer list of the October 1742 New Cambridge petition — the one GERSHOM TUTTLE SR. signed — includes “Joel mitchel.” A MITCHEL was living in that small Connecticut parish fifteen years before the groom’s son married a Tabitha Mitchell.

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ A LEAD, NOT A PROOF — Joel is not shown to be her father. ⚠ And it cuts both ways: the Deerfield entry styles the GROOM “of New Cambridge” but gives the bride no parish, which is what a clerk does for a LOCAL bride. Test both hypotheses in parallel: ① a TABITHA MITCHELL born at Farmington c.1735–1740 (cheapest test; would give her parents for the first time); ② Joel Mitchel’s own family; ③ the Deerfield/Hampshire Co. Mitchells. ⚠⚠ TESTED ONE TURN LATER AND LARGELY FAILED [TUTTLE-24]. Joel Mitchel is confirmed real and really at Farmington — his eldest child Mary was born there 2 Sept 1743, eleven months after the petition — but his first recorded child is 1743, AFTER Tabitha’s c.1735–40 birth window, no Tabitha appears among his seven children, and he moved to WETHERSFIELD from 1746 onward. ⛔ By the 1757 marriage the Mitchels had left New Cambridge. ⛔ And no Connecticut or Massachusetts birth for a Tabitha Mitchell 1730–1742 exists in the FS index at all — 3,822 results, and the only New England hit is the 1757 marriage itself. She still has no parent, no birth date and no place. ⭐ The DEERFIELD hypothesis is now the stronger one — the entry styles the GROOM “of New Cambridge” (an outsider) and gives the bride no parish (a local). NEXT: the Deerfield and Hampshire Co. Mitchells, and the 1757 entry read AT THE IMAGE — the printed abstract may drop a father or a parish the register carries.

Pamelia Clark's father (of Farmington, Connecticut; name not recorded)

Ahnentafel #386 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father (8 generations back) · Known unknown — the person existed; the name does not survive

Pamelia Clark's father was a man of Farmington, Connecticut, of the Revolutionary generation, whose daughter was born there on 4 November 1770 — but his own name has not been recovered. He is remembered indirectly through his grandson, General Newman S. Clarke of the United States Army, who was born in Connecticut, commissioned an officer in 1812, and went on to command the Department of the Pacific before his death in 1860.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 7 August 2026 — AND THIS IS THE SINGLE HIGHEST-VALUE GAP ON THE CHART [TUTTLE-46]. He is a Farmington, Connecticut man of the Revolutionary generation with a daughter born 4 November 1770. Naming him serves both of Alec’s stated priorities at once — it opens a whole colonial branch and he is a strong candidate to be a patriot in his own right.

⭐ Two levers, neither of them used before.
(a) The nephew. Gen. Newman S. Clarke, USA — Heitman’s Historical Register confirms the Tuttle book in every checkable particular: “Clarke, Newman S. Conn. Vt. Ens 11 inf 12 Mar 1812 … col 6 inf 29 June 1846 … bvt brig gen 29 Mar 1847 … died 17 Oct 1860” — born in Connecticut, commissioned from Vermont, died at San Francisco commanding the Department of the Pacific. His father was necessarily one of Pamelia’s brothers. ⛔ Appleton’s Cyclopædia gives him a full career paragraph and no parentage.
(b) The brother at the arsenal. ⛔ He was NOT the commandant — the Watervliet Arsenal’s official chronology 1813–1993 contains no Clark anywhere in the 1813–1850 narrative, and no commander died in office; Weise’s History of the City of Troy yields eleven arsenal references and no Clark. So he was a military storekeeper or ordnance officer, and the Registers of Officers and Agents for 1826 and 30 September 1835 both tabulate storekeepers against the station “Ar. Watervliet.”

Outstanding — the one record to pull: the Barbour Collection, FARMINGTON — the birth of Pamelia Clark, 4 November 1770, which names her father outright, and the marriage Tuttle × Clark, 8 July 1788. ⚠ Also worth one query: the DAR Child Search (child Parmelia Clark, spouse Gershom Tuttle) — it was attempted and could not be submitted; a control query with known-good values failed identically, so nothing was concluded from the blank result.

George Roush (July 1761, Frederick Co., Virginia – 31 May 1845, Meigs Co., Ohio)

Ahnentafel #480 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (8 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

George Roush was born in July 1761 in Frederick County, Virginia, and died 31 May 1845 in Sutton Township, Meigs County, Ohio. He served in the Revolutionary War, enlisting in the autumn of 1779 under his own brother, Captain John Roush, with further tours of duty in 1780 and 1781, and later drew a pension while living in Ohio. He is recognized today as Daughters of the American Revolution Patriot Ancestor A098975.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ A REVOLUTIONARY PATRIOT, AND THE DAR HAS ALREADY APPROVED THE DESCENT [ROUSH-54, 7 August 2026].

DAR Ancestor # A098975 — Service: VIRGINIA, rank Private. Service description: “1) Capts John Roush, Joseph Pugh; 2) served as a substitute, Capt All.” Residence of record: Shenandoah Co., Virginia. Pension S18579. He enlisted in the autumn of 1779 and served two months in his own brother Captain John Roush’s Virginia company, with further tours in 1780 and 1781, and was pensioned in 1832 while living in Ohio.

⭐⭐ Five approved DAR applications descend through this man’s son JOHN and his wife REGINA ROUSH — National Nos. 426059, 671858, 728245, 569934 (vol. 871) and 976675. That is Alec’s own line, already accepted.

⚠ A printed error corrected here. The 1928 Roush genealogy prints his pension as “S. 8579”, and the Roush Family Association’s own website repeats it. S8579 is Jacob Goss of North Carolina — read in full and checked. The book dropped a digit; the true number is S18579.

Outstanding: his own pension file, read as images rather than as an abstract — it should name Catharine and their children.

Catharine Zerkel (wife of George Roush)

Catharine Zerkel was the first wife of George Roush, named as such independently by both the 1928 Roush family genealogy and the Daughters of the American Revolution's own record for her husband.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 7 August 2026 [ROUSH-54]. Named as George Roush’s first wife independently by two sources that did not consult each other: the 1928 Roush genealogy (Catharine Zerkel) and the DAR ancestor record for A098975, which lists spouse “CATHERINE ZIRKLE”. The DAR’s approved applications through son John are all marked to spouse [1] — i.e. to her, not to George’s second wife.

⭐ ZERKEL / ZIRKLE / ZERCKEL is a name that keeps recurring around this family — a Zerkle man was living in the household where James Nease appears in two censuses, two Mason County Neases married Zerkel women in 1845 and 1852, and Moses B. Roush’s third wife was a Margaret A. Zerkle. Recorded as a pattern, not a conclusion.

Outstanding: her dates, her parents, and the marriage record itself.

Jonas Roush (Sept 1763, Frederick Co., Virginia – 14 Dec 1850, Mason Co., Virginia)

Ahnentafel #482 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father (8 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Jonas Roush was born in September 1763 at Holman's Fort, Frederick County, Virginia, and died 14 December 1850 in Mason County, Virginia. He served in the Revolutionary War as a substitute for his brother Henry in the summer of 1781, and on a second tour was present at the siege of Yorktown and Cornwallis's surrender on 19 October 1781. He later told his story in his own sworn pension statement of 6 November 1832, and is recognized today as Daughters of the American Revolution Patriot Ancestor A098985. His brother Henry Roush was also honored as a Revolutionary patriot.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ A REVOLUTIONARY PATRIOT WHO WATCHED CORNWALLIS SURRENDER [ROUSH-54, 7 August 2026].

DAR Ancestor # A098985 — Service: VIRGINIA, rank Private; “Capt All; Cols Bird, Darke.” Pension S4785, and the file has been read in full transcription. In his own sworn statement of 6 November 1832, made from Mason County, Virginia, he says he “entered the service as a substitute for his Brother Henry Roush who was drafted for a tour of three months in the summer of 1781,” served under Captain All in Colonel Bird’s regiment at Falmouth near Fredericksburg, and on a second three-month tour was present at the siege of Yorktown and at Cornwallis’s surrender, 19 October 1781. He fell ill near Fredericksburg while escorting prisoners. Pensioned at $20 a year from 4 March 1831; the pension was transferred to Meigs County, Ohio in 1837.

⭐⭐ Two approved DAR applications descend through his daughter REGINA and her husband JOHN ROUSH (National Nos. 569934 and 1076887). ⭐⭐⭐ And member 569934 is approved on BOTH Jonas and George — that member carries the same double descent Alec has.

⭐ He was living in Mason County when he applied — the very county where his great-grandson John G. Roush would later marry and die. ⚠ A conflict to hold: the pension says he removed to Meigs County, Ohio in 1837 and the 1928 book says he died in his daughter’s house there, but the DAR records his death in Mason County, Virginia. Not resolved.

⛔ A bounded negative worth knowing: the pension file does not name his wife Barbara or any child, including Regina. It records only that his wife died in February 1837 and that he had a daughter in Ohio. The file corroborates the descent; it does not by itself prove it.

⭐ His brother is a patriot too: HENRY ROUSH, DAR # A098977, b. 1752 Frederick Co., Va., d. 26 Oct 1831 Meigs Co., Ohio — “Capt Jacob Holeman, Dunmore Co. Militia, 1775; paid for substitute” — and the DAR’s cited source for Henry’s service is Jonas’s own pension file. The two brothers’ service is documented out of the same sheaf of paper.

Barbara Fox was the wife of Jonas Roush and died in February 1837, the same year her husband moved to Ohio to live with their daughter. She is named as his wife in both the 1928 Roush family genealogy and Daughters of the American Revolution records.

Henry Roush Sr. (1752 – 26 Oct 1831, Plants Cemetery, Meigs Co., Ohio)

Ahnentafel #500 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s mother’s father’s father (8 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Henry Roush Sr. lived from 1752 until 26 October 1831 and was buried at Plants Cemetery, Letart Falls, Meigs County, Ohio, where his gravestone gives his age as 79. He probably served in the Revolutionary War as a private in Captain John Tipton's company, paid off at Romney after eighty-eight days of service, and his brother Jonas's own pension application recalls serving three months as a substitute for him. He is probably the same man recognized by the Daughters of the American Revolution as Patriot Ancestor A098977, though this has not yet been confirmed from the DAR's own record.

Evidence

John Tipton’s Co., paid off at Romney, 88 days, receipt 25 Oct 1775 (Virginia State Library certification, 1926, quoted in full) — plus Jonas’s pension: “served three months for his brother Henry.” Signed the 1793 Solomonburg church deed “HENRICH ROUSH (in German).” ?= DAR A098977 (b. 1752 Frederick Co., d. 26 Oct 1831 Meigs Co. — triple date/place match; DAR page unread). Dorothea, whose surname has not been recovered, was the wife of Henry Roush Sr. She signed a church deed by her mark in 1793, the same document her husband signed in German, and is believed to be buried beside him at Plants Cemetery.

The patriarch of the Rickard family came from Germany by way of Maryland and settled in Mason County, in what later became West Virginia. He was the father of four immigrant brothers — Michael, Jacob, John and Joseph Rickard — as well as of daughters Elizabeth and Susannah, but even the family's own 1928 genealogy could not recover his name, printing only a blank dash in its place.

The wife of the Rickard patriarch has not been named in any record found so far, though her existence is implied by the large family she and her husband raised.
