John Bell of Camden District (candidate) (b. probably c.1710–1730 (if Robert Bell of Bertie's son John, b. before c.1717); seated on Bear Swamp, Duplin Co., N.C. by 1762, there 1773; will dated 18 Oct 1786, Camden District, S.C. (abstract only))

Ahnentafel #512 · his father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (9 generations back) · Hypothesis — a candidate, not tied in

If the abstract is right, he is the man who sat on Bear Swamp in Duplin County before the war, sold his plantation to his eldest son in 1765 for £100 and went on living on it, and then — somewhere after January 1773 — followed both his sons four hundred miles south to the Black River country, where he died with a will that named his children in order: Frederick first, then William Rasor, then Penelope, whose own children would carry the name of their “Grandfather John Bell” into the deed books of two states. And if he is who the Bertie papers suggest, his own father could not write — he signed with a big interlocked R B — while John and his brothers all signed their names.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ A named, located candidate — still not tied in (22 Aug 2026). Holcomb’s 1978 abstract of a will dated 18 Oct 1786 names wife Mary, “eldest son Frederick Bell,” William Rasor Bell and “eldest daughter Penelope Stuckey”; the Kershaw General Index to Estates reads “Bell, Jno — Frederick Bell — Adm’r — Apt 6 — Pkg 180” (apartment and package read directly; year cell illegible). Independent support: John Stuckey of Duplin’s wife was Penelope (1779); Lewis Stuckey’s 1792 deed says his property “descended to me from my Grandfather John Bell” and by 1794 he is “now of the County of Salem, South Carolina”; in 1790 there was exactly one Frederick Bell in the state; and a 1787 Claremont deed puts Jacob Hurst beside Frederick [BELL-446–448, 458, 481, 482].

⭐⭐ The family this John probably belongs to is now documented in its own right: Robert Bell of Chowan and Bertie Co., N.C., died intestate 1738; his children signed for their shares — George (“his Eldest Son and heir at Law”), John, Archibald, Penelope — with William Byrd as son-in-law; the family moved to Duplin by 1754 (Archibald “of Duplin County” 1756), and Sampson County was cut out under them in 1784. A literate John Bell Sr of Bear Swamp, seated by 1762, sold to Frederick Bell in 1765, was “Sr” in 1773, and then vanishes from every North Carolina record [BELL-472, 475, 478, 481]. The working hypothesis is that this John is Robert’s son and the 1786 testator.

⛔ Why still hypothesis, not probable: the only text calling William Rasor a son is a 2004 message-board condensation; Holcomb’s own index returns “William Rasor Bell” on p. 5 but refuses “son William Rasor Bell” (adversary, [BELL-458]); and no John Bell or Mary Bell appears anywhere near Frederick in Camden District 1775–90, on ground where the corpus finds Frederick by name seven times [BELL-482]. The Camden probate books for 1786–91 were lost to the Claremont courthouse fire of 1801 [BELL-463–464].

Outstanding: read Holcomb, Camden District Wills and Administrations, printed p. 5 (2017 reprint, ~$20) — does it say son?; then the packet, SCDAH L 49055 Apt 6 Pkg 180. Patriot question: the 1783 Camden petit-juror “Bell, John” is probably the Broad River John [BELL-461–462]; only the engrossed 1783 schedule (SCDAH S 165001) could revive it.

⭐⭐⭐ 23 Aug 2026 — the ladder above hardened dramatically. The originals were read at the ink: Henry Woodnot’s 1718 will names Anne, a widow Ferguson, as Robert Bell’s wife; John Clemet’s 1734/5 will names “his two Sons John Bell and Archiball Bell” — Robert’s son John is documented twice over; John’s Bertie record (patent on Pellmell Pocoson 1739 as “John Bell, Junior”; last land sold Feb 1746/7) stops dead in the exact 1747–49 window the whole kin-group — Bells, Kings, Snells, Herrings — relocated to Duplin, and Archibald pairs with John at both ends (Bertie 1738/1749; the 1766 Duplin witness line). “John of Bertie = Bear Swamp John Sr” is now graded PROBABLE — the adversary’s own kill-tests all passed (no John Bell on any Bertie tax list 1751–63; the ten-year-late witness-oath proof of his last deed; the 1737 “second John” an OCR phantom) and the ruling adversary promoted it 24 Aug [BELL-497–498], with the explicit boundary that the promotion must not leak upward into THIS node’s rung; the Bear-Swamp-John-to-this-testator rung is still the hypothesis this node’s grade rests on [BELL-486–492]. See 00_BELL_LINEAGE.html for the full ladder.

⛔⭐⭐ 24 Aug 2026 [BELL-500] — the abstract’s “son-” prefix was the poster’s, not Holcomb’s. A controlled phrase-oracle over the 1978 volume (two passes, 700+ logged queries [BELL-500, 505]) proves page 5 reads “my eldest son Frederick Bell,” “my eldest daughter Penelope Stuckey,” “wife Mary” — and then TWO formally parallel bare bequests: “to John Bell” and “…sterling to William Rasor Bell” (cash, not land), neither with a kin term — the classic shape of legacies to grandsons or kinsmen; Holcomb’s own 1978 index binds the W. Rasor Bell of p.35 to the man of p.5 (the words “his” and “children,” which the 2004 condensation rests on, occur zero times on the page). The candidacy is not dead — W.R.B. stands in the will as a legatee of unstated relation (son-unstyled, grandson, or kinsman), and the Stuckey and Kershaw-index corroboration hold — but the relationship itself is now known to be UNSTATED in the abstract, and only the original packet can state it. If W.R.B. should prove Frederick’s son, the ladder renumbers — flagged, not adopted.

Mary (—) Bell (candidate) (living 18 Oct 1786, Camden District, S.C.)

Sarah Pigford's father (of New Hanover or Duplin Co., North Carolina, mid-1700s; name not recorded)

Sarah Pigford's father lived in New Hanover or Duplin County, North Carolina, in the mid-eighteenth century, but his name has not survived. Even the family's own genealogist, writing in 1967 after searching the marriage records of both counties, concluded that "her family name continues to be beyond the reach of this recorder."

Ensign Gershom Tuttle I (b. 11 Aug 1714 Wallingford – Chippens Hill, New Cambridge – d. 13 Aug 1777, Claremont, N.H.)

Ahnentafel #768 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (9 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Ensign Gershom Tuttle I was born 11 August 1714 in Wallingford, Connecticut, the son of Timothy Tuttle and Thankful Doolittle. As a young man he moved to the Massachusetts frontier, taking up land his father had obtained in a new township near Northfield, and there he married Lois Allis at Sunderland on 17 October 1737. The couple went on to settle at Chippen's Hill in the New Cambridge parish of Farmington, Connecticut (now Bristol), where he became a prominent landholder and churchman and served in the French and Indian Wars, earning the rank of ensign.

Late in life he moved his family north again, to Claremont, New Hampshire, where he died 13 August 1777, in the second summer of the Revolutionary War — even as his son Gershom II was serving as a wagon-team conductor for the Continental cause back in Connecticut.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE RANK CONFLICT IS RESOLVED, AND HIS EXACT NEIGHBOURHOOD IS NAMED — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-39]. Miscellaneous Notes on New England Families, continuing: “On his return from the north, Gershom Tuttle joined THE COLONY OF PEOPLE FROM CHESHIRE AND VICINITY who removed to the town of Farmington and settled on CHIPPEN’S HILL, in what was called New Cambridge and is now the town of Bristol. There he had his home and was prominent in church and town affairs. HE SERVED IN THE FRENCH AND INDIAN WARS AND IS CALLED ENSIGN GERSHOM TUTTLE.” ⭐⭐ THAT SETTLES THE CONFLICT LOGGED IN TUTTLE-33. The gravestone calls him ENSIGN; the 1883 book calls him “Capt. of the train-band”. The Ensign’s commission came from the FRENCH AND INDIAN WARS, not the local militia — so the stone is recording war service, and the two titles are two different things. ⭐ NEW RESEARCH TARGET: the Connecticut French and Indian War rolls, c.1755–1762. ⭐⭐⭐ AND IT EXPLAINS THE 1742 PETITION GROUP. He did not drift to Farmington — he went with a COLONY of Cheshire people, which is why Simon Tuttle and the other names on that petition [TUTTLE-20/23] are the same names as the Cheshire records. They moved as a body, and Chippen’s Hill is where they landed. ⚠⚠⚠ AND A LEAD FLAGGED, NOT WRITTEN AS A FINDING. The manuscript continues: “As is well known there was much dissention there in New Cambridge before and during the Revolutionary War. CHIPPENS HILL WAS A CENTER OF EPISCOPALIAN AND ‘TORY’ FEELING. Many of the families…” — and the transcript breaks off there. ⚠ DO NOT DRAW THE OBVIOUS CONCLUSION. Two facts already on this chart point in opposite directions: his son Gershom II is a DOCUMENTED PATRIOT (DAR A131791, paid by the Connecticut Assembly in 1780), and a Simon Tuttle was one of ten men who in 1747 “publikly declared themselves of the Church of England” at New Cambridge [TUTTLE-20]. Read the next page of the manuscript before a word of this goes in as fact. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ “CARLETON” IS SOLVED, AND IT IS ARLINGTON — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-38]. Miscellaneous Notes on New England Families (FS film #007549089, ark 3:1:3QS7-89DX-THK7) — a manuscript that had already solved this problem and cites its sources: “Gershom Tuttle was the son of Timothy Tuttle and his wife THANKFUL DOOLITTLE, and was born in Wallingford, Aug. 11, 1714 (Wallingford Vitals, vol. 2, p. 799). … As a young man Gershom Tuttle removed from Wallingford to northern Massachusetts, where he took up THE LAND WHICH HIS FATHER TIMOTHY TUTTLE HAD OBTAINED IN THE NEW TOWN OF ARLINGTON, JUST ABOVE NORTHFIELD (which later became WINCHESTER, NEW HAMPSHIRE). During the time he was up there he evidently stayed some time in NORTHFIELD (where HIS UNCLE THE REV. BENJAMIN DOOLITTLE was the Pastor of the church) and Sunderland, somewhat farther south. There, in Sunderland, Mass., Gershom Tuttle married, Oct. 17, 1737, LOIS ALLIS (from Sunderland Town records, as given in the History of Sunderland). She was the daughter of WILLIAM ALLIS of Sunderland…” ⭐ The clerk’s hand read “Carleton” and “Earlenton” — it is ARLINGTON, and the flag on that one word was worth keeping. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND THE MOVE WAS KINSHIP, NOT SPECULATION. His father held the proprietor’s right; his MOTHER’S BROTHER was the minister twenty miles down the road at Northfield; he lodged with his uncle and married a girl from Sunderland. That also confirms THANKFUL DOOLITTLE as his mother from a third independent direction — this compiler calls Rev. Benjamin Doolittle his UNCLE. ⭐⭐⭐ AND IT MAKES HIS LIFE A SHAPE: Arlington became WINCHESTER, NEW HAMPSHIRE — so the frontier township he settled at twenty-two was in what is now New Hampshire, and he died in New Hampshire, at Claremont, forty-one years later. He went north twice, and the second time he stayed. ⭐ AND WE NOW HAVE THE CITATION FOR HIS BIRTH ENTRY: WALLINGFORD VITALS, VOL. 2, PAGE 799. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE DEED READ AT THE PAGE — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-37]. FamilySearch ark 3:1:3QS7-89ZD-JW67, image 445 of 849, Hampden/Springfield, Massachusetts Land Records 1735–1737: “Know all Men by these presents that I TIMOTHY TUTTLE of Wallingford in the County of New Haven and Colony of Connecticutt in New England, for divers good causes and considerations and perticularly for LOVE, GOOD WILL AND FATHERLY AFFECTION that I have and [bear] to MY SON GERSHOM TUTTLE of Carleton in the County of Hampshire and province of the Massachusetts Bay in New England, have therefore given, granted and firmly pass over … unto him my said son Gershom Tuttle and to his heirs and assigns forever ALL MY RIGHT OF LAND IN SAID [CARLETON], as well that which is already laid out and all other Divisions that shall be hereafter in said TOWNSHIP on my said RIGHT to be laid out and surveyed … I am the True, Sole and Lawfull owner of the above given and Granted premises … to Warrant, Secure and Defend him the said Gersham Tuttle…” ‘Fatherly affection’ is the standard phrase of a New England gift deed, and it is the plainest statement of parentage the period produces. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND IT REWRITES THE ORDER OF EVENTS. He is already “of … the County of HAMPSHIRE, Massachusetts” in 1736 — a year and more BEFORE he married Lois Allis at Sunderland in October 1737. He did not go north because of the deed; he was already there, and his father signed his own share over to him. Sunderland, Hatfield and Montague — the whole Allis country — are in that same county. So the marriage is not a mystery any more: he was a young man on the Massachusetts frontier who married a local girl. ⚠⚠ WHAT “CARLETON” IS, IS STILL OPEN — and the deed itself narrows it hard. It is a TOWNSHIP in which Timothy held a PROPRIETOR’S RIGHT, with later divisions still to be laid out — that is the signature of one of the NEW MASSACHUSETTS TOWNSHIPS granted in 1735, not an old settled town. The transcript renders the name twice and differently (“Carleton”, “Earlenton”), so the clerk’s hand is ambiguous. Identify it against the 1735 Hampshire County township grants and the proprietors’ records — that will say exactly where he was living at 22. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE LAST UNDOCUMENTED LINK IN THE TUTTLE LINE IS CLOSED — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-36]. A DEED OF GIFT OF 1736 IN WHICH TIMOTHY TUTTLE CALLS GERSHOM HIS SON, TWICE. FamilySearch full-text, Springfield, Hampden, Massachusetts. Land Records 1734-1735, 1735-1737, 1736-1744, YEARS: 1736, nine matched passages — “…presents that I TIMOTHY TUTTLE OF WALLINGFORD in …” · “…and are to my SON GERSHOM TUTTLE of Carleton in …” · “…unto him my said son Gershom Tuttle and to his …” · “…to him the said Gershom [Tuttle] his heirs &c…” · “…to and with the said Gershom Tuttle his heirs and…” · “…I the said Timothy Tuttle do bind my self and …” · “…whereof I the said Timothy Tuttle have hereunto …” · “…date above written Timothy Tuttle the above …” The same instrument appears again in Hampden, Massachusetts. Cemetery Land Deeds 1733-1737 — “…and Bare to my son Gershom Tuttle of…” ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND IT EXPLAINS THE MARRIAGE THAT HAS PUZZLED THIS FILE FOR DAYS. Why would a Cheshire, Connecticut man marry LOIS ALLIS at SUNDERLAND, MASSACHUSETTS in October 1737? Because in 1736 his father deeded him land in the Massachusetts Connecticut Valley. (Springfield’s registry served the whole valley — Hampden was part of HAMPSHIRE County until 1812, so these ARE the Hampshire records.) Father gives land 1736 → son marries a valley girl 1737 → son is buying in Farmington by 1739-40. The sequence finally reads. ⚠ TWO THINGS STILL TO DO ON IT: ① open the deed IMAGE — everything above is FamilySearch’s OCR of the deed book, which is the record’s own words but not yet read at the page; ② “Gershom Tuttle of CARLETON” is unexplained — no Massachusetts or Connecticut town of that name fits 1736. Probably an OCR misreading of the son’s residence. Flagged, not smoothed over. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ SPOFFORD’S TRANSCRIPTION READ AT SOURCE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-35] — Grave Stone Records from the Ancient Cemeteries in the Town of Claremont, N.H. (1896), image 78: “Tuttle Gershom, Esq.  Aug. 13, 1777, 63y.” ⚠⚠ SELF-CORRECTION #16: TUTTLE-33 transcribed the photograph as “in ye 63d Year of his Age”. Spofford, standing at the stone in 1896, reads it as an AGE — 63 years — and the arithmetic agrees: b. 11 Aug 1714, d. 13 Aug 1777 is 63 years and two days. Spofford’s reading is the one to carry; mine was made from a photograph at an angle. The day I hedged as ‘5th or 15th’ is the 13th. ⛔⛔ AND A BOUNDED NEGATIVE THAT MATTERS. Spofford transcribed EVERY ancient cemetery in the town, and there are only THREE Tuttles in the whole book: “Tuttle Gershom, Esq. Aug. 13, 1777, 63y. · Tuttle Hannah, wife of Oliver. Sept. 12, 1785, 42y. · Tuttle Daniel, son of Oliver and Hannah. March 2, 1777, 4y.” NO WIFE OF GERSHOM I IS BURIED AT CLAREMONT. Lois Allis is not beside him, and she is not in that town at all. So she died before the family left Connecticut, or she is buried somewhere else entirely — see node 769. ⭐⭐⭐ WHAT IS THERE INSTEAD IS A HARDER STORY. The other two Tuttles are his son OLIVER’S wife and OLIVER’S small son: Daniel, aged four, buried 2 March 1777 — and Gershom I himself five months later, 13 August 1777. This family buried a grandson in the spring and the grandfather in the summer of the same year. And Hannah, Oliver’s wife, died 12 September 1785 aged 42 — which is very likely why Oliver left: the 1883 book has him going from Claremont to New York “about 1790”, five years after burying her. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE GRAVE IS FOUND, AND IT IS NOT WHERE THE BOOK SAID — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-34]. ENS Gershom Tuttle, b. 11 Aug 1714 Wallingford, Connecticut; d. 13 Aug 1777, aged 63, at CLAREMONT, SULLIVAN COUNTY, NEW HAMPSHIRE. Buried UNION CEMETERY, Claremont — Section B, Lot 106. Find a Grave memorial 33451086 (created 2009), citing Charles B. Spofford, Grave Stone Records from the Ancient Cemeteries in the Town of Claremont, N.H., 1896 — a published transcription, not a user guess. The transcription records “Esq. — age 63 years.” ⚠⚠⚠ THE 1883 BOOK IS WRONG ON THE PLACE AS WELL AS THE DATE. Entry 925 says “rem. from Cheshire to Bristol, and d. there, a. 74”. He did not die at Bristol and he was not 74. He died at Claremont, New Hampshire, two days after his sixty-third birthday. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND THIS EXPLAINS SOMETHING WE HAD NO EXPLANATION FOR. Entry 9251 says his son the patriot “went FIRST TO NEW HAMPSHIRE, thence about 1790 to Clinton, Oneida Co., N.Y.” The New Hampshire stage was his FATHER’S move. Gershom I took the family from Connecticut to Claremont and died there in the second summer of the Revolution. ⭐⭐⭐ AND THE WHOLE SIBLING GROUP WENT AND LEFT TOGETHER: the same 1883 book, image 713, has “OLIVER Tuttle, from CLAREMONT, N.H., to N.Y. state about 1790; rem. from Paris, Oneida Co., N.Y.…” — and Oliver, b. 1739, is Gershom I’s second son. Two brothers, same town, same year, same destination. Cheshire/Bristol, Conn. → Claremont, N.H. → Paris & Clinton, Oneida Co., N.Y. ⭐⭐ HIS CHILDREN, as the memorial links them: Gershom II 1738–1818 (the patriot), Oliver 1739–1834, Samuel 1747–1805, and LOIS, d. 1769 — ⭐⭐⭐⭐ a daughter named LOIS, which is his wife’s name. The 1883 book prints only “He m. ——.” A daughter called Lois is exactly what a man married to LOIS ALLIS (node 769) names his girl. ——— Earlier note: ✅ THE 1777 HEADSTONE IS THIS MAN — GERSHOM I — confirmed by Alec 6 Aug 2026. Not the patriot (II, d. 5 Jan 1818) and not the Colonel (III, d. 5 Sep 1823). The stone’s own arithmetic is what rules them out: “in ye 63d Year of his Age” in 1777 requires a birth in 1714–15, which is I and only I. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HIS HEADSTONE, 6 Aug 2026 — ALEC FOUND IT ON FIND A GRAVE AND SENT THE PHOTOGRAPH [TUTTLE-33]. A slate stone with a winged soul effigy, reading: “In Memory of En[sig]n GERSHOM TUTTLE Who died Aug[us]t [1]5th AD 1777, in ye 63d Year of his Age. A Man of Sober Life, Who Lived beloved & Lamented died, & may his Rest…” ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE ARITHMETIC IS EXACT AND IT PROVES THE MAN. Born 11 August 1714, he turned 63 on 11 August 1777 — so a death in early August 1777 leaves him 62 years old, i.e. IN HIS 63d YEAR, to the week. A gravestone cut in 1777 and a genealogy printed in 1883 agree to the month, from opposite ends of a century. ⭐ UPGRADED part → ok. He now has: a contemporary monument; a birth date corroborated by it; the 1742 New Cambridge petition signed “Gerfhum Tuttle”; Farmington land purchases from 1739-40; his marriage at Sunderland 17 Oct 1737; and the Farmington birth record of his son naming him as father. ⚠⚠ AND IT REFUTES THE 1883 BOOK — sixth error caught in that volume. The book says “rem. from Cheshire to Bristol, and d. there, a. 74”, which would put his death about 1788. The stone says 1777 and 62. The book is wrong by eleven years and the printed “a. 74” is withdrawn. ⚠ RANK CONFLICT, LOGGED: the stone calls him ENSIGN; the 1883 book calls him “Capt. of the train-band.” Both can be true across one life (ensign, then captain) — but the stone is the contemporary document and it chose Ensign. ⭐⭐ HE DIED IN THE SECOND SUMMER OF THE REVOLUTION, while his son Gershom was conducting provision teams for the Connecticut Assembly. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND THE FAMILYSEARCH TREE WAS RIGHT — SAY SO. This node’s own outstanding note read: “The FS tree calls him ‘Ensign Gershom Tuttle, 1714–1777’ — a lead to TEST, not to adopt.” Tested. Both the rank and both years are on the stone. That tree has been wrong six times in this project and it is right here, and it deserves the same plain statement the errors got. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE WALL IS DOWN — 6 Aug 2026, ENTRY 925 OF THE 1883 TUTTLE GENEALOGY, READ IN FULL [TUTTLE-30]: “925. Gershom Tuttle, b. Aug. 11, 1714; rem. from CHESHIRE to BRISTOL, and d. there, a. 74. He appears on the Farmington, Conn., rec. as a buyer of land, first in 1739-40, and frequently afterward as buyer or seller. He was CAPT. OF THE TRAIN-BAND. He m. ——.” A birth date, a death, an origin town and a militia rank, for a man who six hours ago had none of them. ⭐ Every part of it corroborates what we already held from records, which is the only way a published genealogy earns credit: he is buying land in Farmington in 1739-40, two years before he signs the October 1742 New Cambridge petition [TUTTLE-20]; and Bristol IS the New Cambridge parish of Farmington until 1785, so the book and the petition describe one man in one place. ⭐⭐ AND IT IDENTIFIES SIMON. Entry 928 Simon Tuttle “rec. a deed of land … from his father, Timothy Tuttle” — so the second Tuttle on the 1742 petition was Gershom’s own BROTHER. ⚠⚠ THE BOOK LEAVES HIS WIFE A DASH — AND WE HAVE HER NAME AND THE BOOK DID NOT. The standard authority on this family for a hundred and forty years prints “He m. ——”; the Sunderland, Massachusetts town record gives LOIS ALLIS, 17 October 1737 (node 769). It also explains the move: a Cheshire man does not start buying Farmington land in 1739-40 by accident — he had married in October 1737. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HIS FATHER IS NOW KNOWN: DEA. TIMOTHY TUTTLE of Cheshire (node 1536), whose child-list on image 575 runs “IV. Mary, b. Oct. 3, 1712 … V. Gershom, b. Aug. 11, 1714” — entry 924 and entry 925, exactly as the numbering predicted. ——— Earlier notes on this node follow. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — a whole generation added at Alec’s request. Named as the father on the Farmington, Connecticut birth record of Gershom the patriot, 22 Aug 1738 (ark 1:1:F74C-P4J). ⭐ His family is reconstructable from the Farmington births: Gershom 1738, Oliver 1739, Eunice 1743, Hannah 1745, Samuel 1747, Huldah 1749 — a child every two years, then an eleven-year gap before the next set (Solomon 1760, Solomon 1762, Theoderit 1763), which belong to his SON and Tabitha Mitchell [TUTTLE-15].

Outstanding: ⛔ His own birth is NOT in the FamilySearch index at all — searched Gershom Tuttle b.1695×1725 across every collection: zero. So the next generation back cannot be reached by index. Routes: Farmington land records (father-to-son conveyance is the classic proof), Hartford District probate, and — most likely — Hampshire Co., Massachusetts records, since he married there. ⚠ The FS tree calls him “Ensign Gershom Tuttle, 1714–1777” — a lead to TEST, not to adopt. ⭐⭐⭐⭐ FOUND IN A DATED DOCUMENT, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-20]: he signs the October 1742 petition of the inhabitants of NEW CAMBRIDGE to hire a winter preacher, printed as “Gerfhum Tuttle” (long-s for Gershum). His son was four years old in 1742, so this is the father. It puts him there as a ratepaying householder, not just a name in a birth register — and it confirms from a third direction that Bristol and Farmington are one place: New Cambridge petitioned for winter preaching precisely because it was a remote parish of Farmington. ⭐⭐ A second Tuttle household is there too: SIMON TUTTLE, signer in 1744 and one of ten who in 1747 “publikly declared themselves of the Church of England” — the most promising associated name for Gershom Sr.’s family. ⛔ The Bristol history gives NO origin for any Tuttle. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ [TUTTLE-17]: Hampshire Co., Mass. probate, 1739/40, records the appraisers of Samuel Taylor’s estate as “Nathan TUTHILL, William Allis, Joseph Root” — TUTHILL is a spelling of TUTTLE. So there were Tuttles AT SUNDERLAND in 1739, standing beside William Allis in the same document, two years after Gershom married Lois there. That is what explains how a Tuttle came to be in that town at all. Look for Gershom Sr. among the SUNDERLAND / Hampshire County TUTHILLS, not among the Connecticut Tuttles — and SPELL IT BOTH WAYS.

Lois Allis (b. 13 Jan 1707/8 Hatfield; m. 17 Oct 1737, Sunderland; sister of Mary Allis)

Ahnentafel #769 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (9 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Lois Allis was born 13 January 1707/8 in Hatfield, Massachusetts, the daughter of William Allis and Elizabeth Davis, and the sister of Mary Allis. She married Gershom Tuttle I at Sunderland, Massachusetts, on 17 October 1737, in her own family's town.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE SUNDERLAND TOWN MARRIAGE RECORD ITSELF, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-41] — History of the Town of Sunderland, image 690: “TUTTLE, Gershom, and LOIS ALLIS, were m. Oct. 17, 1737.” A fourth independent witness, and this one is the town’s own register rather than a genealogy. ⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND HER SISTER MATTERS. Image 355 gives William Allis’s children including “MARY, b. Feb. 18, 1705; m. Joseph Mitchell” and “LOIS, b. Jan. 13, 1708; m. Oct. 17, 1737, Gershom Tuttle”. Mary Allis is TABITHA MITCHELL’S MOTHER (node 771) — so Lois’s son married Lois’s niece. The Tuttles did not merely keep their Massachusetts ties; they married back into the same house twice. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐ A THIRD SOURCE, AND IT NAMES HER FATHER [TUTTLE-38]: “There, in Sunderland, Mass., Gershom Tuttle married, Oct. 17, 1737, LOIS ALLIS (from Sunderland Town records, as given in the History of Sunderland). She was the daughter of WILLIAM ALLIS of Sunderland” — confirming node 1538 as her father from outside the Allis genealogy and outside the FamilySearch index. ⭐ The same compiler notes “the marriage of Gershom Tuttle is NOT GIVEN in the Tuttle genealogy, and it took considerable intuitive research to discover it” — someone else hit the same blank dash in the 1883 book and had to go round it the same way we did. ⭐⭐ NEW LEAD ON WHERE SHE WENT: the History of Sunderland prints the town records — look there for her death, and for any later Tuttle entries. ——— Earlier note: ⛔⛔ WHERE SHE IS NOT — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-35]. Her husband died at Claremont, New Hampshire in 1777 and is buried there. Spofford’s 1896 transcription of every ancient cemetery in that town contains only three Tuttles, and none of them is his wife. Lois is not buried beside him. ⭐ THE TWO LIVE POSSIBILITIES: ① she died in Connecticut before the family moved north — her last recorded child is Huldah, 1749, and she would have been 41; or ② she outlived him elsewhere. ⭐⭐ AND THERE IS A HINT ON HIS MEMORIAL: a daughter LOIS, d. 1769 — a girl named for her mother, dying eight years before her father. Look for BOTH Loises in Connecticut, and in the Bristol/Farmington burying grounds. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ CONFIRMED AND UPGRADED part → ok, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-18]: the Genealogy of William Allis of Hatfield (1919) records at entry 66, from the bride’s side and with no interest in the Tuttle line at all: “66. Lois, b. Jan. 13, 1708; married GERSHOM TUTTLE on October 17, 1737.” Two independent sources now record this marriage. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED hyp → part, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-16]: “Lois Allis, christened 13 January 1707[/8], HATFIELD, Hampshire, Massachusetts — parents WM. ALLIS and ELIZABETH”, agreed by four independent index entries. (The 1707/1708 split is Old Style dating, not a conflict — the year began 25 March.) ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ And her family MOVED FROM HATFIELD TO SUNDERLAND between Dec 1710 and Oct 1713 — her brother Zebediah was born at Sunderland in 1713. So the 1737 Sunderland marriage is not a coincidence of place: Sunderland was the Allis family’s own town, and a bride marries in her father’s parish. ⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026. “Gershom Tuttle and LOIS ALLIS — marriage 17 October 1737 — Sunderland, Hampshire, Massachusetts Bay” (ark 1:1:FC3B-2FM). Ten months and five days before the Farmington birth. ⭐⭐⭐ The corroboration that makes this more than a name-match: the SON married Tabitha Mitchell at DEERFIELD, Massachusetts in 1757 — and Deerfield and Sunderland are six miles apart. Father marries at Sunderland, son marries at Deerfield: that is a family tie to one small stretch of the Connecticut River valley, in two generations.

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ HYPOTHESIS, not proof. The marriage does not say the couple removed to Farmington, and the Farmington birth does not name a mother. What settles it: any record naming “Gershom Tuttle AND LOIS HIS WIFE” together — a Farmington deed, a Hartford probate, or a church admission. Do not upgrade to ‘ok’ until that is in hand. ⚠ Watch this seam: Lois was about 30 at marriage and 41 at her last recorded child — possible, but late. If a Farmington record ever names a different wife, this is where the reconstruction fails.

Joseph Mitchell (b. 1705, a weaver; Deerfield → Sunderland → Ashfield; d. 16 Sept 1794)

Ahnentafel #770 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father (9 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Joseph Mitchell was born in 1705 and worked as a weaver, living successively at Deerfield, Sunderland and Ashfield, Massachusetts, before his death on 16 September 1794. He married Mary Allis on 2 November 1726, and among their children was a daughter, Tabitha, who would go on to marry Gershom Tuttle II.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HIS FATHER IS NAMED AND THE MAYFLOWER QUESTION IS ANSWERED — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-42]. Sheldon, Deerfield vol. 2, image 505: “4, Joseph, s. of MICHAEL (1), b. 1705; WEAVER; lived a few yrs. in Sund.; was at Ash[field] 1769; per. d. there Sept. 16, 1794. He m. Nov. 2, 1726, MARY ALLIS of Sund.; she d. Nov. 8, 1783, a. 77.” A weaver, not a farmer — and Mary Allis’s death is now dated too: 8 November 1783. ⛔⛔ AND HIS FATHER IS NOT A PLYMOUTH MITCHELL. See node 1540 — the Mayflower door is shut. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-41] — TABITHA MITCHELL’S FATHER, from the town record. History of the Town of Sunderland, image 567: “MITCHELL, Joseph, m. Nov. 2, 1726, MARY, dau. WILLIAM ALLIS.” ⭐ Sheldon’s Deerfield gives the family: son Joseph, b. 3 Mar 1726-7 AT SUNDERLAND — four months after the wedding — then TABITHA, b. 9 Feb 1736/7, and daughters Hannah (m. Zadock King 1751), Mary (m. 1753, of Ashfield), Thankful and Lydia, each styled “dau. Joseph Mitchell”. The family moved Sunderland → Deerfield.

Outstanding: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HIS OWN ORIGIN IS THE LAST OPEN QUESTION ON THE MAYFLOWER FILE. The Sunderland book gives his marriage but not where he came from. ⛔ Tabitha’s MOTHER’S line is now known and is Allis — Massachusetts Bay, 1640, not Plymouth. So the entire Mayflower question rests on this one man. ⭐ WHY IT IS STILL WORTH ASKING: the Plymouth Mitchells descend from EXPERIENCE MITCHELL (the Anne, 1623), who married JANE COOKE, daughter of FRANCIS COOKE, Mayflower 1620 — and Sheldon records “Experience Mitchell of Ashfield” marrying a FULLER in 1774, one generation on, in this same valley. “Experience” is the name that gets carried. ⚠⚠ A NAME IS NOT A DESCENT. ⭐ ROUTES: Sheldon’s MITCHELL head-of-family paragraph (image 505, line-broken); the Sunderland proprietors’ and town records before 1726; and a SAMUEL Mitchell who had a Sunderland land grant in 1737 “if he abide five years” — probably kin.

Mary Allis (b. 18 Feb 1705, Hatfield; m. 2 Nov 1726 – d. 8 Nov 1783, aged 77)

Ahnentafel #771 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother (9 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Mary Allis was born 18 February 1705 in Hatfield, Massachusetts, and married Joseph Mitchell on 2 November 1726. She died 8 November 1783. She was the elder sister of Lois Allis, and in a fitting turn of family history, Mary's daughter Tabitha and Lois's son Gershom later married one another — making their shared Allis parents ancestors of this family twice over.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ HER DEATH, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-42]: Sheldon gives “she d. Nov. 8, 1783, a. 77” — which is the first death date we have for either Allis sister, and her own sister Lois still has none. ⚠ Small discrepancy logged: born 18 Feb 1705 she would have been 78 in November 1783, not 77. A one-year age on a death notice is the least reliable figure in the record; the birth entry is preferred. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-41] — AND SHE IS LOIS ALLIS’S ELDER SISTER. History of the Town of Sunderland, image 355, in William Allis’s family: “Ch.: MARY, b. Feb. 18, 1705; m. Joseph Mitchell.” and three lines later “LOIS, b. Jan. 13, 1708; m. Oct. 17, 1737, Gershom Tuttle.” Two sisters, three years apart. Mary’s daughter Tabitha married Lois’s son Gershom in 1757. ⭐ Her birth of 18 February 1705 matches the 1919 Allis genealogy’s “Mary 1705” [TUTTLE-16] exactly, from a separate book and the town’s own records.

Outstanding: ⭐⭐ PEDIGREE COLLAPSE: her parents are WILLIAM ALLIS (node 1538) and ELIZABETH DAVIS (node 1539), who are already on this chart as the parents of Lois. They are not duplicated here — the same couple simply occupies two positions, which is what a first-cousin marriage does to a pedigree. ⚠ Her death is not recorded here. The Sunderland and Deerfield registers are the place to look.

10 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-79/-VERDICT]: her parentage re-verified with a second compilation (the 1919 Allis genealogy, agreeing to the day). Her parents’ own ancestry continues — fully charted — under their other occurrence: 1538/1539 → 3076/3077 → 6152/6153 (and 3078/3079 on the Davis side). The trail does not stop here; it runs through her sister Lois’s slot.

John Adam Roush (1711, the Palatinate – 19 Oct 1786, Shenandoah Co., Virginia)

Ahnentafel #960 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (9 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

John Adam Roush was born in 1711 in the Palatinate region of Germany and died 19 October 1786 in Shenandoah County, Virginia. He is recognized by the Daughters of the American Revolution as a patriot for furnishing supplies during the Revolutionary War, and he was the immigrant ancestor from whom the American Roush family descends. He probably was the father of sons John, George, Jonas, Henry, Jacob, Philip and Daniel, a connection the family's own history has long carried, though it has not yet been confirmed by a document in his own hand, such as a will. In keeping with the family's practice of tracing each line back only to its immigrant ancestor, research on him stops at his arrival in America, without following the family further back into Germany.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE IMMIGRANT — AND HE IS HIMSELF A RECOGNISED PATRIOT [ROUSH-54, 7 August 2026].

DAR Ancestor # A098982 — Service: VIRGINIA, PATRIOTIC SERVICE: “FURNISHED SUPPLIES.” Source: Library of Virginia, APA 241, Impressed Property Claims Certificates 1780–1783, Shenandoah County, #2453. Born 1711 in the Palatinate; died 19 October 1786, Shenandoah County, Virginia. This is Johann Adam Rausch, the man every American Roush of this family descends from.

⚠⚠ GRADED HYPOTHESIS, AND THE REASON MATTERS. That Jonas and George were his sons is the settled family tradition and is repeated everywhere — but this project has not yet read a record that says so, and tradition is not record. The DAR recognises all three men separately; it does not, on the pages read, tie these two to that father.

⭐ If it is proved, it produces a real pedigree collapse — John Roush and Regina Roush were cousins, so this one man would occupy two positions on Alec’s chart at once, and anything found about him would count twice.

⭐⭐ Per Alec’s standing rule, this is where the line stops. He is the immigrant. The Palatinate is not to be researched until the American tree is finished.

Outstanding: his will or estate, Shenandoah County, 1786 — the one document that would name his sons in his own words and move this node and the two above it in a single stroke.

⭐ UPGRADED hyp → part, 8 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [ROUSH-60/-VERDICT]: the 1928 book ties George AND Jonas to John & Susannah in four internal places (incl. the Old Pine Church 1786 baptismal-sponsor entry); Daniel Roush's 1832 will and George's pension S18579 (read in full) document the sibling set — John (Capt.), George, Jonas, Henry, Jacob, Philip, Daniel — though no document yet names the father outside the book. 'ok' refused pending John Adam's own 1786 Shenandoah probate; the 1773 Mill Creek grant (400 ac., him + wife Susannah) is the named anchor document.

Susannah Roush (d. about 1796, Shenandoah Co., Virginia; maiden name unknown)

Ahnentafel #961 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (9 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Susannah was the wife of John Adam Roush, the immigrant. She is named in the recital of an original land grant of 1773 on Mill Creek, "to him and his wife Susannah," and appears as a baptismal sponsor at their church in 1770 and 1786. She probably died around 1796 in Shenandoah County, Virginia, and lies in an unmarked grave at Old Pine Church; her own maiden name has not been recovered.

Evidence

⭐ ADDED at PROBABLE, 10 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [ROUSH-67/-VERDICT]. The immigrant’s wife: named in the 1773 Mill Creek grant recital (“to him and his wife Susannah”); baptismal sponsor 1770 and 5 Jan 1786; and — recovered by the adversary — **“Susannah Roush, widow,” in the Old Pine communicant rolls 1788–1796, then never again**: d. ~1796, her grave unmarked beside John Adam’s. Maiden name: the tree-circulating “Sehler/Schlern” stays OFF pending any source. One ultimate source (the 1928 book) = Probable.

Michael Zerckel (Zerkel/Zirkle) (of Shenandoah Co., VA; fourteen children; son of Ludwig Zerkel (lead))

Ahnentafel #962 · his mother’s mother’s mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father (9 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Michael Zerckel, whose name also appears as Zerkel or Zirkle, lived in Shenandoah County, Virginia, and was the father of fourteen children. Among them was Catharine, probably the eldest of the fourteen and probably the woman who became George Roush's wife. He is believed to have been a son of a man named Ludwig Zerkel, though that generation is only a lead so far. Another daughter, Mary, married Henry Nease in 1791, drawing the Zerkel family into a further branch of the wider family tree.

Evidence

⭐ ADDED at PROBABLE, 10 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [ROUSH-68/-VERDICT]: three in-book threads name his daughter Catharine as George Roush’s wife — and the adversary’s arithmetic proved her the OLDEST of his fourteen (b. ~1760–62; a married sponsor by Oct 1780; the cited “youngest child” passage is the compiler’s own slip = self-correction #28). Children incl. Mary (m. Henry Nease 1791 — another strand of the braid), Michael Jr. (b. 15 Apr 1772), Henry (→ Mason Co.). Single ultimate source = Probable.

Outstanding: the Old Pine/Solomon’s register (record-level baptisms); the Ludwig Zerkel generation (lead only); DAR GRS Zerkel search.

John Adam Roush (1711, the Palatinate – 19 Oct 1786, Shenandoah Co., Virginia)

John Adam Roush was born in 1711 in the Palatinate region of Germany and died 19 October 1786 in Shenandoah County, Virginia.
