Dea. Timothy Tuttle (b. c.1682 New Haven – d. 1756; m.(1) Thankful Doolittle 2 Nov 1706, Wallingford)

Ahnentafel #1536 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (10 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Deacon Timothy Tuttle was born about 1682 in New Haven, Connecticut, and moved with his family to Wallingford as a small child. He later cleared land with his own hands a mile east of the center of what became Cheshire, Connecticut, and built a house there said to be among the town's earliest frame houses. He married Thankful Doolittle on 2 November 1706 at Wallingford; after her death he remarried three more times, the last time in 1749, and he died in 1756.

A respected figure in his community, he served as sergeant, church moderator and deacon. In 1736 he deeded his own share of land in a Massachusetts frontier township to his son Gershom, giving it, as the deed itself records, out of "love, good will and fatherly affection."

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE 1736 DEED, READ AT THE PAGE [TUTTLE-37] — ark 3:1:3QS7-89ZD-JW67, image 445: “I Timothy Tuttle of Wallingford … for love, good will and FATHERLY AFFECTION … to my son Gershom Tuttle … all my Right of land in said [Carleton] … on my said RIGHT to be laid out and surveyed.” ⭐⭐ A NEW FACT ABOUT HIM: the Cheshire deacon was a PROPRIETOR IN A MASSACHUSETTS FRONTIER TOWNSHIP. He held a share in one of the new Hampshire County grants and signed the whole of it over to his second son — which is how a Connecticut family ends up marrying into the Connecticut Valley of Massachusetts a year later. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HE NAMES GERSHOM AS HIS SON IN A 1736 DEED [TUTTLE-36] — “I TIMOTHY TUTTLE OF WALLINGFORD … to my SON GERSHOM TUTTLE …”, Springfield (Hampshire Co.), Massachusetts land records, 1736, nine matched passages. With this, EVERY parent-to-child link in the Tuttle line from Alec back to the immigrant rests on a record, not on a book. ⭐ It is also the second father-to-son conveyance in this family we have found: his own father Simon deeded HIM 9½ acres on 10 July 1711. Three generations, two deeds, one habit. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ BEACH’S HISTORY OF CHESHIRE CORROBORATES THE WHOLE OF HIS LIFE, FROM THE TOWN’S OWN PAPERS [TUTTLE-35]: “Timothy Tuttle erected a house about 1706, at the end of the road to the south side of Broad Swamp” (image 100) — the year he married Thankful Doolittle. He built the house and took the wife in the same season. The 19 December 1715 petition of the farmers on the west side of the river, printed in full (image 73): “…ye Bounds we Desire assigned is west from ye rever as high as Timothy tuttle & timothy Beach’s…” — and Beach adds (image 74) “Timothy Tuttle lived near the west end of the road ‘to ye south of Broad Swamp’”, about four miles west of the river and three miles north of Timothy Beach. 16 September 1723 — the first meeting of the New Cheshire society — “Timothy Tuttle was chosen MODERATOR” (image 83). On the committee “to manage the affair of the ordination” with Sergt. John Hotchkiss and Stephen Hotchkiss (image 92). 1 December 1741, “decon Timothy Tuttle” on the committee to petition for town privileges (image 118). ⭐ And his fourth marriage from the same register (image 579): “Dea. Timothy Tuttle m. Sarah Humerston June 28, 1749” — matching the 1883 book’s Sarah (Atwater) Humiston to the day, from an independent transcription. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HIS FIRST MARRIAGE, AND THE 1682 BIRTH, CONFIRMED FROM A THIRD DIRECTION — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-34]. “On 02 Nov 1706 she married Timothy Tuttle in Wallingford, CT and raised a large family. Timothy was the son of Simon and ABIGAIL BEACH Tuttle.” — Find a Grave memorial 139363422 for Thankful Doolittle Tuttle, now node 1537. A man marrying in November 1706 is 24 on the 1682 reading and 54 on the book’s printed 1652. The marriage settles it. ⭐ Find a Grave and WikiTree independently give him 1682–1756, inside the bracket we had derived (dead between June 1749 and May 1761). ⭐ It also supplies his mother’s surname: ABIGAIL BEACH — see node 3073. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED part → ok [TUTTLE-32] — his father deeded him land and the deed is dated. “July 10, 1711, he gives to son, TIMOTHY, the land, 9½ acres, ‘I bought of Roger Tyler’” — Simon Tuttle to Timothy Tuttle, Wallingford, 10 July 1711. A father-to-son conveyance is the classic proof of descent in New England, and it is what was missing. ⭐ And Simon’s own deed sequence dates the family’s move: “of New Haven” to 1683, “of Wallingford” from 1685 — which matches “rem. with his father’s family when about 3 years of age” and puts Timothy’s birth at c.1682, not 1652. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — GENERATION 12 ON THE TUTTLE SIDE, and the generation that broke the wall [TUTTLE-30]. Entry 92 of the 1883 Tuttle genealogy, image 574: “Timothy Tuttle, b. 1652; prob. in N. Haven; rem. with his father’s family when about 3 years of age to WALLINGFORD, Conn. In early life he explored the country to the westward of W. and selected land for a farm, which he cleared with his own hands … working through the week as it was too far to go home every night and returning only on Saturdays, guided by a line of blazed trees he had marked through the woods. The spot is about a mile east of the central part of the village of CHESHIRE. … Here he afterwards erected the first frame house in the town of Cheshire (according to family tradition, but Joseph P. Beach … accords that honor to the Hitchcock house … ‘the Tuttle house may have been the second’). … under its roof were born children, grand children, great gr. chil. and great great gr. chil. of the builder; it was standing until a few years since, when it was burned by an incendiary, being at the time unoccupied.” ⭐ A PUBLIC MAN: he is “Sergeant Tuttle” in the West Wallingford (Cheshire) society records — school-house committee with Matthew Bellamy and Isaac Moss 17 Nov 1729, society MODERATOR 8 Nov 1732, society affairs again 1 Nov 1736 — and “Dea. Timothy Tuttle of Cheshire” after. In 1715 the Wallingford school-district division runs “as far as west of the river, as high as Timothy Tuttle’s and Timothy Beach’s.” ⭐ HIS CHILDREN, numbered exactly as the ladder predicted: 924 Mary b. 3 Oct 1712 (m. Miles Hull of Derby 1729); 925 GERSHOM b. 11 Aug 1714 — OURS; 926 Timothy b. 4 Dec 1716 (rem. to Goshen, d. 23 Oct 1760); 928 SIMON, b. 12 June 1721 — “rec. a deed of land in Cambridge Parish in Southington, Conn., from his father, Timothy Tuttle; he set. in Prospect; m. Nov. 20, 1746, by Rev. Jeremiah Curtis of Southington, who omitted the name of the bride in his record”. ⚠⚠ PARTLY WITHDRAWN THE SAME NIGHT (self-correction #15): I wrote last turn that this is the Simon Tuttle of the 1742 New Cambridge petition [TUTTLE-20]. The book says his land lay in “Cambridge Parish in SOUTHINGTON”, not New Cambridge/Bristol — a different parish of Farmington. He would have been 21 in 1742, so it remains possible and is still the best candidate, but it is a hypothesis again, not an identification. He married four times; the fourth, 28 June 1749, was Sarah (Atwater) Humiston, b. 6 Apr 1693, widow of James Humiston, who “d. his wid. May 28, 1761” — so he died between June 1749 and May 1761.

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ THE PRINTED BIRTH YEAR 1652 CANNOT STAND — FOUR OF THE BOOK’S OWN FACTS REFUTE IT [TUTTLE-30]: ① his father Simon was baptised 28 March 1647 and cannot have a son in 1652; ② the same entry says the family “probably continued in New Haven until after the death of his mother, Dec., 1684”, and Timothy moved with them “when about 3 years of age” → born c.1681–82; ③ his children are born 1712, 1714, 1716 — a father of 30–34 on the 1682 reading, of 60–64 on the 1652 one; ④ he married a fourth wife in 1749 — age 67 on the 1682 reading, age 97 on the 1652 one. Entered here as b. c.1682, with the correction STATED rather than silently made. ⚠ STILL TO DO: read the PAGE IMAGE of 574 to learn whether the error is the book’s or the scan’s. ⚠ ‘part’ on purpose — no record of him has been read yet, only a book. ⭐ THE RECORDS THAT WOULD MOVE HIM TO ‘ok’: the Cheshire church records, which the book itself cites (“births and baptisms copied from Cheshire chh. rec.”) — they should carry his four marriages and the births of 1712–1716; and Wallingford land and probate, including the deed to son Simon.

Thankful Doolittle (b. 5 Apr 1688 Wallingford – m. 2 Nov 1706 – d. 23 Nov 1728, aged 40)

Ahnentafel #1537 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (10 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Thankful Doolittle was born 5 April 1688 in Wallingford, Connecticut, the daughter of Sergeant John Doolittle and Mary Peck. She married Timothy Tuttle at Wallingford on 2 November 1706 and bore children across the following two decades, including a son, Gershom, before her death on 23 November 1728 at the age of forty, leaving a youngest child of only five. Her brother, the Reverend Benjamin Doolittle, was the minister at Northfield, Massachusetts, and later gave shelter to her son Gershom when he moved north as a young man.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ CONFIRMED AS GERSHOM’S MOTHER FROM A THIRD DIRECTION [TUTTLE-38] — and by a relationship, not a restatement. Miscellaneous Notes on New England Families: “Gershom Tuttle was the son of Timothy Tuttle and his wife THANKFUL DOOLITTLE”, and when Gershom went north he stayed at Northfield “where HIS UNCLE the Rev. Benjamin Doolittle was the Pastor of the church.” Benjamin Doolittle (1695–1748) is her brother. A man’s uncle is his mother’s brother — the sentence carries the parentage inside it. ⭐ And her family is why the Tuttles knew that country at all. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED part → ok WITHIN THE HOUR — THE MARRIAGE IS IN A PUBLISHED RECORD TRANSCRIPTION [TUTTLE-35]. Joseph Perkins Beach, History of Cheshire, Connecticut, from 1649 to 1840, image 578, printing the town’s own marriage register: “Timothy Tuttle m. Thankfull Doolittle November 2, 1706.” This is no longer a Find a Grave assertion — it is the record, in the standard history of the town, transcribed from the register. ⭐ AND BEACH IS THE RIGHT SOURCE BY NAME: the 1883 Tuttle genealogy cites “Joseph P. Beach, Esq., of Cheshire” as its authority on this family. We have gone to the man the genealogy was quoting. ⭐⭐⭐ AND SHE APPEARS ALIVE IN THE CHURCH BOOK: the New Cheshire admissions list, image 339, records “March, 1725 — WIFE OF TIMOTHY TUTTLE” admitted to the church — three years before she died. Her husband is admitted separately in January 1738-9, ten years after her death. ⭐ Beach also has Timothy erecting his house about 1706 — the year he married her. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE EMPTY NODE HAS A NAME — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-34] — and the prediction written into it came true. THANKFUL DOOLITTLE, b. 5 April 1688, Wallingford, Connecticut; m. Timothy Tuttle at Wallingford, 2 November 1706; d. 23 November 1728, Wallingford, aged 40; burial unknown. Daughter of Sgt. John Doolittle (1655–1746) and Mary Peck (1666–1710). Find a Grave memorial 139363422. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE CHRONOLOGY IS EXACT, AND IT WAS PREDICTED. This node was created empty a few hours earlier with the reasoning: “two of Timothy’s four wives are named — Mary Peck m.1729 and Sarah Atwater m.1749 — and BOTH married him after the children of 1712–1721 were born, so the mother is one of the first two wives and the book does not name her.” Thankful died 23 November 1728. Timothy remarried in 1729. THIRTEEN MONTHS. The deduction was right, and she sits exactly in the gap it pointed at. ⭐ She married at 18 and bore children across at least seventeen years — Ephraim 1710, Mary 1712, GERSHOM 1714, Timothy 1716, Simon 1721, Moses 1723 — and died at forty, with the youngest five years old. ⭐ Her 1706 marriage is also the cleanest proof yet that her husband was born c.1682 and not 1652.

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ ‘part’ — a Find a Grave memorial is a COMPILED source and no record has been read. ⭐ But it is not a bare assertion: it carries a specific date, a specific place, named parents and a marriage date, and it lands inside a gap identified independently before it was found. That is corroboration, not coincidence. ⭐ WHAT WOULD MAKE IT ‘ok’: the Wallingford marriage entry of 2 Nov 1706 and the Wallingford death entry of 23 Nov 1728; the 1883 book itself cites “births and baptisms copied from Cheshire chh. rec.” for this family. ⚠⚠ WATCH A REAL TRAP HERE: Thankful’s MOTHER was Mary Peck (1666–1710) — and Timothy’s THIRD wife was also a Mary Peck, widow of Stephen Rowe, m. 1729. Two different women of the same name in the same town. Do not let a compiled source merge them.

William Allis (b. 19 Oct 1680 Hatfield – d. 20 Feb 1763 Montague, Mass.)

Ahnentafel #1538 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father (10 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

William Allis was born 19 October 1680 in Hatfield, Massachusetts, and married Elizabeth Davis of Northampton around 1703 or 1704. The family later moved from Hatfield to Sunderland and then to Montague, Massachusetts, where William died 20 February 1763. Two of his daughters, Lois and Mary, each married into the same family a generation apart — Lois to Gershom Tuttle I, and years later Mary's own daughter to Lois's son — so William Allis stands as an ancestor of this family twice over.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HE IS AN ANCESTOR OF ALEC TWICE OVER [TUTTLE-41]. History of the Town of Sunderland, image 355, gives his children including MARY, b. 18 Feb 1705, who married JOSEPH MITCHELL, and LOIS, b. 13 Jan 1708, who married GERSHOM TUTTLE. Lois is node 769. Mary is node 771. Their children married each other in 1757. So this man descends to Alec down TWO lines — through Lois and again through Mary — and the pedigree collapses on him. ⭐ That is also why his own generation matters twice as much as it looked. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED part → ok, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-32]. Boltwood’s Hadley genealogies independently list him in Samuel Allis’s child list as “William, b. Oct. 19, 1680” — the same day and year the 1919 Allis genealogy gives, from a separate compilation. Add the four agreeing FamilySearch primary indexes naming “Wm. Allis and Elizabeth” as the parents of Lois at her Hatfield baptism of 13 Jan 1707/8 [TUTTLE-16], and the Montague town register naming him as “Mr. William Allis” alive there in May 1758 [TUTTLE-22], and both ends of this man are documented. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ DATED 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-18]: “24. WILLIAM ALLIS was born in Hatfield, Oct. 19, 1680, and married Elizabeth Davis of Northampton in 1703-4. He died in Montague, Feb. 20, 1763.” ⭐ The book’s list of his five children matches the FamilySearch primary indexes exactly (one day’s discrepancy on Zebediah), which is what earns it credit. ⭐⭐⭐⭐ And the MONTAGUE TOWN REGISTER now names him [TUTTLE-22]: the 1758 death entry for his wife styles him “MR. William Allis” — a title of standing in a New England register — and puts him alive at Montague in May 1758. ⚠⚠⚠ The same register records that his son ELIPHALET and wife Mary buried FOUR CHILDREN IN NINE DAYS in January 1759 — Mary (7th), Jonathan (10th), Samuel (12th), David (15th). His son ZEBADIAH is there too, with wife Mary and daughters. So the whole family moved Sunderland → Montague together, and that is now documented rather than inferred. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — GENERATION 11, the first this project has ever reached. Named as father on Lois Allis’s Hatfield baptism of 13 Jan 1707/8. His family, from the indexed births: Mary 1705 (Hatfield), Lois 1707/8 (Hatfield), Eliphalet 1710 (Hatfield), Zebediah 1713 (Sunderland), Elizabeth 1716 (Sunderland) — so he removed from Hatfield to Sunderland between December 1710 and October 1713 [TUTTLE-16].

Outstanding: ⛔ His own birth is NOT in the FamilySearch index — searched William Allis b.1665×1690 across every collection; every result was English. Generation 12 cannot be reached by index. Routes: Hampshire Co. (Northampton) PROBATE first — a will or distribution would name his father AND give Elizabeth’s maiden name; then Hatfield/Sunderland town records as IMAGES; then Hadley/Hatfield land. ⚠⚠ DO NOT attach him to the immigrant William Allis (d.1678) of Braintree/Hadley/Hatfield without a record. The generation gap is about right, which is exactly what makes it dangerous.

Elizabeth Davis (of Northampton; m. 1703-4 – d. 1 May 1758, Montague, Mass.)

Ahnentafel #1539 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother (10 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Elizabeth Davis was of Northampton, Massachusetts, and married William Allis around 1703 or 1704. She died 1 May 1758 in Montague, Massachusetts, where the town register recorded her as "Elizabeth Allis wife to Mr. William Allis." Her maiden name of Davis is probably correct, resting as it does on more than one compiled family history, though it has not yet been confirmed by an original record from her own youth.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ A SECOND WITNESS TO THE SURNAME, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-39]: Miscellaneous Notes on New England Families calls Lois “the daughter of WILLIAM ALLIS, of Sunderland–Montague, and his wife ELIZABETH DAVIS.” ⭐ Note the compiler writes “Sunderland–Montague” as one place-pair — independently matching the family’s move that TUTTLE-22 documented from the Montague town register. ⚠⚠ HELD AT ‘Probable’ ON PURPOSE, AND HERE IS THE HONEST REASON: this manuscript may simply be repeating the 1919 Allis genealogy, which is our other source for the name Davis. Two sources that share an ancestor are one source. Until the dependency is tested — or a Northampton record names her — the surname stays single-sourced. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HER DEATH IS NOW ON A PRIMARY RECORD [TUTTLE-22]. The Montague town register itself: “Allis. May first 1758, ELIZABETH ALLIS WIFE TO MR. WILLIAM ALLIS, died in Montague.” Date, place and the husband’s given name — confirming the 1919 book exactly, from a town record rather than a published genealogy. ⚠ Her maiden name DAVIS still rests on the book ALONE and is NOT upgraded — the register calls her only “Elizabeth Allis wife to Mr. William Allis.” ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HER MAIDEN NAME FOUND, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-18]: DAVIS, of NORTHAMPTON. “married Elizabeth Davis of Northampton in 1703-4 … Elizabeth, his wife, died May 1, 1758.” ⭐ This also explains a loose end: one of the four index entries for Lois’s birth gives the place as Northampton rather than Hatfield — it is the mother’s town. ⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — GENERATION 11. Named as the mother on all five of the Allis children’s records at Hatfield and Sunderland, 1705–1716, including Lois’s baptism [TUTTLE-16].

Outstanding: ⚠ Her MAIDEN NAME is unknown — the baptismal entries give only “Elizabeth.” A marriage search for William Allis × Elizabeth c.1685–1710 drowned in English “Ellis” matches. ⭐ The Hampshire Co. probate of her husband, or a Hatfield marriage record read from the image, is the way in. ⭐⭐ Her husband is now also documented by a LAND RECORD: a Sunderland deed from William Allis to Zebadiah Allis — father to son — sealed by William Allis [TUTTLE-17]. ⭐⭐⭐ NEXT: her husband’s own death entry, 20 Feb 1763 — the Montague register’s year list INCLUDES 1763, so it is on that film. Find it.

Michael Mitchell (of Wallingford, Conn. 1695-6 and 1706; then Deerfield)

Ahnentafel #1540 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s father (10 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Michael Mitchell was living in Wallingford, Connecticut, in 1695-6 and again in 1706, and later moved to Deerfield, Massachusetts. He married Sarah Catlin, daughter of John Catlin, and his family "escaped unharmed" the devastating raid on Deerfield of 29 February 1704 — a night that brought death and captivity to many of his wife's own relatives. His son Joseph became a weaver and was the father of Tabitha Mitchell, who married into the Tuttle family in 1757.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-42] — and he answers Alec’s Mayflower question. Sheldon, Deerfield vol. 2, image 504, the head of the MITCHELL genealogy: “1, MITCHELL, Michael; was in WALLINGFORD, CT., 1695-6, and again in 1706; HIS WHOLE FAMILY ESCAPED UNHARMED 1704. He m. SARAH, dau. JOHN CATLIN; she d. Dec. 30, 1736.” ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HE WAS A WALLINGFORD MAN — THE TUTTLES’ OWN TOWN. Dea. Timothy Tuttle was farming west of the river at Wallingford in exactly those years, and Gershom Tuttle I was born there in 1714. So the Mitchells and the Tuttles were Wallingford neighbours two generations before their grandchildren married at Deerfield in 1757. That marriage was not a chance meeting a hundred and ten miles from home — it was two Wallingford families who had both gone north, marrying each other. ⚠⚠⚠ AND THE 1704 LINE STOPS YOU COLD. “His whole family escaped unharmed 1704” means the Deerfield raid of 29 February 1704 — the same raid that killed ALICE, wife of Samuel Allis (node 3077), and her son Samuel, and carried her daughter Mary to Canada [TUTTLE-19/31/32]. Two of Alec’s ancestral families were in Deerfield that night. One lost a mother and a son. The other escaped unharmed. Fifty-three years later their descendants married.

Outstanding: ⛔⛔ THIS CLOSES THE MAYFLOWER DOOR [MAYFLOWER-01]. The one promising line ran through the Mitchell surname, on the hope that this family descended from EXPERIENCE MITCHELL of Plymouth (the Anne, 1623), who married Jane Cooke, daughter of Francis Cooke of the Mayflower. It does not. Michael Mitchell is a Wallingford, Connecticut and Deerfield man who married Sarah, daughter of JOHN CATLIN of Deerfield. No Bridgewater, no Plymouth, no Middleborough. The door is shut, and it is shut with a document rather than an absence. ⭐ WHAT IS STILL OPEN AND WORTH HAVING: Michael’s own origin — where a Mitchell in Wallingford in 1695 came from; and the CATLIN family (node 1541), who are a Deerfield family of the raid generation. ⚠ Sheldon’s numbering gives him sons William b. 1697, Michael b. 8 Mar 1700-1 (a soldier in Father Rasle’s war, later of Wethersfield, Conn.), JOSEPH b. 1705 — ours — and Samuel b. 7 Mar 1712, who is very probably the Samuel Mitchell granted Sunderland land in 1737.

10 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-82/-VERDICT]: the origin question worked to a seven-source convergent silence (Jacobus’s whole entry: “Michael of W; m Sarah -”; Savage silent; Thomas-the-miller EXCLUDED by his own 1660 probate — widow + daughter, no son; both colony-record series empty). Immigrant-vs-NE-born stays UNRESOLVED — so per house convention this node is the line’s effective stop; no parent slots opened. He held Wallingford PROPRIETOR status (1892 history: “Timothy Merriam, Michael Mitchell, William Hough…” casting lots). Break-it-open tests: his first Wallingford deed (FS full-text); his probate; Jordan-Solari 1988 (library loan).

Sarah Catlin (m. Michael Mitchell about 1694 – d. 30 Dec 1736; survived the 1704 raid)

Ahnentafel #1541 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s mother (10 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Sarah Catlin was the daughter of John Catlin of Deerfield, Massachusetts, and married Michael Mitchell around 1694. She died 30 December 1736, just two months before the birth of her granddaughter Tabitha. Having married ten years before the devastating Deerfield raid of 29 February 1704, she was living in her own household, which came through unharmed that night, even as her birth family was shattered — her father and a brother killed, another brother killed in the fighting that followed, and a brother and several sisters carried captive toward Canada, two of the sisters dying along the way.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐ HER MARRIAGE IS DATED, AND IT IS WHY SHE LIVED [TUTTLE-44]. Sheldon’s CATLIN child list, image 369: “Sarah, —; m. abt. 1694, Michael [Mitchell].” She married ten years before the raid and was in her own household on the night her father’s was burned with him inside it. Her father and one brother died in the fire, another brother was killed in the Meadow Fight, and a brother and three sisters were carried to Canada. Sarah is the reason this line reaches Alec at all. ——— Earlier note: ⚠⚠⚠ HER FAMILY WAS DESTROYED IN THE RAID HER HUSBAND’S FAMILY SURVIVED — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-43]. Sheldon, image 319: “Mary, bap. June 23, 1644; m. Sept. 23, 1662, JOHN CATLIN, with whom she rem. fr. NEWARK to Dfd. abt. 1683; THEIR LARGE FAMILY WERE NEARLY ALL DESTROYED FEB. 29, 1704.” Two of her sisters are named and their deaths are recorded: “Elizabeth, dau. John Catlin; she was cap. 1704 and k. on the march to Canada” (wife of James Corse, image 397) and “Mary, dau. John Catlin; she was cap. and k. on the march to Can., Mar. 9, 1704” (wife of Thomas Wells, image 431, whose own household on lot No. 30 was “all k. or cap. 1704”). ⚠⚠ AND SET THAT BESIDE HER HUSBAND’S ENTRY. Michael Mitchell’s reads “his whole family escaped unharmed 1704” [node 1540]. Sarah Catlin was married to him by then — her eldest was born 1697. So on the same night her own household came through untouched and her father’s was nearly wiped out. ⭐ She died 30 December 1736 — two months before her granddaughter Tabitha was born. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-42]. Sheldon, image 504: “He m. SARAH, dau. JOHN CATLIN; she d. Dec. 30, 1736.” A Deerfield family, and a Deerfield family of the raid generation. ⭐ She was living through the 29 February 1704 attack in which, Sheldon says, “his whole family escaped unharmed” — a sentence that only gets written because so many families around them did not.

Outstanding: ⭐ THE CATLINS ARE THE NEXT GENERATION UP AND THEY ARE WELL DOCUMENTED — Sheldon has a CATLIN genealogy and the 1704 captive and casualty lists are exhaustively studied. ⚠ DO NOT ASSUME John Catlin’s own fate from the raid’s reputation — read his entry. ⚠ Her death of 30 Dec 1736 falls two months before her granddaughter Tabitha was born.

Simon Tuttle (bap. New Haven 28 Mar 1647 – d. 16 April 1719, Wallingford, Conn.; cooper)

Ahnentafel #3072 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (11 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Simon Tuttle was baptized in New Haven on 28 March 1647, the ninth child of the immigrant William Tuttle. He trained as a cooper and lived first in New Haven, moving to Wallingford, Connecticut, about 1685, after his mother's death. Three deeds he signed over the years — in 1677-8, 1686, and 1705 — each describe land as having belonged to "my father, Mr. William Tuttle," directly recording the family connection in his own words. He prospered as a Wallingford landholder and town official, serving as gauger and packer from 1708, and in his later years gave land outright to his own sons Timothy and Daniel. Simon Tuttle died 16 April 1719; his widow, Abigail, died three years later, on 11 August 1722.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED part → ok THE SAME NIGHT, ON DEEDS [TUTTLE-32]. The 1883 book does not merely assert his parentage — it abstracts three separate conveyances in which Simon calls William Tuttle his father: 1677-8, to Benjamin Brockett, cordwainer, “a part of land my father, Mr. William Tuttle, bought of John Punderson”; 1686, to Isaac Turner, six acres “formerly belonging to my father, Mr. William Tuttle”; 1705, to cousin Joshua, “all my right in Branford division set to my father, Mr. William Tuttle”. Three deeds, three decades apart, in the grantor’s own words. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND A DEED TO HIS SON: “July 10, 1711, he gives to son, TIMOTHY, the land, 9½ acres, ‘I bought of Roger Tyler.’” (and 23 June 1715 a 14-acre house lot to son Daniel). The father-to-son conveyance is the classic proof of descent, and it links node 3072 to node 1536 by a record rather than by a numbering scheme. ⭐ A life, from the same pages: “Simon Tuttle of Wallingford, COOPER” (1685); on the New Haven proprietors’ list of 1680, drawing his share in the third division as “two heads, showing that at that date he had a wife but no children”; a 1673 right-of-way agreement signed by six Tuttle brothers, of which the original paper survived to 1883 and his signature was traced from it; Wallingford lots drawn 1686 (No. 52, 104 acres) and 1690 (No. 14); rated £79 on the 1701 Wallingford grand list; town gauger and packer from 1708-9. He died 16 April 1719; his widow ABIGAIL died 11 August 1722. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND HE SETTLES THE 1682 QUESTION INDEPENDENTLY. The deeds show him of New Haven through 1683 and “of Wallingford” from 1685 — “from these records it follows that he removed to Wall’d about 1685”. His son Timothy moved with the family “when about 3 years of age” — so Timothy was born c.1682, and the book’s own printed “1652” is refuted for the fifth time, now by land records. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — GENERATION 13 ON THE TUTTLE SIDE [TUTTLE-30]. Entry 9 of the 1883 genealogy, image 560, in full: “9. Simon Tuttle, bap. in New Haven, March 28, 1647. He appears in the list of proprietors of Wallingford in 1670, and had lot No. 13 with 8 acres of out land assigned to him. Dr. Savage (Gen. Dict.) says he did not stay long. He did not, in fact, take up his allotment and probably continued in New Haven until after the death of his mother, Dec., 1684.” ⭐⭐ THE BARE ENTRY NUMBER 9 IS THE WHOLE POINT: he is the NINTH CHILD OF WILLIAM TUTTLE, and that is why the ladder 92514 → 9251 → 925 → 92 → 9 lands on the immigrant. ⭐ He also dates the family’s move. Wallingford is laid out in 1670 and Simon takes a proprietor’s lot — but stays in New Haven until after his mother dies in December 1684. His son Timothy moved with the family “when about 3 years of age”: the two statements agree on c.1685 and together they refute the book’s own printed 1652 birth for Timothy.

Outstanding: ⚠ WHAT IS STILL OPEN: the deeds above are abstracted by the 1883 compiler, not yet read by us — they are New Haven and Wallingford land records and can be pulled. ⚠ His wife ABIGAIL has no surname (node 3073) and no marriage record; the book infers the marriage from the 1680 “two heads” entry. ⭐ ALSO WORTH HAVING: the New Haven vital records for the baptism of 28 March 1647 and for his mother’s death in December 1684; the Wallingford proprietors’ list of 1670 as an image (lot No. 13); and New Haven probate, which would name his wife and his children and so nail Timothy to him directly. ⚠ His wife is not yet recorded here — do not fill her in from a tree.

Abigail Tuttle (wife of Simon Tuttle; m. about 1679–80 – d. 11 Aug 1722; her surname is disputed)

Ahnentafel #3073 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (11 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Abigail was the wife of Simon Tuttle, probably married to him in 1679 or 1680 — the date inferred from a New Haven land record that credited him with "two heads," meaning a wife but as yet no children. She was the mother of Deacon Timothy Tuttle, born about 1682, and she outlived her husband by three years, dying 11 August 1722. Her surname is disputed: an old family tradition held that she was a Beach, but the best genealogical authorities considered and rejected that identification, and no record has ever named her family outright.

Evidence

⚠⚠⚠ DOWNGRADED IN SUBSTANCE, 7 AUGUST 2026: “BEACH” IS DISPUTED BY THE TWO BEST AUTHORITIES, AND THE NODE IS RENAMED ACCORDINGLY.

Jacobus, Families of Ancient New Haven (Tuttle section), gives Simon’s wife as “Abigail ——” with the surname deliberately left blank. And the 1883 Tuttle genealogy’s own footnote, p. 561, records the doubt at the source: “I was early informed, on what seemed good authority, that the above Abigail was the dau. of that name of Richard Beach… Joseph P. Beach, Esq., thinks that she could not have been a Beach. Richard’s dau. Abigail is not mentioned in settlement of her father’s estate. The point remains in uncertainty.”

⛔ Do not open a Beach line above this node. Her identity as Simon’s wife and Timothy’s mother is not in doubt; her surname is, and the only father ever proposed was ruled out by a Beach family authority in 1883 and by Jacobus fifty years later.

Outstanding: Simon Tuttle’s 1719 New Haven probate — the estate papers naming the widow are the only realistic route to her own family.

⭐⭐⭐⭐ A SURNAME, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-34] — but a compiled one, so the node stays ‘part’. Find a Grave memorial 139363422 states “Timothy was the son of Simon and ABIGAIL BEACH Tuttle.” ⭐ BEACH is exactly the right surname for the place — the 1883 genealogy has Timothy Beach as the Tuttles’ neighbour in the 1715 Wallingford school-district line (“as high as Timothy Tuttle’s and Timothy Beach’s”), Thos. Beach witnessing a Tuttle bond, and Joseph P. Beach, Esq., of Cheshire as its local authority on this family. The name fits the neighbourhood, which is what a real surname does and an invented one usually does not. ⚠ Still: no record has been read, and the 1883 book — which had her death to the day — gives no surname at all. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — one of the wives Alec was right to ask about [TUTTLE-32]. The 1883 genealogy closes Simon Tuttle’s entry: “He d. April 16, 1719. His wid., Abigail, d. Aug. 11, 1722.” ⭐ And it dates the marriage without a marriage record: “He is on the list of proprietors of New Haven in 1680, and received his share in the 3d division, ‘two heads,’ showing that at that date he had a wife but no children. He was probably m. in 1679 or 1680.” That is a real inference from a real document — a division list counting heads in a household — and it is worth more than most stated dates. ⚠ She is therefore the mother of Dea. Timothy Tuttle (node 1536), born c.1682.

Outstanding: ⛔ NO SURNAME. The book gives none, and there is no marriage entry. ⭐ ROUTES: New Haven marriages 1679–80; Wallingford probate 1719–1722 — Simon died in 1719 and Abigail survived him three years, so a widow’s thirds or her own estate should exist and would name her kin; and any Wallingford deed carrying her release of dower. ⚠ Do not fill her surname in from a tree.

John Doolittle (b. 14 June 1655 New Haven – d. after 1717, Wallingford, Conn.)

Ahnentafel #3074 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father (11 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

John Doolittle was born in New Haven on 14 June 1655, the son of Abraham Doolittle, an early settler and founder of Wallingford, Connecticut. He married Mary Peck, and together they raised a family that included Thankful Doolittle, who married into the Tuttle family in 1706; a daughter Esther, who also married a Tuttle; and a son, the Reverend Benjamin Doolittle, who became the settled minister at Northfield, Massachusetts. John Doolittle is last documented alive at a second marriage on 29 January 1717; the exact year of his death is not established, since an earlier record of "1746" turned out to belong to a son of the same name.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED AND SOURCED, 7 August 2026. His daughter Thankful’s parentage now rests on two independent published authorities: Jacobus, Families of Ancient New Haven (Tuttle section: “Thankful da. John & Mary (Peck) Doolittle”, with the marriage date 2 Nov 1706 matching the Wallingford town register, and the Doolittle section giving her death 23 Nov 1728 to the day) and Tuttle 1883, p. 575n. His own father is Abraham Doolittle, the migrant (Jacobus p. 42; Davis’s Wallingford p. 835; Savage 2:70).

⚠⚠ TWO CAUTIONS, BOTH LOGGED BEFORE THEY COST A CORRECTION. First, the rank “Sgt.” is not his in any source read — Jacobus attaches it to his father Abraham. Second, the death year 1746 is at risk of conflation: Davis p. 839 has a younger John Doolittle, son of this John and Mary, dying at Wallingford in 1746-7. The chart’s “1655–1746” may have absorbed the son’s death.

Outstanding: the Wallingford register — Thankful’s birth entry of 5 Apr 1688 naming her parents, and the Doolittle × Peck marriage of 13 Feb 1682/3, ideally on the same film. That one pull converts this node and the next to Documented.

⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED hyp → part [TUTTLE-38]. His son REV. BENJAMIN DOOLITTLE (1695–1748) was the settled minister at NORTHFIELD, Massachusetts — independently attested, and named as Gershom Tuttle’s UNCLE in Miscellaneous Notes on New England Families. That ties this Doolittle household to our line by a stated relationship rather than by a name match, and it explains how a Wallingford family came to have a foothold a hundred miles north. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-34]. Named as the father of Thankful Doolittle (node 1537) on Find a Grave memorial 139363422: “Thankful was the daughter of John and Mary Peck Doolittle.” A long life — 1655 to 1746, ninety-one years — and a sergeant’s title. ⭐ His children include Rev. Benjamin Doolittle (1695–1748) and a daughter Esther Doolittle (1683–1756) who ALSO married a Tuttle — two Doolittle sisters marrying into the same family, which is what happens in a town this size and is a good sign the identification is real.

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ ‘hyp’ — one compiled source, no record read. ⭐ ROUTES: Wallingford town births c.1688 for Thankful; New Haven district probate for John Doolittle, d. 1746, which would name his daughters and their husbands and tie node 1537 to him from a record.

Corrected 7 Aug 2026 (self-correction #17): the former “1746” death was a son’s (a John Doolittle who d. Wallingford 1746-7 ae. 35, so b. c.1711); our John is last seen alive at his 2nd marriage, 29 Jan 1717. “Sgt.” belonged to Abraham, not John.

Mary Peck (1666–1710; wife of Sgt. John Doolittle)

Ahnentafel #3075 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother (11 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Mary Peck was born in New Haven on 4 March 1665/6, the daughter of John and Mary (Moss) Peck, and she married John Doolittle of Wallingford. She died there on 1 September 1710. She was the mother of Thankful Doolittle, who carried the family forward into the Tuttle line — though care is needed here, since a different woman also named Mary Peck, the daughter of Joseph and Sarah (Alling) Peck, separately married the widower Timothy Tuttle in 1729; the two Mary Pecks belonged to entirely different Wallingford families.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED FROM HYPOTHESIS TO PROBABLE, 7 August 2026. Jacobus gives her as “Mary da. John & Mary (Moss) Peck, b 4 Mar 1665/6 NHV, d 1 Sep 1710 WV”, and Tuttle 1883 p. 575n says the same independently: “Mary Peck was dau. of John and Mary (Moss) Peck.”

⚠⚠ THE TRAP, NOW SOURCE-DOCUMENTED. Thankful’s mother was this Mary Peck (1666–1710). Dea. Timothy Tuttle’s second wife, married 1729 (his third, 1749, was Sarah Atwater), was also a Mary Peck — the widow of Stephen Rowe, and “Mary da. Joseph & Sarah (Ailing) Peck, wid. Stephen Rowe” (Jacobus p. 354; his p. 181 gives her own family entry — the earlier ‘Lydia (Ball)’ reading was a transcription error: Jacobus’s only ‘Lydia (Ball)’ is in the unrelated Smith family, p. 120 [TUTTLE-58]), a different Peck family entirely. Two women, same name, same town. Any source that has Timothy marrying his own mother-in-law has merged them — treat that as a red flag on the source.

⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-34]. Named as the mother of Thankful Doolittle on Find a Grave memorial 139363422.

Outstanding: ‘part’ — compiled sources only (Jacobus + Davis + Tuttle 1883); the Wallingford VR images (marriage 13 Feb 1682/3, Thankful’s birth 5 Apr 1688) would move this to ‘ok’ — browser ★5. ⚠⚠ AND A NAME-COLLISION TRAP: her son-in-law Timothy Tuttle later married, in 1729, a DIFFERENT Mary Peck — the widow of Stephen Rowe. This Mary Peck died in 1710 and cannot be that one. Any source showing Timothy marrying his own mother-in-law has merged them; treat that as a red flag on the source.

Samuel Allis (24 Feb 1647 Braintree – 9 Mar 1691 Hatfield; carpenter and builder)

Ahnentafel #3076 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s father (11 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Samuel Allis was born in Braintree, Massachusetts, on 24 February 1647, the son of Lieutenant William Allis. He worked as a carpenter and builder in Hatfield, where the town granted him a house lot as a young man of about twenty-two, and he lived on the east side of Hatfield street. He married Alice about 1675-6, and together they had seven children, among them William, born 19 October 1680, from whom this family line descends. Samuel Allis died in Hatfield on 9 March 1691, leaving his widow to raise their children alone; two of those children were later caught up in the 1704 raid on Deerfield that killed their mother — one son killed alongside her, and a daughter carried captive to Canada who survived and came home.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ BOLTWOOD’S FULL ENTRY, READ 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-32] (Genealogies of Hadley Families, printed with Judd’s Hadley, image 586): “3. Samuel, s. of William, (1) d. Mch. 9, 1691. m. ALICE, who m. (2) Sergt. John Hawks, and was slain in Deer. Feb. 29, 1704. Children — Mehitable, b. July 2, 1677, m. Dec. 13, 1698, Benoni Moore; Samuel, b. Feb. 20, 1679, slain Feb. 29, 1704; WILLIAM, b. Oct. 19, 1680; Mary, b. July 6, 1682, m. Nathaniel Brooks; Thomas, b. Mch. 12, 1684, rem. to Guilford; Sarah, b. abt. 1685; Rebecca, b. Nov. 29, 1687.” SEVEN CHILDREN, DATED — and it agrees with the 1919 Allis genealogy on every date the two books share, including our William’s 19 October 1680. ⭐⭐⭐ AND IT CONFIRMS LAST NIGHT’S SHELDON FIND FROM A SECOND DIRECTION: Mary Allis, b. 6 July 1682, is the one who married Nathaniel Brooks — Sheldon says she was “a fellow cap. of 1704”, so she was twenty-one when she was marched to Canada in the raid that killed her mother and her brother, and she came home. ⭐ It also settles the Mehitable conflict logged last turn: Boltwood gives b. 2 July 1677 independently, so the “a. 90” on her 1757 death is the gravestone’s error, not the books’. ⭐ UPGRADED part → ok: two independent authorities — the 1919 Allis genealogy and Boltwood, working a century apart from the Hampshire County records — agree on his death date, his wife, his father and his children’s dates, and a primary Hatfield town record [TUTTLE-27] grants him his house lot in 1669–71. ——— Earlier note: ⭐ UPGRADED hyp → part [TUTTLE-19]: Boltwood independently confirms “Samuel, s. of William, (1) d. Mch. 9, 1691” and the children Mehitable (2 Jul 1677), Samuel (20 Feb 1679) and William (19 Oct 1680). Two independent published authorities now agree on this generation. ⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — GENERATION 12. “3. Outstanding: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ A PRIMARY RECORD AT LAST [TUTTLE-27]: the Hatfield town book records that “the Town of Hatfield hath granted to SAMUEL ALLIS a house lot” and acreage, 1669–1671. Born Braintree Feb 1647, he was about twenty-two — the age a young man took a home lot and set up. ⭐ It fits the family’s movement from a wholly separate source: Boltwood has his father Lt. William Allis “rem. abt. 1661 to Hatfield” — father arrives c.1661, son takes his own lot c.1669. ⚠⚠ A town grant names a grantee, not a father — so this does NOT prove his parentage and the node stays ‘part’. But the man himself is now documented from a record rather than only from books. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ TWO MORE OF HIS CHILDREN, 6 Aug 2026, FROM SHELDON’S DEERFIELD GENEALOGIES [TUTTLE-31] — and both come from OTHER families’ entries, which is the strongest kind of citation because the compiler had no interest in the Allis line at all. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ MARY ALLIS — A CAPTIVE OF 1704 WHO CAME HOME. Sheldon image 354, in the entry for Nathaniel, s. of William (1), b. 1664 — “house burned and whole family cap. 1704; he alone came back; was recovered by Ens. John Sheldon on his second expedition to Can.”, whose first wife was killed on the march after a fall on the ice — “He m. (2) Feb. 3, 1710, Mary, dau. Samuel Allis of Hat., A FELLOW CAP. OF 1704.” A daughter of this house was carried to Canada in the same raid that killed her mother Alice and her brother Samuel — and she survived it, came back, and married another survivor of it six years later. ⭐⭐⭐ MEHITABLE ALLIS. Sheldon image 506: “MOORE, Benoni … d. Nov. 18, 1753. He m. Dec. 13, 1698, Mehitable, dau. Sam’l Allis of Hat.; she d. May 8, 1757, a. 90.” ⚠⚠ CONFLICT LOGGED: “a. 90” implies b. c.1667, ten years before the 1919 book’s 2 July 1677 and earlier than her parents’ own marriage allows. Gravestone ages are the least reliable figure in New England genealogy; 1677 is preferred and the discrepancy is recorded, not smoothed away. ⚠ Sheldon and Judd also show a Samuel Allis at Hatfield in the May 1667 petition dispute — probably NOT ours, who was born at Braintree in 1647 and took his lot only in 1669–71. Flagged, not attached. ⭐⭐⭐ NEXT: the SAME VOLUME is “Town Records 1660–1740, VITAL RECORDS 1660–1740” — so his 1675-6 marriage should be in it, and that is the likeliest place in existence to find ALICE’S MAIDEN NAME.

Alice (m. Samuel Allis 1675–6; m.(2) Sergt. John Hawks 1696; killed at Deerfield, 29 Feb 1704)

Ahnentafel #3077 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s mother (11 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Alice married Samuel Allis of Hatfield about 1675-6 and bore him seven children before his death in 1691 left her a widow. She remarried on 20 November 1696 to Sergeant John Hawks, a veteran of the Falls Fight who had been wounded defending Hatfield in 1676. On 29 February 1704 Alice was killed in the French and Indian raid on Deerfield, along with her twenty-five-year-old son Samuel; her daughter Mary was carried captive to Canada in the same attack but survived and eventually came home. Alice's own family name was never recorded by any of the several genealogists who studied her — she is remembered, in every source, only by her first name.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ A SIXTH SOURCE, AND THE RICHEST — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-46]. Sheldon, History of Deerfield VOLUME ONE, image 61, in the entry for her second husband: “HAWKS, John, son of John of Hadley… He was active through Philip’s war; was in the FALLS FIGHT; and one of the brave men from Hadley who went to the succor of HATFIELD, when attacked by Indians May 30, 1676, when he was WOUNDED. He married in 1667, MARTHA BALDWIN, with whom he lived ten years. He became a permanent settler in 1683, and married in 1696, ALICE, WIDOW OF SAMUEL ALLIS. SHE WAS KILLED FEB. 29, 1704, WHEN ALL HIS CHILDREN AND GRANDCHILDREN OF THE NAME WERE LOST. In his old age he removed to Waterbury, Conn., to live with his only surviving child, who had married Jonathan Scott of that town.” ⚠⚠ “All his children and grandchildren of the name were lost” — the HAWKS name was wiped out in that raid, and his wife with it. He was a Falls Fight veteran who had been wounded relieving Hatfield in 1676; twenty-eight years later he lost his whole family in a morning, and ended his life in Connecticut with the one daughter who was left. ⭐⭐⭐ AND A LEAD WORTH CHASING: his FIRST wife was MARTHA BALDWIN, m. 1667. MARY BALDWIN — node 3083, Alec’s own ancestor — married John Catlin in 1662. Two Baldwin women marrying into Deerfield within five years: almost certainly sisters, and almost certainly daughters of Joseph Baldwin of Milford. ⚠ Recorded as a lead, not a link. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘part’ → ‘ok’, 6 Aug 2026, AT ALEC’S CHALLENGE [AUDIT-01]. The colour grades WHO THIS PERSON IS AND WHOSE CHILD THEY ARE — not whether every fact about them is known. Loose ends belong in Outstanding, below, and this node has some. They do not make the ancestor uncertain. ⛔⛔ HER SURNAME IS A DOCUMENTED BLANK IN EVERY STANDARD AUTHORITY — FIVE OF THEM NOW [TUTTLE-31/32]. ⭐ But her CHILDREN are no longer a blank. Boltwood gives all seven with dates: Mehitable 2 Jul 1677; Samuel 20 Feb 1679 (slain with her, 29 Feb 1704); WILLIAM 19 Oct 1680; Mary 6 Jul 1682 (carried to Canada in the same raid, and survived); Thomas 12 Mar 1684; Sarah c.1685; Rebecca 29 Nov 1687. She was widowed in 1691 with seven children under fifteen, remarried in 1696, and was killed in 1704. Allis genealogy 1919: a literal blank where the surname goes. Boltwood, in Judd’s Hadley, image 647: “(2) Nov. 20, 1696, Alice, wid. of Samuel Allis of Hat. She was slain by French and Indians, Feb. 29, [1704]”. Sheldon, Deerfield vol. 2, image 453: “(2) Nov. 20, 1696, Alice, wid. of Samuel Allis; she was k. 1704.” Three independent compilers, each with the Hampshire County records in front of them, each declining to name her. ⭐ What they DO agree on is not small: widow of Samuel Allis; remarried Sergt. John Hawks 20 Nov 1696; killed 29 Feb 1704 — TUTTLE-19 now confirmed twice from sources the 1919 book never used. ⛔⛔ AND THIS BURIES THE “ALICE MEEKINS” LEAD [TUTTLE-21]. A gravestone index that supplies a surname three record-reading compilers could not find is supplying something, not reporting it. Not entered. ⭐ If her name is recoverable it will come from a RECORD, not a book: the Hampshire Co. probate of Samuel Allis (d. 9 Mar 1691), the Hatfield marriage of 1675-6, or a Hatfield deed carrying her release of dower. ——— ⚠⚠⚠ UPGRADED hyp → part, and her death FOUND [TUTTLE-19]. Boltwood: “m. ALICE, who m. (2) Sergt. JOHN HAWKS, and was SLAIN IN DEER[FIELD] FEB. 29, 1704.” The Deerfield raid research project, independently: “John Hawks’s wife ALICE, was killed, as was her son SAMUEL ALLIS (b.1679). Their seven-year-old daughter ELIZABETH, was captured and killed on the march to Canada.” Widowed at Hatfield in 1691 with seven children; remarried Nov 1696; killed in the raid on Deerfield, 29 February 1704, together with her 25-year-old son. Alec’s line runs through her third child, WILLIAM, b. 19 Oct 1680 — who was not in Deerfield that night. ⭐ Her second husband John Hawks survived; of his household only a daughter living in Connecticut did.

Outstanding: ⛔⛔⛔ SHE HAS NO MAIDEN NAME IN ANY OF THREE SOURCES [TUTTLE-20]. Boltwood: none. The 1919 Allis genealogy: “he married in 1675-6, Alice , ” — the printed text has a literal BLANK where the surname belongs, so the compiler knew one was missing. Samuel Davis (b. Roxbury ~1645 – d. 26 July 1690, Northampton, Mass.)

Ahnentafel #3078 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s father (11 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Samuel Davis was born about 1645, probably in Roxbury, Massachusetts. A 1649 Roxbury church record lists a Samuel Davis among the children of William Davis, and Samuel Davis was later dismissed from that church to Northampton, where he had settled by 1668 and become a freeman by 1676. He held shares in a local silver and lead mine venture and was a subscriber to Harvard College. He had two wives, the second Elizabeth Smead, daughter of William Smead, whom he married in 1681; his children included sons Samuel and John and three daughters whose names were not recorded. Samuel Davis died in the Northampton epidemic on 26 July 1690.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 7 August 2026 — added at PROBABLE after an adversary pass CONFIRMED the link at exactly this grade [TUTTLE-56]. Savage 2:31: “SAMUEL, Roxbury, perhaps s. of William of the same, rem. to Northampton 1668, was freem. 1676, had two ws. of wh. sec. was Eliz. d. of William Smead, m. 1681; and he d. 26 July 1690, leav. beside three ds. Samuel, b. 1672; and John, 1677.” Trumbull’s Northampton documents the man independently — proprietor in the silver/lead-mine venture (sold shares to Medad Pomeroy), Harvard College subscriber, died in the great 1690 epidemic; his wife was fined in 1676 for wearing silk “in a flaunting manner.” The adversary’s sweep found exactly ONE Davis household of this generation in Northampton — and none in Hadley, Deerfield, or Savage’s valley pages with an Elizabeth of the right age.

⚠ Why Probable: Savage’s three daughters are UNNAMED — no record yet names Elizabeth as his; the tie runs through the 1919 Allis book’s “of Northampton” plus elimination. Migrant candidate William Davis of Roxbury = Savage’s own “perhaps” — a LEAD and a STOP, not on the chart.

Outstanding: The closers: the claimed Northampton birth record (Elizabeth Davis, 20 Jun 1679 — FS image S3HT-691S-CW4, img 00019, cited but UNVIEWED) and Samuel’s 1690 Hampshire Co. probate, which would name the three daughters and test “William of Roxbury.”

Hannah Edwards (proposed first wife of Samuel Davis; m. 4 Mar 1668/9 – d. 11 Sep 1680, both uncertain)

Ahnentafel #3079 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s mother (11 generations back) · Hypothesis — a candidate, not tied in

Hannah Edwards is a candidate — not a confirmed identification — for the first wife of Samuel Davis of Northampton. One compiled source names her as the daughter of Alexander Edwards of Northampton, married to Samuel on 4 March 1668/9 and dead by 11 September 1680, but the underlying record naming both parents has not yet been verified, and the identification remains no more than a hypothesis. An equally unproven alternative is that the relevant daughter descended instead from Samuel's second wife, Elizabeth Smead. Neither possibility has been settled.

Evidence

⚠⚠ NEW NODE, 7 August 2026 — entered at HYPOTHESIS, because the adversary pass REFUTED the finder’s ‘Probable’ [TUTTLE-56]. The identification of Elizabeth’s mother as Hannah, daughter of Alexander Edwards of Northampton (b. 1645 Springfield, m. 4 Mar 1668/9, d. 11 Sep 1680) rests on tree consensus whose one record-shaped leg — the USGWArchives Northampton VR transcription (“Davis, Samuel / Edwards, Hannah, 3/4/1668-9”) — could not be verified from this VM. Savage is silent twice (names neither Samuel’s first wife nor a husband for Alexander Edwards’s Hannah), and the finder’s silk-fine argument was killed on verification (the 1676 list is a town-wide sumptuary roll, not an Edwards grouping).

⚠ The live alternative: Savage never assigns the three daughters to a wife. Samuel remarried Elizabeth Smead in 1681 — a daughter born 1682–86 fits the 1703-4 marriage and 1758 death too. If the Allis bride is a Smead daughter, the father stands and the mother flips.
