Mr. John Catlin (b. about 1643 – killed defending his house at Deerfield, 29 Feb 1704)

Ahnentafel #3082 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s mother’s father (11 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

John Catlin was born about 1643, the son of a Catlin family of Wethersfield. He lived successively in Wethersfield, Branford, and Newark, New Jersey — where he served as a schoolteacher in 1676, as the town's attorney, and as a selectman for five years — before moving to Hartford and then, about 1683, to Deerfield, Massachusetts, where he was given the honorific title "Mr." and took a leading part in town affairs. On 29 February 1704, during the French and Indian raid on Deerfield, John Catlin and his son Jonathan were killed defending their house, which was then burned; another son was killed in the fighting that followed in the meadow, and a third son, also John, was carried captive to Canada, later returning to rebuild on the same ground and becoming known locally as "Master Catlin." Two of his daughters, Elizabeth and Mary, died on the forced march to Canada after the raid; a third daughter, Sarah, had already married and left the household, which is why this line of the family survived at all.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE HOME LOT, THE MANNER OF HIS DEATH, AND THE CAPTURED SON — ALL NAMED [TUTTLE-47]. Sheldon VOLUME ONE, image 636, in the survey of Deerfield home lots (lot 32, Samson Frary’s part, four acres): “…sold in 1685 to JOHN CATLIN. HERE HE AND HIS SON JONATHAN WERE KILLED WHILE DEFENDING HIS HOUSE IN 1704, BUT IT WAS TAKEN AND BURNED. HIS SON JOHN WAS CAPTURED. HE CAME BACK AND BUILT A HOUSE HERE ABOUT 1715. HE WAS A HOUSEWRIGHT AND WAS ALWAYS SPOKEN OF BY TRADITION AS ‘MASTER CATLIN.’” ⚠ A CORRECTION OF NUANCE TO TUTTLE-44: vol. 2 said he and Jonathan were “consumed” with the burning buildings. Vol. 1 says they were KILLED WHILE DEFENDING THE HOUSE, and the house was then taken and burned. He died fighting, not trapped. ⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND THE CAPTURED SON, unnamed when vol. 2’s sentence broke at the page, IS JOHN CATLIN JUNIOR — carried to Canada at 1704, came home, and by about 1715 had rebuilt on his father’s ashes. A housewright: the son of a burned-out house became a builder of houses. ⭐ He also bought lot 33 in 1685 (from John Parsons) and lot 36 was his in 1704 — three home lots on Deerfield street. ⚠ The 1704 casualty table (image 324) lists “John Catlin, (?) 60” — an age of about sixty, matching a birth c.1643. ⚠⚠ The same table’s adjacent columns are badly mangled by OCR (names, ages and the phrase “burnt in the cellar” run together across two columns) — NOTHING from that table is attributed to this family beyond his own line until the page image is read. ——— Earlier note: ⚠⚠⚠ HIS FATE IS ESTABLISHED, AND IT IS AS BAD AS THE RECORD GETS — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-44]. Sheldon, image 368, entry 2 of the CATLIN genealogy, in full: “2. John, s. of John (1), b. abt. 1643; of Weth., 1662; of BRANFORD, 1665, whence he rem. to NEWARK, where he was a TEACHER 1676; in 1678 he was ‘Town’s Attorney (not lawyer), an “honest brother,” to take care that all town orders be executed, and if a breach occurs to punish the offender’; SELECTMAN 1676-81; was of Hart. 1683, of Dfd. soon after, as one of the early permanent settlers, where he was DIGNIFIED BY THE TITLE OF “MR.” and took a prominent part in affairs; NO FAMILY SUFFERED MORE THAN HIS AT THE DESOLATION OF THE TOWN 1704; HIS BUILDINGS WERE ALL BURNED, AND HIMSELF AND S. JONATHAN CONSUMED WITH THEM; ONE S. WAS K. IN THE MEADOW FIGHT; ANOTHER AND THREE DAUS. WERE [captured]…” ⚠⚠ He and his son Jonathan burned to death in their own house. A second son was killed in the Meadow Fight later that morning. A third son and three daughters were carried off. Two of those daughters — ELIZABETH (m. James Corse) and MARY (m. Thomas Wells) — died on the march to Canada, Mary on 9 March. ⭐⭐⭐ AND HE WAS NOT A FARMER. A SCHOOLMASTER at Newark in 1676, the town’s attorney and an “honest brother”, selectman five years, and at Deerfield a man given the title “Mr.” — which in a New England town record is a rank, not a courtesy. ⭐ His route is one of the widest on this chart: Wethersfield 1662 → Branford 1665 → NEWARK, NEW JERSEY 1676-81 → Hartford 1683 → Deerfield. ⚠ His daughter SARAH escaped only because she had already married Michael Mitchell about 1694 and was in her own household — which is why his line survives to reach Alec at all. ⭐ UPGRADED Probable → Documented: the child list in his own entry (image 369) carries “Sarah, —; m. abt. 1694, Michael”, so the link to node 1541 is inside the Catlin genealogy and not borrowed from another family. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-43]. Sheldon, image 319: “m. Sept. 23, 1662, JOHN CATLIN, with whom she rem. fr. NEWARK to Dfd. abt. 1683; their large family were nearly all destroyed Feb. 29, 1704.” ⭐ A REAL MIGRATION, AND AN UNUSUAL ONE: the Catlins run Wethersfield, Conn. → Branford → NEWARK, NEW JERSEY → DEERFIELD c.1683 — the first New Jersey connection anywhere on this chart, and it comes in from the New Haven Colony party that founded Newark in 1666. ⭐ His father is entry 1 of Sheldon’s CATLIN genealogy (image 368): “CATLIN, CATLING, CATTELL, John, OF WETHERSFIELD; estate appraised July 17, 1644, amt. £69. He m. ISABELLA, prob. sister of Lawrence Ward of New Haven, Branford, Ct., and Newark, N.J., where he d. 1670…” ⚠ Two Johns in succession — do not merge them. The 1644 estate belongs to the elder.

Outstanding: ⚠⚠⚠ HIS OWN FATE ON 29 FEBRUARY 1704 IS NOT ESTABLISHED HERE AND MUST NOT BE ASSUMED FROM THE RAID’S REPUTATION. Sheldon says the family was “nearly all destroyed” and names two daughters killed on the march — he does not, in these passages, say what became of the father. ⭐ READ SHELDON’S CATLIN GENEALOGY ENTRY FOR HIM DIRECTLY, and the 1704 casualty and captive lists, which are exhaustively studied. ⚠ Note a “John Catlin” appears in Deerfield office-holder lists for 1689–1704 and a later Capt. John Catlin 1721–58 — at least three men of the name; sort them before writing anything. ⭐ Graded Probable only because the link Sarah → this John rests on Sheldon’s single statement “Sarah, dau. John Catlin” and there were several.

Mary Baldwin (bap. 23 June 1644; m. 23 Sept 1662 – survived the raid of 29 Feb 1704)

Ahnentafel #3083 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s mother’s mother (11 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Mary Baldwin was baptized 23 June 1644, the daughter of Joseph Baldwin of Milford, Connecticut, and his first wife, Hannah. She married John Catlin on 23 September 1662, and the couple moved with him from Newark, New Jersey, to Deerfield, Massachusetts, about 1683. On 29 February 1704, in the raid that killed her husband and several of their children, Mary Baldwin herself survived; family tradition, recorded generations later by a descendant and printed by the historian of Deerfield, held that she had given water to a wounded French officer during the attack and was spared as a result, and that afterward, alone in the emptied house, she told a frightened boy in hiding to run rather than stay with her, since she was already a captive and he was not. That account was passed down as tradition rather than as a contemporary record, but it is the fullest picture that survives of how she came through that morning.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ SHELDON’S OWN VERDICT ON HER, AND A CORRECTION THAT A FLAG CAUGHT — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-49]. Printed page 312, read at the page image: “…the house, being the largest in the town, was reserved as a depot for captives. Here, then, was the place where MRS. CATLIN RELIEVED THE WOUNDED FRENCH LIEUTENANT, AND SECURED HER OWN FREEDOM BY HER PRACTICAL CHRISTIANITY.” The historian, weighing a family tradition a century old, accepted it — and put the reason for her survival in five words. ⚠⚠ AND A CORRECTION THE FLAG EARNED. TUTTLE-47 recorded that the OCR of the casualty table ran two columns together, and that nothing from it would be attributed to this family until the page was read. The page is read: the “two years old MERCY” whose “brains [were] dashed out on the doorstone” is MERCY SHELDON, of Ensign Sheldon’s household — NOT a Catlin. Had that been written up from the OCR it would have put a murdered two-year-old into the wrong family and she would have stayed there. ⭐ The same page adds a Catlin in the other defended house: “probably Joseph Catlin with his wife and child” in Benoni Stebbins’s, which held out and drove the assailants back with loss. ⭐ And the building she stood in survived her by 145 years — the “Old Indian House,” standing until 1849; its scarred and battered door is in Memorial Hall. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE LETTER READ AT THE PAGE, AND THE ENDING IS THE POINT — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-48]. Sheldon vol. 1, printed page 310, the close of Lucy D. Shearer’s letter: “…Mrs. Catlin was left. After they were all gone, a little boy came that was hid in the house. Mrs. Catlin said to the boy, ‘go run and hide.’ The boy said, ‘Mrs. Catlin, why don’t you go and hide?’ She said, ‘I AM A CAPTIVE; IT IS NOT MY DUTY TO HIDE, BUT YOU HAVE NOT BEEN TAKEN, AND IT IS YOUR DUTY TO HIDE.’ Who this boy was I do not know. SOME THOUGHT THE KINDNESS SHOWN TO THE FRENCHMAN WAS THE REASON OF MRS. CATLIN’S BEING LEFT.” Alone in an emptied house with the column already gone, she held herself bound by a parole nobody was there to enforce — and sent the child away instead. ⭐ And the family’s own explanation of why she lived is in the last line: they thought the water she gave the dying officer was the reason she was left behind. ⚠ Still tradition, not record — Mrs. Shearer was born in 1804 — and “Mrs. Catlin” is unnamed; the narrator is the captured son, so his mother is the natural reading. ⭐⭐⭐ AND THE SCALE, from the facing paragraph: “When the night of February 28th closed down, 291 SOULS were under their rooftrees … from Widow Allison of eighty-four years, to John, the youngling of Deacon French’s flock, of four weeks. Among them were three negro slaves, one Indian, and three Frenchmen from Canada. In a few hours ALL BUT ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-SIX of the inhabitants were either killed or in the hands of a cruel enemy, on a march over the snow to Canada, THREE HUNDRED MILES AWAY.” Of forty-one houses, fifteen stood inside the stockade. Alec has three families in that count. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ THE SILENCE IS BROKEN, AND IT IS BROKEN BY HER OWN WORDS — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-47]. Two turns ago this node recorded that no source read stated her fate and left the silence standing. Sheldon’s VOLUME ONE, images 325–326, states it — in a letter from a descendant, which Sheldon prints because he judged it “an apparently authentic tradition” that “should have a permanent record here”: “His father and uncle were killed in the house; he took his father’s gun, and his uncle’s powder horn, and was going to use them when the Indians took him. The captives were taken to a house … and a Frenchman was brought in and laid on the floor; he was in great distress, and called for water; MRS. CATLIN FED HIM WITH WATER. Some one said to her, ‘How can you do that for your enemy?’ She replied, ‘IF THINE ENEMY HUNGER, FEED HIM; IF HE THIRST, GIVE HIM WATER TO DRINK.’ The Frenchman was taken and carried away, and the captives marched off. MRS. CATLIN WAS LEFT. After they were all gone, a little boy came that was hid in the house. Mrs. Catlin said to the boy, ‘go run and hide.’” ⭐ Sheldon places the scene (image 328): the Sheldon house, “being the largest in the town, was reserved as a depot for captives. Here, then, was the place where Mrs. Catlin relieved the wounded French lieutenant.” On the morning her husband and one son burned in their own house and another son was carried off, she gave water to a dying enemy officer, answered the person who challenged her with Romans 12:20, and then told a hidden child to run. ⚠⚠ GRADE IT HONESTLY: this is a FAMILY TRADITION, written down by Mrs. Lucy D. Shearer of Colrain — born 1804, a hundred years after the event — and printed by Sheldon as tradition, not record. ⚠ And “Mrs. Catlin” is not named. The narrator is the captured son, so his mother is the natural reading and Sheldon reads it that way — but it could be a daughter-in-law, and the node says so rather than smoothing it. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐ A SISTER, PROBABLY — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-46]. Sheldon vol. 1, image 61, has Sergt. John Hawks marrying MARTHA BALDWIN in 1667 — five years after Mary Baldwin married John Catlin. Two Baldwin women marrying into the same small frontier town within five years are almost certainly sisters, and Joseph Baldwin of Milford is the obvious father of both. ⚠⚠ AND IF SO, THE DEERFIELD KNOT TIGHTENS AGAIN: John Hawks’s SECOND wife was ALICE, widow of Samuel Allis (node 3077) — so Alec’s ancestor Mary Baldwin would be the sister of the first wife of the man who married another of Alec’s ancestors. Recorded as a lead. Two women of one surname in one town is a pattern, not a proof. ——— Earlier note: ⭐ UPGRADED Probable → Documented [TUTTLE-44] — her husband’s own entry in Sheldon’s CATLIN genealogy is now read and its child list names Sarah, so the chain Mary → Sarah → Joseph Mitchell → Tabitha runs inside one genealogy. ⚠⚠ HER OWN FATE ON 29 FEBRUARY 1704 IS STILL NOT STATED. Sheldon says “no family suffered more than his”, names her husband and son Jonathan as burned in the house, a son killed in the Meadow Fight, and a son and three daughters carried off — and does not say what became of the mother. That silence is left standing here rather than filled in. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-43]. Sheldon’s BALDWIN genealogy, image 319, gives her baptism and her marriage: “Mary, bap. June 23, 1644; m. Sept. 23, 1662, John Catlin, with whom she rem. fr. Newark to Dfd. abt. 1683.” ⭐ Her father is JOSEPH BALDWIN, emigrant — “prob. s. of Richard of Cholesbury, County Bucks, Eng.; of MILFORD, CT., 1639; rem. to Had[ley] 1662-3” — an English parish named for a New England ancestor, which is rare on this chart. ⚠⚠ AND A TANGLE WORTH SEEING BEFORE IT CONFUSES SOMEONE: Joseph Baldwin’s SECOND wife was “Isabella [Ward?], wid. of JOHN CATLIN of Newark, N.J.” — the elder John Catlin’s widow. So Mary Baldwin married the son of her own stepmother. The two families interlock twice in one generation.

Outstanding: ⚠ Graded Probable with her husband — the link to Sarah runs through him. ⭐ Sheldon has a full BALDWIN genealogy; the Cholesbury, Buckinghamshire claim for Joseph Baldwin is the kind of English origin that has to be tested, not adopted. ⚠ Her own fate in the 1704 raid is not stated in the passages read.

William Tuttle (the immigrant — came from old to New England in 1635; New Haven 1639; d. 1673)

Ahnentafel #6144 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father (12 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

William Tuttle came from England to New England in 1635 and settled in New Haven in 1639, becoming the head of a large family whose descendants filled an entire published genealogy two centuries later. He died in 1673, and his estate, including lands in the Branford division, was still being divided among his children as late as 1705. His son Simon's own deeds, drawn up decades after William's death, repeatedly describe land as having belonged to "my father, Mr. William Tuttle," directly recording the connection between the generations in the family's own words.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘part’ → ‘ok’, 6 Aug 2026, AT ALEC’S CHALLENGE [AUDIT-01]. The colour grades WHO THIS PERSON IS AND WHOSE CHILD THEY ARE — not whether every fact about them is known. Loose ends belong in Outstanding, below, and this node has some. They do not make the ancestor uncertain. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED hyp → part [TUTTLE-32] — because his son’s DEEDS name him. Three Wallingford/New Haven conveyances by Simon Tuttle (node 3072), dated 1677-8, 1686 and 1705, each call William Tuttle “my father” by name. That is a contemporary record of the link, not a compiler’s assertion. ⭐ A fourth, in 1705, has Simon conveying “all my right in Branford division set to my father, Mr. William Tuttle, now (Feb. 14, 1705) laid out for his children” — the immigrant’s estate was still being divided among his children thirty-two years after his death. That division is a document, and it will list them. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — GENERATION 14, the deepest node on the Tuttle side and the deepest in the whole chart [TUTTLE-30]. He is the head of the 1883 work, whose full title is The Descendants of William and Elizabeth Tuttle, who came from old to New England in 1635, and settled in New Haven in 1639. Our line reaches him through his ninth child, Simon (node 3072), whose wife died in December 1684.

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ ‘hyp’ ON PURPOSE, AND IT SHOULD STAY THAT WAY UNTIL A RECORD IS READ. Every step from node 192 down to node 3072 now rests on ONE book — and that book has been caught in this same session printing Aug. 28 for Aug. 22, printing a birth year that four of its own facts refute, and misprinting Clark Strong Tuttle’s county. We are not taking an immigrant ancestor on a book’s word. ⭐ WHAT WOULD EARN IT: the New Haven colony records and William Tuttle’s estate (he d. 1673) — a distribution naming Simon would tie generations 13 and 14 together from a record instead of from a numbering scheme. ⚠ His wife Elizabeth is deliberately not yet a node.

Elizabeth Tuttle (b. about 1609 in England – d. 31 Dec 1684, New Haven, aged 76)

Ahnentafel #6145 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother (12 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Elizabeth Tuttle was born about 1609 in England and crossed to New England with her husband, William, in 1635, when she was about twenty-six. She outlived him by eleven years, dying in New Haven on 31 December 1684 at the age of seventy-six, and her gravestone survives in the city's Grove Street Cemetery, reading "Age 76: 31:10:1684 — Elisabeth Tutle." An epitaph once carved on the stone, since worn away but preserved in print, read, "The blest live as They are: the blest That live at Rest." Her children included Simon, from whom this family descends, along with John, Hannah, Thomas, Jonathan, Joseph, a second Elizabeth, Benjamin, and Mercy; her own parentage and English origins were never recorded, and a later claim connecting her to a Devon family was examined and withdrawn as unsupported.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘part’ → ‘ok’, 6 Aug 2026, AT ALEC’S CHALLENGE [AUDIT-01]. The colour grades WHO THIS PERSON IS AND WHOSE CHILD THEY ARE — not whether every fact about them is known. Loose ends belong in Outstanding, below, and this node has some. They do not make the ancestor uncertain. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HER GRAVESTONE EXISTS AND IS TRANSCRIBED — Alec asked for it and it is there [TUTTLE-34]. GROVE STREET CEMETERY, NEW HAVEN, CONNECTICUT. Find a Grave memorial 8395040, six photographs. The inscription: “Age 76: 31:10:1684 — Elisabeth Tutle” ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ READ THE DATE PROPERLY AND IT IS THE ONE WE PREDICTED. “31:10:1684” is OLD STYLE — day 31, MONTH 10, 1684 — and under the old calendar, which began the year in March, MONTH 10 IS DECEMBER. So the stone reads 31 December 1684 — and the 1883 book’s entry 9 says Simon Tuttle “probably continued in New Haven until after the death of his mother, Dec., 1684”. The stone and the book agree to the month, and the stone gives the day. ⭐ The book also preserves an epitaph since lost from the stone (Vol. I, p. LX): “The blest live as They are: the blest That live at Rest.” ⭐ “Age 76” puts her birth c.1608–09, so she was about 26 when the family crossed in 1635 and about 65 when William died in 1673; she outlived him by eleven years and her death is what scattered the New Haven household. ⭐⭐ HER CHILDREN ARE LINKED ON THE MEMORIAL AND OURS IS AMONG THEM: SIMON TUTTLE (node 3072) — with John 1631, Hannah 1633, Thomas 1634, Jonathan 1637, Joseph 1640, Elizabeth 1645, Benjamin 1648, Mercy 1650. ⚠ DATE CONFLICT LOGGED: the memorial gives Simon 1647–1716; the 1883 book gives his death as 16 April 1719, with his widow Abigail dying 11 Aug 1722. Not resolved here — the Wallingford probate will settle it. ⭐⭐⭐⭐ AND A NOTE ON THE SOURCE ITSELF, WHICH IS UNUSUALLY GOOD. The memorial’s maintainer withdraws a claim in writing: that Elizabeth was daughter of Edward Mathews and Elizabeth Nashe of Devon came “from an unstated source that cannot be found”, the 1883 book gives no origin for her at all, and Devon is 180 miles from Ringstead, Northamptonshire, the Tuttle home parish. A compiled source that shows its working and retracts its own guess is worth more than one that simply asserts. Her parents are NOT entered. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-32]. She is named in the title of the standard authority itself — The Descendants of WILLIAM AND ELIZABETH TUTTLE, who came from old to New England in 1635, and settled in New Haven in 1639 — and the 1883 compiler built an 838-page book on the pair. ⭐ Her death is dated from the other side of the family: entry 9 says Simon “probably continued in New Haven until after the death of his mother, Dec., 1684” — so Elizabeth Tuttle died in December 1684, eleven years after her husband, and her death is what moved this branch of the family out to Wallingford.

Outstanding: ⛔ NO MAIDEN NAME HERE. The 1883 title page gives her only as Elizabeth. ⭐ ROUTES: the New Haven colony records; William Tuttle’s estate, d. 1673, and the division of the Branford lands “laid out for his children” on 14 Feb 1705, which Simon’s own deed cites — that division should list the whole family; and the 1635 crossing, which is where the emigration claim has to be tested.

Abraham Doolittle (c.1620 – 11 Aug 1690, Wallingford, Conn.)

Ahnentafel #6148 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s father (12 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Abraham Doolittle was in New Haven by 1644, where he served over the years as corporal, freeman, marshal, and townsman; the family name was written "Dowlittle" in the earliest records. He was one of the founding settlers of Wallingford, Connecticut, arriving on the ground by 1668 and serving as deputy in 1668 and 1672. He died 11 August 1690, and his will named his wife and ten children — seven sons and three daughters. His English origins were deliberately left unexplored; the American story of this family begins with him.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐ A MIGRANT REACHED, 7 August 2026 — and per Alec’s rule, this is where the Doolittle line stops.

At New Haven by 1644 (Savage 2:70), and in the New Haven town records right through 1649–1662 as corporal, freeman, marshal and townsman. A founder of Wallingford, removing there in 1670 — Davis: “Abraham Doolittle and John Peck were on the ground in 1668.” Deputy 1668 and 1672. Died 11 August 1690; his will names his wife, seven sons and three daughters. ⭐ Savage notes the family “early it was Dowlittle.”

⭐ New Haven Colony, and that is the point. Like every other New England family on this chart he is a Great Migration man of the 1630s–40s, not a Plymouth one — which is why no Mayflower line runs through here either.

⚠ Graded Probable: the link to his son John is Jacobus’s and Davis’s, not yet a record read here. ⛔ His English origin is deliberately not researched — the American tree comes first.

Joan (Alling/Allen) (bp. 12 Oct 1617, Kempston, Beds. — first wife of Abraham Doolittle)

Ahnentafel #6149 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s mother (12 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Joan Alling was baptized 12 October 1617 in Kempston, Bedfordshire, the daughter of James Alling and sister of Sergeant Roger Alling, treasurer of New Haven Colony. She became the first wife of Abraham Doolittle, and their children included John Doolittle, born 14 June 1655, along with Elizabeth, born 1652, and Sarah. No marriage record for Joan and Abraham survives, and while the general fact that John descended from Abraham's first wife rather than a later one rests on several independent accounts, her specific identification as this Kempston-born Joan comes from a single genealogical authority.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED hyp → PROBABLE, 7 August 2026 — after an adversarial refutation pass, verdict CONFIRMED [TUTTLE-55]. The competing “Abigail Moss” attribution is rejected as John’s mother on an impossibility: Davis’s own head-entry gives Abigail Moss d. 5 Nov 1710 ae. 69 → b. c.1641 → she was ~14 when John was born (1655) — and the same Davis entry explicitly lists John under the FIRST marriage (“Children by 1st marriage: … 4 John, b June 14, 1655”), so the old “Davis conflict” was Davis contradicting himself. Savage 2:70 concurs on the structure (“these, it is said, were by first w.”). Jacobus supplies the first wife’s name on the children flanking John — “Elizabeth da. Abraham & Joan (Ailing) Doolittle, b 12 Apr 1652”; “Sarah da. Abraham & Joan (Ailing) Doolittle” — and his Alling entry: “3 Joan, bp 12 Oct 1617; m Abraham Doolittle of NH”, daughter of James Alling of Kempston, sister of Sgt. Roger Alling, Treasurer of New Haven Colony.

⚠ Why the ceiling is Probable: the maternity STRUCTURE (first wife, not Moss) is multiply documented, but the identification of that wife as the Kempston Joan rests on Jacobus alone — no marriage record for Abraham × Joan survives anywhere.

Outstanding: New Haven births/baptisms for the Doolittle children of the 1650s would document the maternity from a record. ⛔ The Alling side is English — per the stop-at-the-migrant rule it is deliberately left unpursued.

John Peck (b. c.1638 – d. 1724, Wallingford, Conn.)

Ahnentafel #6150 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s father (12 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

John Peck was born about 1638, the son of William Peck, and became a freeman in 1669. On 3 November 1664 he married Mary Moss, daughter of John Moss, and the couple settled in Wallingford, Connecticut, where Mary died on 16 November 1725. John Peck himself died in Wallingford in 1724.

Evidence

⭐ UPGRADED to DOCUMENTED, 8 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [TUTTLE-70/-VERDICT]: three converging authorities, the earliest explicit and independent — Savage (1860–62), p.391: “JOHN, New Haven, s. of William of the same, freem. 1669, m. 3 Nov. 1664, Mary, d. of John Moss… b. at Wallingford, where his w. d. 16 Nov. 1725”; Jacobus (records-first, independent); Darius Peck 1877 (“2. JOHN… b. New Haven, probably in 1638… d. at Wallingford in 1724”). The old “Henry Peck” rival is dead twice over: Jacobus’s nesting [TUTTLE-50] and Savage’s own same-name sweep (Henry’s John “d. young”; Ralph’s b. 1655, too late).

Outstanding: Dea. William Peck’s 9 Mar 1689 will (★2 browser) remains wanted as the record-side seal — it names son John.

Mary Moss (bap. 11 Apr 1647 New Haven – d. 16 Nov 1725, Wallingford)

Ahnentafel #6151 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s mother (12 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Mary Moss was baptized in New Haven on 11 April 1647, the daughter of John Moss. She married John Peck on 3 November 1664, and the couple settled in Wallingford, Connecticut, where she died on 16 November 1725 at the age of seventy-eight.

Evidence

⭐ UPGRADED to DOCUMENTED, 8 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [TUTTLE-70/-VERDICT]: Savage names her from BOTH directions in one volume (Moss entry: “Mary, 11 Apr. 1647… Mary m. 3 Nov. 1664, John Peck”; Peck entry: “Mary, d. of John Moss… his w. d. 16 Nov. 1725, aged 78”), with Jacobus and Darius Peck 1877 concurring on bap., marriage, and death to the day.

Outstanding: her father John Moss (d. 1707 “aged 103, perhaps with slight exagg.” — Savage) is an un-opened Gen-14 name if the line is ever extended.

Lt. William Allis (freeman of Braintree 1640; commanded the Hatfield garrison 1676 – d. Sept 1678)

Ahnentafel #6152 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s father’s father (12 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

William Allis became a freeman at Braintree, Massachusetts, in 1640 and removed to Hatfield about 1661. During King Philip's War he commanded the garrison at Hatfield, thirty-six men strong — the largest force in the valley after Springfield's — and oversaw soldiers cutting timber for the fortifications at Hatfield and Deerfield. He married first, in 1641, a woman named Mary, who died 10 August 1677; their son Samuel, born 24 February 1647, carried the family line forward, while another son, William, was killed in the Falls Fight at Turners Falls on 19 May 1676. Lieutenant Allis remarried on 25 June 1678, to Mary, daughter of John Bronson and widow of John Graves of Haddam, but died only ten weeks later, in September 1678.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ HE HAS A MILITARY RECORD, AND IT IS A COMMAND — 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-46]. Sheldon, VOLUME ONE, image 142, on the garrisoning of the valley in King Philip’s War: “Garrisons were established in the valley towns; thirty-nine men under Pynchon, at Springfield; twenty-nine at Westfield, under Capt. Aaron Cooke; twenty-six at Northampton, under Lieut. Wm. Clark; THIRTY-SIX AT HATFIELD, UNDER LIEUT. WM. ALLIS; and thirty remained at Hadley, under Capt. Poole.” The largest garrison in the valley after Springfield, and he had it. ⭐ Image 204: “six soldiers were ordered to Hatfield, to be under Lieut. Allis, and employed in the winter time in getting out timber for the fortifications at Hatfield and Deerfield.” ⭐ And image 209 confirms his death from a third direction — a 1680 Deerfield land redistribution notes in brackets: “[Lieut. Wm. Allis had died September, 1678.]” Boltwood said 6 September 1678; Sheldon says September 1678; the 1919 book agrees. Three authorities, one month. ⚠ And his lieutenancy is now a record, not a title in a genealogy — which is what the ‘Lt.’ on this node had been resting on. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘part’ → ‘ok’, 6 Aug 2026, AT ALEC’S CHALLENGE [AUDIT-01]. The colour grades WHO THIS PERSON IS AND WHOSE CHILD THEY ARE — not whether every fact about them is known. Loose ends belong in Outstanding, below, and this node has some. They do not make the ancestor uncertain. ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED hyp → part, 6 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-32] — BOLTWOOD’S OWN ENTRY, image 585: “1. ALLIS, William, freeman May 13, 1640, Braintree, rem. abt. 1661 to Hat., and d. Sept. 6, 1678. m. (1) Mary, who d. Aug. 10, 1677; m. (2) June 25, 1678, Mary, dau. of John Brownson, and wid. of John Graves, of Haddam, Ct. … Children — John, b. Mch. 5, 1642; SAMUEL, b. Feb. 24, 1647; Josiah, d. Oct. 15, 1651; Josiah, b. Oct. 20, 1651; William, b. Jan. 10, 1653, d. ae. 9 mos.; William, b. Jan. 10, 1656, SLAIN IN THE FALLS FIGHT, MAY 19, 1676; Hannah, m. June 28, 1670, William Scott; Mary, d. unm. Jan. 25, 1690.” Boltwood’s child list gives Samuel’s birth as 24 February 1647 — the exact date the 1919 book gives. Two independent authorities on the parent-child link, so the generation stands. ⚠⚠ THE 1630 “WINTHROP’S FLEET” CLAIM IS STILL REFUSED — it is the 1919 book alone, and that book has been caught inventing a martyrdom. Boltwood starts him as a Braintree freeman in 1640 and says nothing about a crossing. ⭐⭐ NEW, AND WORTH A LINE OF ITS OWN: a second son William, b. 10 Jan 1656, was killed in the FALLS FIGHT at Turners Falls on 19 May 1676 — Samuel’s brother. This family lost a son to King Philip’s War in 1676 and a daughter-in-law and grandson to the Deerfield raid in 1704. ——— Earlier note: ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ NEW NODE, 6 Aug 2026 — GENERATION 13, the deepest this project has ever reached. “1. WILLIAM ALLIS was born between 1613 and 1616, probably in ESSEX or LONDON, England … and came to this country with WINTHROP’S FLEET in 1630.” Braintree 1632–1658, then Wethersfield, Connecticut, then Hadley and Hatfield. Lieutenant. Married Mary in 1641; after her death married, 25 June 1678, Mary daughter of John Bronson; died 6 September 1678 [TUTTLE-18].

Outstanding: ⚠⚠ ‘hyp’ ON PURPOSE. A single published genealogy, unverified against any record. ⚠ Our line runs through the FIRST wife, since Samuel was born 1647. ⚠⚠ BOLTWOOD DOES NOT SAY “WINTHROP’S FLEET” [TUTTLE-19]. He gives only “freeman May 13, 1640, Braintree, rem. abt. 1661 to Hat[field], and d. Sept. 6, 1678” — which confirms the death date and the Braintree→Hatfield move but leaves the 1630 crossing and the “Essex or London” origin as the 1919 book’s claims ALONE. Given that the same book’s Hatfield-massacre claim has just been refuted, do not repeat the Winthrop’s-fleet story as fact.

Mary (first wife of Lt. William Allis; m. 1641 – d. 10 August 1677)

Ahnentafel #6153 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s father’s mother (12 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Mary married William Allis in 1641 and bore him several children, among them Samuel, born 1647, from whom this family descends, and a son William, later killed in the Falls Fight of 1676. She died 10 August 1677. Her own surname was never recorded — the primary Braintree town register names her only as "Mary his wife" across her children's births, and no marriage entry survives. An old family story that she was killed in a massacre turned out, on closer reading, to belong instead to her daughter-in-law Alice, who died in the Deerfield raid of 1704, a full generation later.

Evidence

⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ RE-GRADED ‘part’ → ‘ok’, 6 Aug 2026, AT ALEC’S CHALLENGE [AUDIT-01]. The colour grades WHO THIS PERSON IS AND WHOSE CHILD THEY ARE — not whether every fact about them is known. Loose ends belong in Outstanding, below, and this node has some. They do not make the ancestor uncertain. ⭐⭐⭐ UPGRADED hyp → part [TUTTLE-32]. Boltwood, image 585: “m. (1) Mary, who d. Aug. 10, 1677” — a dated death from an authority independent of the 1919 book, and our line runs through her, since Samuel was born in 1647. ⭐ Her husband remarried within the year — 25 June 1678, Mary, dau. of John Brownson and widow of John Graves of Haddam — and died ten weeks later. ——— Earlier note: ⛔⚠⚠ SELF-CORRECTION #12, made one turn after the error [TUTTLE-19]. TUTTLE-18 recorded, from the 1919 Allis genealogy, that she was “killed in the Hatfield massacre on 19 September 1677” — and flagged it as the kind of detail a family genealogy embroiders. It is WRONG and is WITHDRAWN. Boltwood: “m. (1) MARY, WHO D. AUG. 10, 1677.” She died on 10 August 1677 — five and a half weeks BEFORE the raid. She was already dead. Cause unknown.

Outstanding: ⭐ Her surname is now a CLOSED bounded negative, 7 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-54]: five sources — the 1919 book’s printed blank, Boltwood, Sheldon, Judd, and the primary Braintree register (Bates 1886), which names her only ever as “Mary his wife” across her children’s births and carries no marriage — the name does not survive. ⭐ The violent death the 1919 book was reaching for is real, but it is her daughter-in-law ALICE, killed at Deerfield in 1704 — the wrong generation, town, decade and war. ⚠ Treat every other unverified claim in that book with the same suspicion.

William Davis (c.1617 – 9 Dec 1683 (æ66), Roxbury, Mass.)

Ahnentafel #6156 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s father’s father (12 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

William Davis lived in Roxbury, Massachusetts, where a 1649 church record names his sons John, Samuel, and Joseph among the children of "William Davis whose wife was admitted" to the congregation. His son Samuel was later dismissed from the Roxbury church to Northampton. William Davis had two wives — Elizabeth, and later Alice Thorp, who died in childbirth — and was, according to one genealogist, probably a brother of a man named Tobias. He died 9 December 1683, at about the age of sixty-six.

Evidence

⭐ ADDED at PROBABLE, 10 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed — and the verdict pass recovered the primary record [TUTTLE-73/-VERDICT]: the 1649 Roxbury church admission (printed p.118, read at the image): “John Davis, Samuel Davis, Joseph Davis — Children of William Davis whose wife was admitted…” — naming Samuel as his son in a contemporary document; plus “Samuel Davis dismissed to North-Hampton” (p.91, ~1673). Savage’s William entry concurs (son “Samuel, 21 Feb. 1645”; wives Elizabeth then Alice Thorp; “prob. br. of Tobias”); Savage’s Samuel entry still hedges “perhaps” — with the infant-to-adult bridge resting on elimination, Probable is the honest grade. His second wife’s death: “Alice wife of William Davis who dyed in child birth undelivered” (p.193).

Outstanding: Samuel’s 1690 Hampshire probate (would close the bridge); the 1668-vs-1673 removal/dismissal lag; William’s own 6 Dec 1683 will (mentioned by Savage, unread).

Elizabeth Davis (admitted to the Roxbury church 1647×49 – d. 4 May 1658)

Ahnentafel #6157 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s father’s mother (12 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Elizabeth was William Davis's first wife, admitted to the Roxbury church between 1647 and early 1649, shortly before a church ordinance recorded that her husband's children had been brought into the congregation through her admission. She is understood, by the timing of that record, to have been the mother of Samuel Davis, though the ordinance itself does not name her directly. She died, or was buried, on 4 May 1658.

Evidence

⭐ ADDED at PROBABLE, 10 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [TUTTLE-73/77]. William Davis’s first wife and — by a tight cross-page inference — Samuel’s mother: her own church admission (printed p.86, bracketed 1647–early 1649) precedes the Oct 1649 ordinance (“Children of William Davis whose wife was admitted & so brought her children…”); Savage: “His w. Eliz. d. or was bur. 4 May 1658”; Alice Thorp only from 1658/1663. The ordinance never NAMES her — hence Probable.

Outstanding: the Roxbury BIRTHS register (mother named on the 1643/1645/1649 entries = the path to ok).

John Catlin (of Wethersfield; estate appraised 17 July 1644; his given name is unsettled)

Ahnentafel #6164 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s mother’s father’s father (12 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

The first husband of Isabel, and father of John Catlin who later settled at Deerfield, was a man of Wethersfield, Connecticut, whose estate was appraised at £69 on 17 July 1644. His given name is not settled: one genealogist records it as John, while another states plainly that the baptismal name of Isabel's first husband is unknown. The uncertainty concerns his identity, not his place in the family — he remains Isabel's husband and the elder generation behind John Catlin of Deerfield regardless of what he was called.

Evidence

ADDED at PROBABLE, 10 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [TUTTLE-78/-VERDICT]. Isabel’s first husband and John-of-Deerfield’s father. Sheldon’s entry 1 names him “John” with the 1644 estate appraisal; Savage twice disclaims the given name (“The bapt. name of her first h. is unkn.”) — a genuine identification conflict = Probable by definition.

Outstanding: the Wethersfield 1644 estate record itself (would settle the name); Savage-vs-Sheldon adjudication.

Isabel Catlin (m.(2) James Northam; m.(3) Joseph Baldwin; d. 8 Dec 1676, Hadley)

Ahnentafel #6165 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s mother’s father’s mother (12 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Isabel married three times. Her first husband, a Catlin of Wethersfield, died by 1644, leaving her with a son, John, who later settled at Deerfield; she then married James Northam, who died in either 1660 or 1662, the sources disagreeing; and she married a third time, to Joseph Baldwin of Milford and Hadley. She was probably the sister of Lawrence Ward, of New Haven, Branford, and Newark. She died 8 December 1676 at Hadley. In a striking turn, her son John Catlin later married Mary Baldwin, the daughter of Isabel's own third husband — so the two families were connected in two directions within a single generation.

Evidence

⭐ ADDED at PROBABLE, 10 Aug 2026 (ok refused on shared-source evidence) [TUTTLE-78/-VERDICT]. The thrice-married matriarch: Savage independently states her as John-of-Deerfield’s mother; Sheldon and the 1881 Baldwin Genealogy agree on her chain to the day AND word — likely one source absorbed. “Prob. sister of Lawrence Ward of New Haven, Branford… and Newark” (Sheldon) — the Newark thread is her BROTHER’s, untangling the old anachronism. Her son married her third husband’s daughter — the families interlock twice.

Outstanding: the Northam death-date conflict (1660/1662); Hampshire probate for the independent-date test (path back to ok).

Joseph Baldwin (Milford, Conn. 1639 → Hadley c.1663; d. 2 Nov 1684; will 20 Dec 1680, proved Northampton)

Ahnentafel #6166 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s mother’s mother’s father (12 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Joseph Baldwin was among the first settlers of Milford, Connecticut, in 1639, and later removed to Hadley, Massachusetts, about 1663, becoming a freeman there in 1666. He married three times — first Hannah, then Isabel, and finally Elizabeth (Hitchcock) Warriner. He died 2 November 1684; his will was drawn 20 December 1680 and proved at Northampton. As the immigrant generation of this family, his English background — a case has been made connecting him to a Cholesbury, Buckinghamshire family — was noted but deliberately not pursued; the family's American story begins with him.

Evidence

ADDED at PROBABLE, 10 Aug 2026 (ok refused — the two compilations are one source-lineage on these details) [TUTTLE-78/-VERDICT]. First settler of Milford; freeman at Hadley 1666; three wives (Hannah; Isabel; Elizabeth (Hitchcock) Warriner). THE IMMIGRANT — the English case (Richard of Cholesbury’s 1633 will naming the three brothers) noted, not chased.

Outstanding: his 1684 Hampshire probate file (the path back to ok for him AND Isabel).

Hannah Baldwin (joined the Milford church 23 June 1644, the day her first four children were baptized)

Ahnentafel #6167 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s father’s mother’s mother’s mother (12 generations back) · Probable — the IDENTIFICATION or the LINK rests on one source, or is in conflict

Hannah was the first wife of Joseph Baldwin of Milford, Connecticut. She joined the Milford church on 23 June 1644, the same day their first four children were baptized — among them Mary, whose own baptism is dated to that day. Little else of her own life survives; her name comes from a single source, and no record of her death has been found.

Evidence

ADDED at PROBABLE, 10 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-78/-VERDICT]. Joseph’s first wife and Mary Baldwin’s mother: “Joseph’s wife Hannah joined the Church June 23, 1644, and their first four children were then baptized” — Mary’s charted baptism is that very day. Single source for her name.

Outstanding: her death (before Joseph’s ~1660s remarriage); her own family.

Deacon William Peck (b. 1601 London – d. 4 (or 14) Oct 1694 at Lyme, at his son Joseph’s house)

Ahnentafel #12300 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s father’s father (13 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

William Peck was born in London in 1601 and worked there as a merchant before emigrating with his wife, Elizabeth, and son Jeremiah, probably on the ship Hector, in the company of Governors Eaton and Hopkins and the Reverend John Davenport. He was one of the signers of the founding compact for New Haven Colony in June 1639, and served as a deacon there from 1659 until his death. He married a second time, to Sarah, widow of William Holt, though his will named only the children of his first marriage. William Peck died at Lyme, Connecticut, at the home of his son Joseph, in October 1694 — the exact day recorded variously as the 4th or the 14th — and his gravestone was still to be seen there generations later.

Evidence

⭐⭐ UPGRADED to DOCUMENTED, 10 Aug 2026, micro-adversary [TUTTLE-75]: the old ‘part’ rationale (Henry-vs-William) died on 8 Aug [TUTTLE-50/70]; three non-derivative authorities now carry both the man and the John-link. Savage p.394, recovered verbatim: “a merch. from London, b. 1601, with w. Eliz. and s. Jeremiah, came prob. in the Hector, as compan. with Govs. Eaton and Hopkins, Rev. John Davenport… one of the first compact for N.H. in June 1639… deac. from 1659 to his d.… In his will, of 9 Mar. 1689… the sec. w. and his four ch. are ment. but no more.” Second wife: Sarah, widow of William Holt (m. after 1683; the four children all Elizabeth’s). His gravestone “still to be seen” in Savage’s day.

Outstanding: death date 4 vs 14 Oct 1694 (compiler variance); age 83 (Lyme) / 90 (New Haven, reputed) / 93 (computed); the will IMAGE — New Haven Probate Book II p.176 (★2) — the record-side seal.

Elizabeth Peck (emigrated on the Hector, 1637 – d. 5 Dec 1683, visiting her son at Lyme)

Ahnentafel #12301 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s father’s mother (13 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

Elizabeth emigrated from England to New England in 1637, probably aboard the ship Hector, together with her husband, William Peck, and their son Jeremiah. Her children, including a son John, were later named in her husband's will as the children of his first marriage. She died 5 December 1683 while visiting her son at Lyme, Connecticut. Her own maiden name was never recorded by any source consulted.

Evidence

⭐⭐ ADDED at DOCUMENTED, 10 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [TUTTLE-74/-VERDICT]: two independent authorities agreeing TO THE DAY with non-overlapping detail — Darius Peck 1877 (“His wife, Elizabeth, died December 5, 1683… His children were all by his first wife” — the four incl. our John, named in the will) and Savage (“with w. Eliz. and s. Jeremiah, came prob. in the Hector… d. 5 Dec. 1683, on a visit to her s. at Lyme”). Savage’s will-paraphrase shows record access, not folklore. No prior-English-wife hint anywhere.

Outstanding: her maiden name (no source ventures one); the will image (★2) as the family’s record-side seal.

John Moss (New Haven 1639; Wallingford 1670; d. 1707 “aged 103, perhaps with slight exagg.”)

Ahnentafel #12302 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s mother’s father (13 generations back) · Documented — person and parent-link established

John Moss was in New Haven by 1639 and later moved to Wallingford, Connecticut, by 1670. He and his wife had eleven children baptized between 1640 and 1655, among them Mary, baptized 11 April 1647, who married John Peck in 1664. One later compiler called him "the ancestor of all who bear the name in these parts." He was said to have died in 1707 at the remarkable age of 103, a figure that traces back to a sermon preached decades later and that should be treated with some skepticism rather than taken as fact.

Evidence

⭐⭐ ADDED at DOCUMENTED, 10 Aug 2026, adversary-confirmed [TUTTLE-74/-VERDICT]: Savage’s full entry (eleven children incl. “Mary, 11 Apr. 1647… Mary m. 3 Nov. 1664, John Peck”) + Jacobus matching the baptism to the day + Davis (“the ancestor of all who bear the name in these parts”). The Sr./Jr. hazard is arithmetically dead: Mary (b. 1647) predates John Jr. (b. 1650). ⚠ The “aged 103” figure roots in Dana’s 1770 centennial sermon (Savage’s own citation) — carry with Savage’s raised eyebrow, not as fact.

Outstanding: his wife (node 12303); the deed method for her name.

John Moss's wife (name not recorded)

Ahnentafel #12303 · his mother’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s father’s mother’s mother’s mother’s mother (13 generations back) · Known unknown — the person existed; the name does not survive

The wife of John Moss of New Haven and Wallingford is known only through the eleven children she and her husband had baptized between 1640 and 1655, among them Mary, who married John Peck. Neither of the genealogical authorities consulted for this family named her, and no record has yet supplied her identity — her name simply does not survive.

Evidence

KNOWN UNKNOWN, 10 Aug 2026 [TUTTLE-74/-VERDICT]. Eleven baptized children 1640–1655 prove her; Savage’s and Davis’s full entries name no wife — an informative silence, read whole. Recovery path: a New Haven/Wallingford conveyance with the “for love and affection to my wife ———” formula.
